The Persians (472 BC)

The Persians

(Susa, the Persian capital, before the council house, with the tomb of Darius in view. The Elders of the kingdom come in, old men left in charge while the army is away.)

Chorus.
	We are the ones they call the Faithful.
	The Persians have gone to the land of Greece
	and we were left to keep the seats,
	the rich seats, the seats heavy with gold.
	Xerxes the King, Darius's son,
	chose us himself, for our age and our rank,
	to watch over the country.
	But about the King's return,
	about the army and all its gold,
	something inside me has started
	to prophesy disaster.
	The whole strength of Asia is gone,
	and every house is muttering the young man's name,
	and no runner comes, no rider comes,
	to the city of the Persians.
	They left the walls of Susa, of Agbatana,
	the old stronghold of Cissia,
	and went, some on horses,
	some in ships, some on foot at the march,
	a solid mass of war.
	Men like Amistres and Artaphrenes,
	Megabates and Astaspes,
	marshals of Persia,
	kings themselves, and vassals of the Great King,
	are hurrying on at the head of that huge army,
	masters of the bow, masters of the horse,
	terrible to see and terrible in a fight,
	with hearts that have decided to endure.
	Artembares, who fights from the chariot,
	and Masistres, and Imaeus, the good archer,
	and Pharandaces,
	and Sosthanes, who drives the horses.
	And others the great Nile sent, the river that feeds so many:
	Susiscanes,
	Pegastagon, born in Egypt,
	great Arsames, lord of holy Memphis,
	Ariomardus, who governs ancient Thebes,
	and the rowers out of the marshes,
	frightening men, too many to count.
Chorus.
	Behind them come the soft-living Lydians in a crowd,
	and the men who hold down the whole mainland,
	whom Metrogathes and brave Arcteus, their commanding kings,
	and Sardis, heavy with gold, sent riding out
	in chariot after chariot,
	teams of two poles and of three,
	a sight to make you afraid to look.
	And the men who live by holy Tmolus swear
	they will throw the yoke of slavery over Greece:
	Mardon and Tharybis, anvils for any spear,
	and the Mysians, who throw the javelin. And Babylon,
	heavy with gold, sends a mixed crowd streaming out,
	men who ride the ships
	and men who trust in the pull of the bow.
	And the whole nation that wears the short sword
	follows out of every part of Asia
	at the King's terrible summons.
	That is the flower of the Persian land,
	the men who are gone,
	and all Asia, the country that nursed them,
	groans for them with a longing that burns,
	and parents and wives count the days
	and shake, as the time stretches out.
Chorus.
	The King's army, the sacker of cities,
	has already crossed
	to the neighbouring land on the far shore.
	It went over the strait of Helle, Athamas's daughter,
	on a raft of boats lashed with flax,
	a road bolted tight,
	a yoke thrown over the neck of the sea.
Chorus.
	The hot-headed ruler of Asia, land of so many men,
	is driving his marvellous flock,
	his herd of men, against the whole earth,
	in two divisions, on land and off the sea,
	trusting in his hard, rough commanders,
	he himself, a man like a god,
	of the race that was born from gold.
Chorus.
	With the dark blue glare
	of a deadly snake in his eyes,
	with his thousands of hands and thousands of sailors,
	whipping on his Syrian chariot,
	he brings a War of arrows
	against men famous for the spear.
Chorus.
	And no one has yet been found who can stand
	against that great river of men
	and hold back with solid walls
	the unfightable wave of the sea.
	The Persian army cannot be approached,
	and its people have brave hearts.
Chorus.
	For from the gods, long ago,
	Fate took hold and laid it on the Persians
	to carry through the wars
	that break down towers,
	the confusion of charging horsemen,
	the uprooting of cities.
Chorus.
	And they learned to look out
	on the sea's sacred grove,
	the broad roads of water
	whitening under the wild wind,
	trusting their thin-spun cables
	and the machines that carry a people across.
Chorus.
	But the god's trick, the cunning deception,
	what man alive can escape it?
	Who is so quick on his feet
	that he can leap clear of it lightly?
Chorus.
	For Ruin is friendly at first. She fawns
	and coaxes a man into her nets,
	and from there no mortal
	slips out above the trap and away.
Chorus.
	This is why my heart is dressed in black
	and torn with fear.
	Oh, for the Persian army!
	I am afraid the city will hear
	that the great town of Susa has no men left in it,
Chorus.
	and that the fortress of the Cissians
	will sing the answer back,
	Oh, a crowd of women
	crying that one word out loud,
	and the tearing will fall on the fine linen dresses.
Chorus.
	For every man who rides a horse
	and every man who walks the plain
	has left, like a swarm out of the hive
	when the bees go with the leader of the host,
	and crossed the headland that is yoked to both shores,
	the sea's own cape,
	the land that the two continents share.
Chorus.
	And the beds fill with tears
	for the men they miss.
	The women of Persia are soft in their grief. Each one
	sent off her fierce, spear-carrying husband
	with love and longing,
	and is left
	in a yoke made for two, alone.
Chorus.
	But come, Persians. Let us sit down here
	under this ancient roof
	and think carefully, think deep.
	The need has come for it.
	How is Xerxes the King doing,
	Darius's son,
	of the race that gave our people their name?
	Is it the drawn bow that has won,
	or has the strength of the spear
	with its iron head prevailed?
	But look. Here comes a light like the eyes of the gods:
	the King's mother, my Queen.
	I fall to the ground before her.
	And it is right that every one of us
	speaks to her with words of greeting.

(The Queen comes in on a chariot, richly dressed, with attendants. The old men prostrate themselves and rise again.)

Chorus. Queen, highest of the deep-belted women of Persia, aged mother of Xerxes, wife of Darius, greetings. You were the wife of the Persians' god and you are the mother of a god, unless the old luck of the army has turned its back on it now.

Queen. That is exactly why I have come, out of the palace with its gold hangings, out of the room where Darius and I slept. Worry is clawing at my heart too. I will tell you something, friends, and I am in no way free of fear when I tell it: I am afraid that great wealth may kick up the dust from the ground and trip up, with its own foot, the prosperity that Darius built, and did not build without some god's help. So there is a double anxiety in my mind, and it is hard to put into words. A pile of money with no men to it is nothing to honour. And men with no money never shine with the light their strength deserves. We have wealth enough, and no one could fault it. My fear is for the eye. The eye of a house, I think, is the presence of its master. That is how things stand. So be my advisers in this, Persians, old and faithful men. Every piece of good counsel I have is in you.

Chorus. Be sure of this, Queen of this land: you will never have to ask twice for anything, in word or deed, that it is in our power to guide you to. You are calling on advisers who wish you well.

Queen. Ever since my son fitted out his army and went off meaning to lay waste the land of the Ionians, I have lived with dreams, night after night. But I have never seen one as clear as last night's. I will tell it to you.

Two women in beautiful clothes seemed to come before my eyes. One was dressed in Persian robes, the other in Dorian dress. They were far taller than women are now, flawless in their beauty, and sisters, of one family. For a homeland, one had drawn Greece by lot, the other the land of the barbarians. And the two of them, as I seemed to see it, were starting some quarrel between themselves. My son noticed it, and tried to hold them back and calm them down. He yoked them both under his chariot and put the collar straps on their necks. One of them towered up proud in that harness and kept her mouth obedient to the reins. The other fought. She tore the chariot's tackle apart with her hands, dragged it off with her by force, bit and all, and broke the yoke in the middle. My son fell. And his father Darius stood there beside him, pitying him. And when Xerxes saw him, he tore the clothes off his own body.

That is what I say I saw in the night. When I got up and touched my hands in the clear running water of the spring, I went to the altar with an offering in my hand, meaning to sacrifice a cake to the gods who turn evil away. That is their due. And I saw an eagle running for refuge to the hearth of Apollo, and I stood there without a sound, my friends, in fear. And then I saw a hawk come rushing in on its wings and pluck at the eagle's head with its claws. And the eagle did nothing but crouch down and offer up its body. Those were terrors for me to see, and they are terrors for you to hear. For you know this well: if my son does well, he will be a man everyone marvels at. And if he does badly, he answers to no city. If he comes home safe, he rules this land exactly as before.

Chorus. Mother, we do not want to frighten you too much with what we say, or to reassure you too much either. Go to the gods with your prayers. If what you saw was bad, ask them to turn it aside and finish it off, and to bring the good things to fulfilment for you and your children and the city and everyone you love. Then you must pour offerings to the Earth and the dead. And ask your husband Darius, whom you say you saw in the night, ask him kindly to send good things up out of the earth into the light for you and for your son, and to hold the opposite of them down under the ground, fading in the dark. That is my advice, and I mean it well. My heart is my prophet. Our judgement is that all of this turns out well for you, in every way.

Queen. Well, you have read the dream kindly, at least, as its first interpreter, and with goodwill to my son and my house. May the good things come true. Everything you ask, I will do for the gods and for the dear ones under the earth, as soon as I go back into the house. But there is something I want to find out, my friends. Where in the world do they say Athens is?

Chorus. Far away, towards the west, where our lord the Sun goes down and dies.

Queen. And my son really wanted to hunt down this city?

Chorus. Yes. Because then all Greece would be the King's subject.

Queen. Do they have so many men in their army?

Chorus. Enough of an army to have done the Medes a great deal of harm already.

Queen. And what else do they have? Enough wealth in their houses?

Chorus. They have a spring of silver, a treasure in their soil.

Queen. Is it the arrow and the drawn bow that fits their hands?

Chorus. Not at all. Spears for standing and fighting close, and shields for armour.

Queen. And who is the shepherd over them? Who is master of their army?

Chorus. They are called nobody's slaves, and nobody's subjects.

Queen. Then how can they stand against an invading enemy?

Chorus. Well enough to have destroyed Darius's army, which was large and fine.

Queen. What you say is terrible for the parents of the men who have gone there to think about.

Chorus. But I think you will soon know the whole story, and every word of it true. That is a Persian running, you can tell by the way he moves, and he brings some clear news, good or bad to hear.

Messenger. Cities of all the land of Asia! Land of Persia, great harbour of wealth! At one blow, all that prosperity is destroyed, and the flower of the Persians has fallen and is gone. Oh, it is a bad thing to be the first to bring bad news. But I have to unfold the whole of the suffering, Persians. The whole barbarian army is lost.

Chorus.
	Pain, pain, strange new pain,
	and cruel. Aiai,
	be drenched, Persians,
	as you hear this grief.

Messenger. Yes, because everything over there is finished. And I, past all hope, am looking at the light of home.

Chorus.
	Too long.
	This life has shown itself too long for us,
	old men, to have to hear
	this pain we never expected.

Messenger. I was there, Persians. I did not get it from other men's stories. I can tell you what kind of disaster was worked.

Chorus.
	Otototoi. For nothing,
	all those weapons, all those mixed peoples,
	went out from the land of Asia
	against Greece, the enemy's country.

Messenger. The shores of Salamis and all the coast around are full of the bodies of men who died an ugly death.

Chorus.
	Otototoi. You are telling me the bodies of the men we love,
	rolled by the sea, soaked through,
	dead, are being carried
	back and forth, tossing in their long cloaks.

Messenger. The bows were no use. The whole army was lost, broken by the ramming of the ships.

Chorus.
	Raise the cry, the unlucky cry,
	the cry of misery for the Persians:
	the enemy has made an end of everything,
	utterly. Aiai for the army that is destroyed.

Messenger. Salamis. The most hateful name there is to hear. Oh. How I groan when I remember Athens.

Chorus.
	Hateful Athens, to her enemies.
	We have cause to remember
	how many Persian women she has made,
	for nothing, childless and without a man.

Queen. I have been silent a long while, wretched, struck dumb by the disaster. This calamity is too big to speak of, too big even to ask about. But when the gods send suffering, mortals have to bear it. Compose yourself, and unfold the whole of it, even if you are groaning at our losses as you speak. Who is not dead? Which of the commanders shall we mourn, who held a place of authority and left his post empty of its man by dying?

Messenger. Xerxes himself is alive and sees the light.

Queen. You have spoken a great light for my house, and a white day after a night black as pitch.

Messenger. But Artembares, who commanded ten thousand horse, is being beaten now against the rough shore of Sileniae. And Dadaces, captain of a thousand, took a spear and made a light leap out of his ship. Tenagon, the true-born chief of the Bactrians, is roaming the sea-beaten island of Ajax. Lilaeus, Arsames, and Argestes, a third, were spun round the island where the doves breed and butted the hard ground with their heads. And from the springs of the Egyptian Nile, their neighbour, Arcteus, Adeues, and a third, Pharnuchus of the great shield: these fell out of one ship. Matallus of Chrysa, commander of ten thousand, leader of the Black Horse, thirty thousand strong, died and soaked his thick, bushy, tawny beard, changing its colour with a purple dye. And Arabus the Magian, and Artabes the Bactrian, who has settled now in a hard country, perished there. Amistris, and Amphistreus, who handled a spear that gave a lot of trouble, and good Ariomardus, who has brought grief to Sardis, and Seisames the Mysian, and Tharybis, commander of five times fifty ships, a Lyrnaean by birth, a handsome man, lies dead, poor wretch, not lucky at all. And Syennesis, first of them all for courage, commander of the Cilicians, one man who gave the enemy more trouble than any other, died with glory. Those are the commanders I have called to mind. There were many losses. I am reporting a few.

Queen. Aiai. These are the highest evils I could hear: shame for the Persians, and shrill wailing. But turn back and tell me this. How many Greek ships were there, that they thought themselves fit to join battle with the Persian fleet and ram it?

Messenger. If it had gone by numbers, be sure of this, the barbarians would have won with their ships. The Greeks had ten times thirty ships in all, and a picked squadron of ten besides. Xerxes, and I know this, had a thousand under his command, and the ones that were exceptional for speed were two hundred and seven. That is the count. Do you think we were outnumbered in that fight? No. It was some god that destroyed the army, loading the scales with a luck that was not evenly weighed. The gods keep the goddess Pallas's city safe.

Queen. Then the city of Athens is still unsacked?

Messenger. While she has her men, her wall is secure.

Queen. Tell me how the ships came to engage. Who began the fighting? The Greeks? Or my son, glorying in the number of his ships?

Messenger. What began it all, my lady, was a fiend, or some evil spirit, that appeared from somewhere. A Greek came from the Athenian army to your son Xerxes and told him this: that when the darkness of black night came on, the Greeks would not stay, but would jump to the rowing benches of their ships and save their lives by slipping away, each one where he could. Xerxes heard it, and he did not see the Greek's trick, or the gods' jealousy. He gave every ship's captain this order at once. When the sun stopped burning the earth with its rays and dark took hold of the precinct of the sky, they were to draw up the main body of the fleet in three lines to guard the exits and the sounding channels, and post other ships in a ring around the island of Ajax. And if the Greeks escaped their fate and found some way to slip off with their ships, it was laid down that every captain would lose his head. He said all this with a very confident heart. He did not know what was coming from the gods.

Our men, in good order and with obedient spirits, got their supper ready, and every sailor looped his oar to the thole-pin so it ran well. When the light of the sun had gone and night came on, every master of an oar and every man in charge of arms went aboard. Line called out to line along the long ships, and they sailed each to the station he was given, and all night long the captains kept the whole fleet cruising back and forth across the strait. Night wore on, and the Greek fleet made no attempt anywhere to slip out. But when day with her white horses had spread over all the land, bright to see, the first thing was a noise from the Greeks, a shout like a song of triumph, and at the same instant the echo came back high and clear from the rocks of the island. Terror took hold of all the barbarians. They had been wrong. The Greeks were not singing their solemn hymn for flight. They were going into battle, with brave hearts.

Messenger. Then the trumpet set the whole of their side ablaze. At the word of command they struck the deep brine together, oars roaring, and in a moment all of them were there to be seen. The right wing led off first, well marshalled and in order, and then the whole fleet came on behind, and at the same time we could hear a great shout: "Forward, sons of the Greeks! Free your country! Free your children, your wives, the seats of your fathers' gods and the tombs of your ancestors! Now the fight is for everything!" And from our side a roar of Persian speech went out to meet it, and there was no more time to wait. Ship struck ship, bronze beak on bronze. A Greek ship began the ramming and sheared off the whole stern of a Phoenician, and after that every captain aimed his ship at another. At first the flood of the Persian fleet held its own. But when the mass of the ships was jammed in the narrows, and no one could help anyone, and they were striking each other with their own bronze-mouthed rams and splintering the whole bank of oars, then the Greek ships, who knew exactly what they were doing, hemmed them in and battered them all round. Hulls rolled over. You could no longer see the sea for wrecks and slaughtered men. The beaches and the reefs were full of corpses, and every ship in the barbarian fleet was rowing away in disorder. And the Greeks kept hitting them, hacking at them with broken oars and pieces of wreckage, as if they were tuna or some other haul of fish. Groaning and shrieking together filled the open sea, until the face of black night took it away. As for the full extent of the losses, if I talked in order for ten days I could not get to the end of them for you. But be sure of this: never before did so great a number of men die in a single day.

Queen. Aiai. A great sea of evils has broken over the Persians and the whole barbarian race.

Messenger. And know this: the evil is not yet at the halfway mark. Such a disaster came on them after it as to outweigh all this twice over.

Queen. What worse luck than this could there be? Say what this other blow was, that you say fell on the army and tipped the scale further down into evil.

Messenger. Those Persians who were in the prime of their strength, the bravest in spirit, the best born, and always first in loyalty to the King himself: they are dead, shamefully, by the most inglorious death of all.

Queen. Oh, I am wretched, friends, wretched at this disaster. By what death do you say they died?

Messenger. There is an island in front of Salamis, a small one, a bad anchorage for ships, where Pan, who loves the dance, walks the shore by the sea. Xerxes sent these men there, so that when the enemy were wrecked from their ships and got away to the island, they could kill the Greek force easily and rescue their own men out of the channels of the sea. He read the future badly. For when a god gave the glory of the sea battle to the Greeks, that same day they fenced their bodies in good bronze armour and jumped down from their ships and ringed the whole island, so that our men had no idea where to turn. They were hit again and again with stones thrown by hand, and arrows flying from the bowstring kept falling on them and killing. In the end the Greeks charged with a single roar, and struck, and hacked the poor wretches limb from limb, until they had put out the life of every one. Xerxes cried aloud when he saw the depth of the disaster. He had a seat with a clear view of the whole army, a high hill by the open sea. He tore his robes and shrieked, gave his orders at once to the land army, and let it go in disorderly flight. That is the disaster you have to mourn on top of the first.

Queen. Hateful god. How you have cheated the Persians of their hopes. My son has found a bitter revenge on famous Athens. The barbarians that Marathon destroyed before were not enough. My son thought he would make them pay for those, and he has drawn this whole mass of suffering on himself. But the ships that escaped their fate, tell me: where did you leave them? Can you give a clear account?

Messenger. The captains of the ships that were left put up sail and fled in a rush, in no order, wherever the wind took them. And the rest of the army was dying in the land of the Boeotians, some of them struggling with thirst beside the sparkling springs, and the rest of us, empty and gasping, came through to the country of the Phocians and the land of Doris and the Malian gulf, where the Spercheus waters the plain with its kindly stream. From there the plain of Achaea and the cities of Thessaly took us in, starving. There most of us died, of thirst and hunger, because we had both. We reached the Magnesian country and the land of the Macedonians, the crossing of the Axius, the reedy marsh of Bolbe, Mount Pangaeus, the Edonian land. And that night the god raised winter out of season and froze the whole stream of the holy Strymon from bank to bank. Men who had never thought anything of the gods before prayed then, and bowed to earth and sky. And when the army had finished calling on the gods, it crossed over the frozen ford. Every one of us who set out before the god scattered his rays got across safe. But the sun's bright disc, blazing, heated the middle of the crossing with its flame and cut it through. And they fell in on top of one another, and the lucky man was the one whose breath broke off soonest. The rest, the ones who happened to survive, struggled across Thrace with great hardship and have come home, not many of them, to the land of their hearths. So the city of the Persians may mourn, and long for the dearest youth of the land. That is the truth. And I have left out many of the evils the god hurled down on the Persians.

(The Messenger goes out.)

Chorus. Terrible god, god of hard labours, how heavily you have jumped with both feet on the whole Persian race.

Queen. Oh, I am wretched. The army is destroyed. Vision of my dreams, so plain in the night, how clearly you showed me the evil. And you, you read it far too lightly. Still, since your word has settled it so, first I want to pray to the gods, and then I will come back with gifts for the Earth and the dead, a cake from my own house. I know it is for what is already done. But for whatever is still to come, if there is anything better in it. And you, after what has happened, must bring faithful counsel to faithful men. And my son, if he comes here before I get back: comfort him, and see him into the house, so that he does not add some further evil to the evils we have.

(The Queen goes out.)

Chorus.
	Zeus, King. Now you have destroyed
	the army of the Persians,
	so proud of themselves, so many men,
	and buried the city of Susa and Agbatana
	under a black cloud of grief.
	Women everywhere are tearing at their veils
	with soft hands,
	and the tears run down and soak
	the dresses at their breasts. They share the pain.
	And the wives of Persia, gentle even in their wailing,
	who long to see the men they were only just yoked to,
	have given up the soft sheets of the marriage bed,
	the pleasure of their pampered youth,
	and mourn with cries that can never have enough.
	And I too take up the fate of the men who are gone
	and raise, as is proper, a song of many sorrows.
Chorus.
	Now the whole land of Asia groans,
	emptied of its men.
	Xerxes led them out, popoi,
	Xerxes lost them, totoi,
	Xerxes bungled everything
	with his ships on the sea.
	Why was Darius, in his time,
	so free of harm when he was over us,
	master of the bow to his people,
	the leader the men of Susa loved?
Chorus.
	Soldiers and sailors alike,
	the ships led them out, the dark-eyed ships
	with wings of linen, popoi,
	the ships lost them, totoi,
	the ships, with the ramming that destroys everything,
	and the hands of the Ionians.
	And the King himself, we hear,
	only just escaped
	across the flat plains of Thrace
	and its roads of bitter winter.
Chorus.
	And the ones who were the first to die, pheu,
	left behind because they had no choice, Oh,
	are dragged back and forth along the shore of Cychreia, oa.
	Groan, and bite your lips,
	and roar out heavily
	a grief to reach the sky, oa.
	Stretch out the miserable voice
	into a long, wretched howl.
Chorus.
	Torn by the whirling water, pheu,
	they are gnawed, Oh, by the voiceless children
	of the sea that nothing stains, oa.
	A house robbed of its man mourns him,
	and parents with no children left,
	old men wailing over pain the gods sent, oa,
	are hearing now the whole of it.
Chorus.
	And the peoples across the land of Asia
	will not be ruled by Persian law much longer,
	nor go on paying tribute
	because a master makes them,
	nor fall face down on the ground
	in awe. The King's strength
	is destroyed.
Chorus.
	Men's tongues are no longer
	under guard. The people are let loose
	to say whatever they like,
	now that the yoke of power is broken.
	And the island of Ajax, washed round by the sea,
	its fields soaked in blood,
	holds all that was Persia.

(The Queen comes back on foot, without her chariot and her finery, with attendants carrying the offerings.)

Queen. My friends, anyone who has had experience of misfortune knows how it is with people: when a wave of trouble comes over them, they are frightened of everything. But when the god is sending a fair wind, they trust that the same god will blow their luck the same way for ever. For me, now, everything is full of fear. Before my eyes the gods appear, set against me, and in my ears a noise is shouting that has no healing in it. That is how badly this disaster has shaken me and frightened my mind. So I have come back from the house by this road with no chariot and none of my earlier finery, bringing offerings to soften the heart of my son's father, the things that soothe the dead: white milk, good to drink, from an unblemished cow; bright honey, the drop the flower-worker bee distils; running water from a virgin spring; and this, unmixed, the drink from a wild mother, the shining juice of an old vine. And here too is the sweet-smelling fruit of the pale olive, which spends its whole life in leaf; and flowers plaited together, the children of the earth that bears everything. Come, my friends: sing your hymns over these offerings to the dead, and call up the spirit of Darius, while I send these honours down for the earth to drink, for the gods below.

Chorus.
	Royal lady, whom the Persians honour,
	you send your offerings down to the chambers under the earth,
	and we will ask, with hymns,
	the escorts of the dead
	to be kind to us down there beneath the ground.
	Come then, holy gods of the world below,
	Earth, and Hermes, and you, King of the dead,
	send his soul up from underneath into the light.
	For if he knows of any further cure for our troubles,
	he alone of mortals can tell us where they end.
Chorus.
	Does he hear me, the blessed one,
	the King who was a god's equal,
	as I send up in barbarian speech,
	confused and thick with grief,
	these cries of every misery, these harsh, broken sounds?
	Or must I shout out
	the whole of my wretched pain?
	Does he hear me, down there?
Chorus.
	Earth, and you others,
	you leaders of the dead below,
	consent, and let the proud spirit
	come out of his house,
	the god of the Persians, born in Susa.
	Send him up: a man whose like
	the earth of Persia never covered yet.
Chorus.
	Yes, the man was loved, and the mound is loved,
	because what it hides is a heart we loved.
	Hades, you who send the dead back up,
	Hades, let him come,
	our divine lord Darius. Oh.
Chorus.
	For he never lost his men
	in wars that ruin and destroy.
	The Persians called him
	the counsellor whose counsel came from god,
	and he was that,
	because he steered the army well. Oh.
Chorus.
	Master, old master,
	come, come here.
	Come up to the very top of the mound,
	lifting the saffron-dyed slipper on your foot,
	showing the crest
	of the royal tiara.
	Come, father Darius, who did no harm. Oi.
Chorus.
	So that you can hear
	of griefs that are new and will last for ever,
	master of our master, appear.
	A fog like the fog of the Styx has settled over us,
	for the young men of the land
	are dead now, all of them.
	Come, father Darius, who did no harm. Oi.
Chorus.
	Aiai, aiai.
	You whose death your friends wept for so bitterly,
	why, master, master,
	why has this double blunder, wept for on every side,
	come over your land?
	Every three-banked ship this country had
	is gone. Ships that are ships no more, no more.

(The ghost of Darius rises out of the tomb, in his royal robes and tiara.)

Darius. Faithful of the Faithful, men who were young when I was, old men of Persia, what is this trouble the city is in? The ground groans. It is beaten, it is scored with feet. I see my wife beside my tomb and I am afraid, though I received her offerings kindly. And you stand close to the tomb and wail, and with high cries that call up the dead you call me, pitifully. It is not easy to get out. For one reason above all: the gods under the earth are better at taking than at letting go. Still, I have power among them, and I have come. But hurry, so that no one can blame me for the time I take. What is this new, heavy evil on the Persians?

Chorus.
	I am in awe, and cannot look at you.
	I am in awe, and cannot speak to your face,
	from the old dread of you.

Darius. Well, since I came up from below because your wailing persuaded me, do not make a long story of it. Be short. Speak, and go through all of it, and put your awe of me aside.

Chorus.
	I am afraid to do what you want.
	I am afraid to speak to your face
	and say things hard to say to the people I love.

Darius. Well, since the old fear in your minds stands in your way: you, my noble wife, the aged partner of my bed, stop these tears and this wailing and tell me something clear. Human suffering can come to human beings; that is how it is. Many evils come out of the sea and many out of the land for mortals, if their life is stretched out long enough.

Queen. You who outdid every mortal in prosperity, with a fortunate fate: as long as you looked on the rays of the sun, you lived a life the Persians envied, a happy life, like a god. And now I envy you for dying before you saw the depth of our evils. You will hear the whole story in a short time, Darius. The Persian state is sacked and wrecked, in a word.

Darius. How? Did some thunderbolt of plague fall on the city? Or civil war?

Queen. Neither. Near Athens, the whole army has been destroyed.

Darius. Which of my sons led the army there? Tell me.

Queen. Hot-headed Xerxes. He emptied the whole flat continent of its men.

Darius. Was it on foot or by sea that the poor fool made this mad attempt?

Queen. Both. There were two fronts, two armies.

Darius. But how did a land army as large as that get across?

Queen. He yoked the strait of Helle with engines, and made himself a road.

Darius. He did that? He shut the great Bosporus?

Queen. He did. Some god must have had a hand in the plan.

Darius. Pheu. Some great god came on him, so that he could not think straight.

Queen. Yes. You can see how it ended, what an evil he brought about.

Darius. And how did it go for them, that you groan over them like this?

Queen. The fleet was beaten, and its defeat destroyed the land army.

Darius. So the whole people has been wiped out by the spear, utterly?

Queen. So much so that the whole city of Susa groans that it has no men.

Darius. Oh, popoi. That fine defence, that army that stood by us.

Queen. And the people of Bactria are gone, wiped out, not even an old man left.

Darius. Poor wretch. What a youth of allies he has destroyed.

Queen. And Xerxes, alone, deserted, they say, with not many men...

Darius. Ended how, and where? Is there any hope of safety?

Queen. Reached, to his joy, the bridge that yokes the two lands.

Darius. And is safe on this continent? Is that certain?

Queen. Yes. The report is clear and it holds. There is no argument about it.

Darius. Pheu. So the oracles have been carried out quickly, and it is on my son that Zeus has brought down the end of what was foretold. I trusted that the gods would take a long time to bring it about. But when a man is in a hurry himself, the god joins in. Now a spring of evils seems to have been found for everyone I love. And it was my son who did it, not knowing what he did, in the boldness of youth. He hoped to hold the sacred Hellespont with chains, as if it were a slave, the Bosporus, the god's own flowing stream. He changed the shape of the strait, threw hammered shackles around it, and made a great road for a great army. He was a mortal, and he thought he could master all the gods, Poseidon too. That was not good judgement. Was that not a sickness of the mind that had hold of my son? I am afraid that the great wealth I laboured for will be plunder for whoever gets to it first.

Queen. Hot-headed Xerxes learned that lesson by keeping company with bad men. They kept telling him that you won great wealth for your children with your spear, while he, for want of manhood, played the warrior at home and added nothing to his father's fortune. He heard taunts like that many times from bad men, and he planned this road, and this army against Greece.

Darius. And so they have done a deed, the greatest deed, one that will never be forgotten, one that has emptied this city of Susa by its fall as nothing ever did before, since the day Lord Zeus granted this honour: that one man should rule all of Asia, the land that feeds the flocks, with the sceptre of command in his hand. Medus was the first leader of the army. Another, his son, finished his work, because his mind held the tiller of his heart. Third after him came Cyrus, a fortunate man. He ruled and made peace for everyone who was his. He won the people of the Lydians and the Phrygians and drove the whole of Ionia under him by force, and no god hated him for it, because he was born with good sense. Fourth, the son of Cyrus commanded the army. Fifth, Mardus ruled, a disgrace to his country and its ancient throne; brave Artaphrenes killed him by a trick, in the palace, with the men he trusted, whose duty it was. Sixth was Maraphis, and seventh, Artaphrenes. And I drew the lot I wanted, and I marched out many times with a great army. But I never threw an evil this size on the city. Xerxes, my son, is young, and thinks like a young man, and does not remember what I told him. You know this well, men of my own age: all of us who have held this power could not be shown, put together, to have done so much harm.

Chorus. Then what, lord Darius? Where are you steering your words to? After this, how can we, the people of Persia, do best?

Darius. If you do not march against the land of the Greeks, not even if the Median army is larger than theirs. The land itself is their ally.

Chorus. What do you mean? How is it their ally?

Darius. It kills with hunger any army that is far too large.

Chorus. But we will send out a picked force, trim and easy to supply.

Darius. Not even the army that is left in the land of Greece now will win its way safely home.

Chorus. What do you mean? Is the whole barbarian army not crossing the strait of Helle out of Europe?

Darius. Few out of many, if the oracles of the gods are to be trusted at all, looking at what has happened now; for when they come true, it is not some and not others. It is all of them. And if that is so, he is leaving behind a picked mass of his army, persuaded by empty hopes. They are waiting where the Asopus waters the plain with its streams, a welcome fattening for the soil of the Boeotians. There the highest of evils is waiting for them to suffer, the payment for outrage and godless thoughts. They came to the land of Greece and did not scruple to rob the images of the gods and burn their temples. Altars are wiped out, and the seats of the gods have been torn up from their bases, roots and all, and thrown down in a heap. So they did evil, and they are suffering no less, and more is to come; the spring of their evils is not dried up yet, it is still bubbling up. So great will be the clotted mess of slaughtered blood on the ground at Plataea under the Dorian spear that the heaps of the dead will tell the eyes of men, without a word, down to the third generation, that a mortal must not think too high. For outrage comes into flower, and the crop it ripens is Ruin, and from that it reaps a harvest of nothing but tears. Look at these punishments, and remember Athens and Greece, and let no one of you, despising the luck he has and lusting after more, pour away great wealth. Zeus stands over thoughts that boast too much and punishes them; he corrects with a heavy hand. So teach him, with sensible warnings, the man who needs to learn to keep himself in hand, to stop offending the gods with his boasting recklessness. And you, aged mother, whom Xerxes loves, go into the house, take whatever clothing is fit for him, and go to meet your son. For the grief of these evils has ripped his embroidered robes to shreds all about his body. Soothe him gently with words. He will listen to you, I know, and to no one else. I am going down now, into the dark under the earth. And you, Elders, farewell. Even in evil times, give your hearts some pleasure every day, since wealth is no use at all to the dead.

(The ghost of Darius sinks back into the tomb.)

Chorus. It hurts to hear it, so many sufferings for the barbarians, now and still to come.

Queen. God, how many evils and pains come over me. And this misfortune bites the hardest: to hear of the shame my son is wearing on his body, the rags that wrap him. I will go, and take clothing from the house, and try to meet my son. I will not desert the one I love most in his trouble.

(The Queen goes out.)

Chorus.
	Oh, popoi. What a great and good life we had,
	a life under good government,
	when the old King,
	who provided everything, who did no harm,
	whom no one could fight,
	Darius, equal to a god, ruled the land.
Chorus.
	First, we showed the world armies to be proud of,
	and laws, strong as towers,
	kept everything straight,
	and men came home from war
	unworn, unhurt,
	and well, to their own houses.
Chorus.
	And how many cities he took
	without crossing the ford of the river Halys,
	without stirring from his own hearth:
	the Acheloan cities
	by the Strymonian sea, that neighbour
	the Thracian farms,
Chorus.
	and outside the lake, the cities on the mainland,
	ringed round with a wall,
	obeyed him as their King;
	and the ones that boast of the broad strait of Helle,
	and the deep-set Propontis,
	and the mouth of the Pontus;
Chorus.
	and the islands along the sea's headland,
	washed round by the water,
	lying close to this land of ours:
	Lesbos, and Samos planted with olives,
	Chios and Paros, Naxos, Myconos,
	and Andros, that lies next door to Tenos;
Chorus.
	and he ruled the islands out in the sea,
	between the two shores,
	Lemnos, and the seat of Icarus,
	and Rhodes and Cnidus,
	and the Cyprian cities, Paphos,
	and Soli, and Salamis,
	whose mother city is now
	the cause of all this groaning.
Chorus.
	And the rich cities, full of men, of the Greeks
	in the Ionian allotment,
	he ruled by his own will,
	and he had, at his call, the tireless strength
	of armoured men
	and allies from every people mixed together.
	But now, past all argument,
	we bear the reverse, the gods turned it,
	beaten down hard in war
	by blows off the sea.

(Xerxes comes in on foot, with only a few attendants, his robes torn to rags and an empty quiver on his back.)

Xerxes.
	Io.
	Wretched man that I am, I have met this hateful fate,
	that gave no sign at all of its coming.
	How savagely the god has stamped
	on the race of the Persians. What is to become of me?
	The strength has gone out of my legs
	as I look at these old men of the city.
	Zeus, I wish that the fate of death
	had covered me too
	with the men who are gone.
Chorus.
	Otototoi, King, for the good army,
	for the great honour of Persian rule,
	for the fine order of men
	that the god has cut down now.
	The land cries aiai for its own young men,
	killed by Xerxes, who has crammed Hades
	full of Persians. Many men have walked down to Hades,
	the flower of the country,
	masters of the bow, a thick crowd,
	ten thousand at a time, and are gone.
	Aiai, aiai, for the strong men we trusted.
	And the land of Asia, King of the earth,
	terribly, terribly,
	is bent down onto its knee.
Xerxes.
	Here I am, oioi, a thing to wail over,
	a wretch. I was born to be an evil
	to my race and my father's land.
Chorus.
	To greet you home
	I will send up a cry of bad omen,
	an ill-tuned shout,
	the howl of a Mariandynian mourner,
	I will send it, send it,
	a wail of many tears.
Xerxes.
	Let it go, the harsh voice,
	full of grief, all lament.
	The god has turned round against me now.
Chorus.
	I will let it go, all lament,
	in honour of your sufferings
	and the blows the sea dealt,
	the city's grief for its children.
	I will scream out, scream out
	a wail of many tears.
Xerxes.
	It was the Ionians who took them,
	the Ionian War, fenced with ships,
	that gave the strength to the other side
	and mowed the dark plain of the sea
	and the shore of ill luck.
Chorus.
	Oioioi. Cry out, and learn everything.
	Where is the rest of the crowd of your friends?
	Where are the men who stood beside you,
	men like Pharandaces,
	Susas, Pelagon, and Dotamas, and Agdabatas,
	Psammis, and Susiscanes,
	who left Agbatana behind him?
Xerxes.
	I left them dead,
	fallen from a Tyrian ship,
	drifting on the shores
	of Salamis, knocked
	against the hard beach.
Chorus.
	Oioioi. Cry out. Where is your Pharnuchus,
	and good Ariomardus?
	Where is Seualces the prince,
	or Lilaeus of the noble father,
	Memphis, Tharybis, and Masistras,
	Artembares and Hystaechmas?
	This I ask you again.
Xerxes.
	Io, io, moi, moi.
	They looked on ancient Athens,
	the hateful city, and all with one stroke of the oar,
	eh, eh, the poor wretches lie gasping on the land.
Chorus.
	And your Eye, the faithful Eye,
	who counted the Persians for you
	ten thousand, ten thousand at a time,
	Alpistus, son of Batanochus,
	of the line of Sesames, of Megabates,
	and Parthus, and great Oebares,
	did you leave them, leave them?
	Oh, oh, oh, for the wretched men.
	You are telling the noble Persians of evils beyond evil.
Xerxes.
	Io, io, yes,
	you remind me of the longing for my good companions,
	you stir what cannot be forgotten, hateful, evil beyond evil.
	The heart inside my body shouts, shouts.
Chorus.
	And there are others too that we miss:
	the commander of ten thousand Mardian men,
	Xanthis, and warlike Anchares,
	and Diaexis and Arsaces,
	masters of horse,
	Cegdadatas and Lythimnas,
	and Tolmus, who never had enough of the spear.
	I am amazed, amazed
	that they are not following
	behind your wheeled tent.
Xerxes.
	They are gone, the men who led the army.
Chorus.
	Gone, oi, and without a name.
Xerxes.
	Ie, ie. Io, io.
Chorus.
	Io, io, you gods,
	you made an evil no one hoped for,
	plain to see, like the look in Ruin's eyes.
Xerxes.
	We are struck, what a blow, for all time.
Chorus.
	We are struck. It is plain enough.
Xerxes.
	New misery, new misery.
Chorus.
	We met the Ionian seamen
	and our luck was bad.
	The race of the Persians is unlucky in war.
Xerxes.
	How not? An army so large,
	and I am struck down, wretched.
Chorus.
	And what is not? Everything the Persians had is destroyed.
Xerxes.
	Do you see this, what is left of my robe?
Chorus.
	I see, I see.
Xerxes.
	And this, that held the arrows
Chorus.
	What is this you say was saved?
Xerxes.
	a treasure-house for shafts?
Chorus.
	Little enough, out of so much.
Xerxes.
	We are stripped of defenders.
Chorus.
	The Ionian people do not run from the spear.
Xerxes.
	Too brave by far. And I saw a disaster I never expected.
Chorus.
	You mean the fleet, turned and routed?
Xerxes.
	I tore my robe when the evil fell.
Chorus.
	Papai, papai.
Xerxes.
	And more than papai.
Chorus.
	Yes, for double and triple
Xerxes.
	the pain, and the joy for our enemies.
Chorus.
	And our strength is cut short
Xerxes.
	I am naked of my escort.
Chorus.
	by the ruin of your friends at sea.
Xerxes.
	Wet, wet your grief with tears. And go home.
Chorus.
	Aiai, aiai. Misery, misery.
Xerxes.
	Cry now, and answer me.
Chorus.
	A bad gift, bad on bad, for the wretched.
Xerxes.
	Howl, and match your song to mine.
Chorus.
	Otototototoi.
	This blow is heavy.
	Oi. I feel this too.
Xerxes.
	Row with your arms, row, and groan for my sake.
Chorus.
	I am wet with weeping.
Xerxes.
	Cry now, and answer me.
Chorus.
	That is my care, master.
Xerxes.
	Lift up your voice now in wailing.
Chorus.
	Otototototoi.
	And mixed in with it, oi,
	a black blow, a groaning blow.
Xerxes.
	Beat your breast too, and cry the Mysian cry.
Chorus.
	Pain, pain.
Xerxes.
	And tear out the white hair of your beard for me.
Chorus.
	With clenched fists, clenched fists, wailing hard.
Xerxes.
	And shriek.
Chorus.
	I will do that too.
Xerxes.
	And rip the robe over your chest with the tips of your fingers.
Chorus.
	Pain, pain.
Xerxes.
	And pluck your hair, and pity the army.
Chorus.
	With clenched fists, clenched fists, wailing hard.
Xerxes.
	And wet your eyes.
Chorus.
	I am soaked.
Xerxes.
	Cry now, and answer me.
Chorus.
	Oioi, oioi.
Xerxes.
	Go home wailing.
Chorus.
	Io, io. Land of Persia, hard to walk on.
Xerxes.
	Ioa, through the city.
Chorus.
	Ioa, yes, yes.
Xerxes.
	Wail, you who step so softly.
Chorus.
	Io, io. Land of Persia, hard to walk on.
Xerxes.
	Ie, ie, in the three-banked ships,
	ie, ie, the men lost in the boats.
Chorus.
	I will bring you home with harsh cries of grief.

(Xerxes and the Elders go out together toward the palace, wailing as they go.)

Seven Against Thebes (467 BC)

Seven Against Thebes

(Thebes, on the citadel, before the statues of the city's gods. The city is under siege. Eteocles comes in to a crowd of citizens.)

Eteocles. Citizens of Cadmus's city. The man who guards the ship of state from the stern, with his hand on the tiller and never letting his eyelids close in sleep, has to say what the moment needs. If we come through this well, the credit goes to a god. But if disaster comes, and may it never come, then Eteocles is the one name that will be sung all over the city by every citizen, in a rising roar of blame and the wailing that goes with it. May Zeus the Defender live up to his name and defend the city of the Cadmeans from that.

As for you, all of you: the boy who has not yet reached the peak of his youth, and the man whom time has carried past it but who still keeps a big strong growth of body, and every man in his prime, each as fits him. You must come to the help of the city now, and of the altars of the gods of this land, so that their honours are never wiped out. Help your children, and the Earth, your mother, the nurse who loves you most. When you were small and crawling on her kind ground she took on the whole burden of raising you, and she raised you to be householders who carry a shield, and to be faithful to her in this hour of need.

And up to this day the god's scales have tipped our way. All the time we have been penned in behind our towers, the war has mostly gone well for us, and that is the gods' doing. But now the prophet, the herdsman of birds, who works without the fire of sacrifice, sorting the oracle birds with his ears and his mind by an art that does not lie, this master of prophecy says the greatest Argive assault yet is being debated in the night and planned against the city. So all of you, to the battlements and the gates of the towered walls. Move. Run, in full armour. Man the breastworks, take your stand on the platforms of the towers, hold your posts where the gates open out, and keep your courage up. Do not be too frightened of this mob of foreigners. The god will bring it to a good end. I have sent out scouts myself, men to watch their army, and I trust them not to waste the journey. Once I have heard them, no trick will catch me out.

(The Scout comes in, running.)

Scout. Eteocles, best of the Cadmeans, my lord, I have come back from the army out there with sure news. I saw what they did with my own eyes. Seven men, fierce captains of companies, cut a bull's throat over a black-rimmed shield, dipped their hands in the bull's blood, and swore by Ares, by Enyo, and by Terror who loves blood, that either they would tear this city down to its foundations and gut the town of the Cadmeans by force, or die and knead this earth into mud with their own blood. And they were hanging keepsakes of themselves on Adrastus's chariot, for their parents at home, and crying as they did it. But not one word of pity came out of their mouths. Their iron hearts were blazing with courage and breathing hard, like lions with war in their eyes.

And the news of this has not been slowed by any hesitation of mine. I left them drawing lots, to decide which gate each man would lead his company against, as chance assigned him. So pick the best men in the city and post them at the gate exits, quickly. The Argive army is already close, in full armour, coming on and raising dust, and white foam from the horses' lungs is spattering the plain in drops. You are the good helmsman of this ship. Make the town fast before the squalls of Ares break over it. The wave of their army is roaring in over dry land. Seize the fastest moment there is for this. For the rest, I will keep a faithful daylight watch with my eyes, and with the clear word I bring you will know what is happening outside the gates and come to no harm.

(The Scout goes out.)

Eteocles. Zeus, and Earth, and gods who hold this city, and Curse, the Fury of my father, so strong: do not tear my city up by the roots, do not let it be ripped out and destroyed and taken by the enemy, a city that speaks the language of Greece, with its houses and its hearths. Never let this free land, this city of Cadmus, be held under the yoke of slavery. Be our strength. What I ask is for all of us, I think. A city that does well honours its gods.

(Eteocles goes out with the citizens. The Chorus, the young women of Thebes, come rushing in, in terror.)

Chorus.
	I cry out in fear, great pains, great cries.
	The army is loose. It has left its camp,
	and here it comes, a flood of men, the horsemen out in front.
	The dust rising into the sky tells me so,
	a messenger with no voice, but clear, and true.
	And now the plains of my own country
	ring under the hooves and push the roar into my ear.
	It flies, it rumbles,
	like water no one can fight, crashing down a mountain.
	Oh, oh,
	gods and goddesses, the evil is rushing on us,
	shout it back over the walls.
	The army of the white shields is on its feet,
	ready, running at the city at full stretch.
	Who will save us then? Which of the gods
	or goddesses will stand by us?
	Shall I throw myself down
	before the old statues of my fathers' gods?
	Oh, blessed ones on your firm seats,
	now is the time to hold on to your statues.
	Why do we wait, and groan?
	Do you hear the shields clash, or don't you?
	If not now, when shall we hold up
	robes and garlands and pray?
	I see the clash. That is not the clatter of one spear.
	What will you do, Ares, old lord of this land?
	Will you betray your own?
	Oh, god of the golden helmet, look, look
	at the city you once loved so well.
Chorus.
	Gods who guard the city, all of you, come, over the land.
	Look at this band of girls on our knees,
	begging to be spared slavery.
	A wave of men with plumes slanting on their helmets
	is boiling round the town, driven by the blasts of Ares.
	Zeus, father, who brings all things to their end,
	keep us, whatever it costs, from capture by the enemy.
	The Argives are circling the fortress of Cadmus.
	Terror of their weapons of war shakes us,
	and through the horses' jaws
	the bridles whine of murder.
	Seven proud men, the pick of the army,
	in spear-shaking harness, at the seven gates
	take their stands, as the lot fell to them.
Chorus.
	And you, Zeus-born power who loves the fight,
	be the saviour of the city, Pallas.
	And you, lord of horses, ruler of the deep,
	Poseidon, with your weapon that strikes fish,
	give us release from fear, give us release.
	And you, Ares, oh, oh, guard the city
	that carries Cadmus's name; show that you care for it, out in the open.
	And Aphrodite, first mother of our race,
	keep this off us. We come from your blood.
	Crying to you with prayers a god must hear,
	we come close.
	And you, Wolf-lord Apollo, be a wolf
	to the enemy army, and give them groan for groan.
	And you, girl born of Leto,
	Artemis, our own, make your bow ready.
Chorus.
	Ah, ah, ah, ah,
	I hear the rattle of chariots round the city.
	Lady Hera!
	The hubs screamed under the loaded axles.
	Artemis, our own, ah, ah, ah, ah,
	the air is going mad with shaken spears.
	What is happening to our city? What is coming?
	Where is the god leading us, and to what end?
Chorus.
	Ah, ah, ah, ah,
	a hail of stones is coming at the tops of the battlements.
	Apollo, our own!
	At the gates the clang of bronze-bound shields.
	Son of Zeus, from whom comes
	the holy end of war, the deciding of the battle.
	And you, blessed queen Onca, before the city,
	shield your seven-gated home.
Chorus.
	Oh, gods who can do everything,
	oh, you gods and goddesses who bring things to their end,
	guardians of the towers of this land,
	do not betray a city worn out by the spear
	to an army that speaks another tongue.
	Hear us, hear us girls, as is right,
	our prayers, our hands stretched out.
Chorus.
	Oh, spirits who love us,
	stand round the city and set it free,
	show how you love this town.
	Think of the people's holy offerings,
	and as you think of them, help.
	The city loves its sacrifices and its rites.
	Remember them for me.

Eteocles. I ask you, you unbearable creatures: is this the best way? Is this how the city gets saved? Is this what gives heart to an army penned in behind its towers, you throwing yourselves down in front of the statues of the gods who hold the city and howling and shrieking, the thing every sane person hates? May I never share a house with the female race, not in bad times and not in the good times we love. When a woman has the upper hand her boldness is more than anyone can live with, and when she is frightened she is a worse evil still, for house and city both. Look at you now. Running this way and that in flight, you have shrieked a spineless cowardice into the citizens. The enemy outside the gates could not ask for better help, and we are being sacked from inside by our own people. That is what you get when you live with women. And if anyone will not listen to my command, man, woman, or whatever is in between, the vote against them is death, and there is no escaping the people's stones. What happens outside is the man's business. No woman is to make decisions about it. Stay inside and do no harm. Did you hear me or not? Or am I talking to the deaf?

Chorus.
	Dear son of Oedipus, I was afraid when I heard
	the rattle, the rattle of the crashing chariots,
	when the axle-boxes screamed as the wheels went round,
	and the steering gear in the horses' mouths,
	the fire-forged bits, sang out.

Eteocles. And so? Did a sailor ever find a way to safety by running from the stern to the prow while the ship was labouring in the open sea?

Chorus.
	No. I ran to the gods, to their ancient statues,
	trusting the gods, when the deadly blizzard,
	the snowstorm of stones, roared at the gates.
	That was when fear lifted me up to pray to the blessed ones
	to hold their strength over the city.

Eteocles. Pray that the wall keeps out the enemy's spear. That is the gods' business, yes. But they say the gods of a captured city leave it.

Chorus.
	Never in my lifetime may this gathering of gods
	leave us. Never let me see this city
	overrun, and an army
	setting it alight with the enemy's fire.

Eteocles. Call on the gods, but do not plan badly while you do it. Obedience is the mother of Success, and the wife of Safety. That is the saying.

Chorus.
	It is. But the strength of a god is higher still,
	and often, in bad times, it lifts a helpless man
	up out of hard misery,
	even with the storm clouds hanging over his eyes.

Eteocles. Slaughtered beasts and offerings to the gods, when we test the enemy: that is men's work. Yours is to keep silent and stay inside the house.

Chorus.
	It is through the gods that we live in a city no one has conquered,
	and that the wall holds off the crowd of men who hate us.
	What is there in that to resent?

Eteocles. I do not grudge you honouring the race of the gods. But so that you do not turn the citizens into cowards, be calm, and stop being so terribly afraid.

Chorus.
	I heard a strange clashing, all mixed together,
	and I came, shaking with fear, up to this citadel,
	our honoured seat of worship.

Eteocles. Then if you hear of men dying or wounded, do not snatch at the news with wailing. That is what Ares feeds on: the blood of men.

Chorus. But I do hear it. Horses, snorting.

Eteocles. Then hear it, but not so loudly.

Chorus. The town is groaning from the ground up, as if they were all round us.

Eteocles. It is enough that I am making the plans for that.

Chorus. I am afraid. The battering at the gates is getting worse.

Eteocles. Will you be quiet, and say none of this in the city?

Chorus. Gods gathered here, do not betray our walls.

Eteocles. Damn you. Will you not bear this in silence?

Chorus. Gods of my city, do not let slavery be my lot.

Eteocles. You are making a slave of me, and of the whole city, yourself.

Chorus. Zeus almighty, turn your bolt on the enemy.

Eteocles. Zeus, what a breed you have given us in women.

Chorus. A wretched one, like men whose city is taken.

Eteocles. Words of bad omen again, with your hands on the statues?

Chorus. I have no courage left, and fear is snatching at my tongue.

Eteocles. If you would only grant me one small thing I ask.

Chorus. Say it, as fast as you can, and I will know as fast.

Eteocles. Be quiet, you poor wretch. Do not frighten your own people.

Chorus. I am quiet. I will suffer what is fated, with the others.

Eteocles. I take those words of yours in place of the ones before. And one thing more: get away from the statues, and make the better prayer, that the gods fight on our side. Listen to my prayers, and then raise the holy cry of triumph, the cry of good omen, the Greek custom of shouting over a sacrifice, which gives courage to our friends and unties their fear of the enemy.

To the gods who hold this country's city, the ones who live in the plain and the ones who watch over the market place, and to the springs of Dirce and the water of Ismenus, I say this: if things go well and the city is saved, we will redden the gods' hearths with the blood of sheep and slaughter bulls for the gods. That is my vow. And I will hang trophies in their holy houses, the enemy's clothing, spoils torn from them by the spear. I will hang them up in front of the temples, the garments of our enemies.

Pray to the gods like that, not with your love of groaning, not with wild, useless panting. You will not escape what is fated one bit the more for it. I am going to post six men, and myself the seventh, to face the enemy in the grand manner at the seven exits in the walls, before fast-running messengers and hurrying words arrive and set us on fire with need.

(Eteocles goes out.)

Chorus.
	I obey. But fear will not let my heart sleep.
	Worries, neighbours to my heart,
	keep fanning the terror
	of the army all round the walls,
	the way a dove, trembling all over,
	fears for the young in her nest
	because of the snakes, bad bedfellows.
	Some of them march against the towers
	with all their people, all their host.
	What will become of me?
	Others are hurling jagged stones
	at the citizens caught on every side.
	Gods born of Zeus, by every means,
	save the city and the army
	born of Cadmus.
Chorus.
	What ground will you take in exchange for this,
	what better country, if you hand over to the enemy
	this deep-soiled land,
	and the water of Dirce, the most nourishing drink
	of all the drinks that Poseidon
	who holds the earth
	and the children of Tethys pour out?
	So, gods who hold the city,
	throw onto the men outside the towers
	the doom that destroys men,
	the ruin that makes them drop their weapons,
	and win glory for these citizens.
	Be the saviours of the city,
	stay firm in your seats,
	for these shrill, wailing prayers.
Chorus.
	It is pitiful, to throw a city as old as this
	down to Hades, the spear's prey,
	a slave, crumbling to dry ash,
	sacked without honour by an Achaean man
	because the gods willed it;
	and the women, taken, led away,
	ah, ah, young and old alike,
	dragged by the hair like horses,
	their clothes torn off round them.
	The city screams as it empties out,
	the plunder wailing in every voice as it dies.
	I shudder at the heavy fate before it comes.
Chorus.
	And it is a thing to weep for, that girls just ripe
	should be picked raw, before the wedding rites,
	and go the hateful road away from home.
	What? I say the dead man does better than they do.
	For when a city is beaten down,
	ah, ah, many and miserable are the things it suffers.
	Man drags man away, or kills him, or carries fire.
	The whole town is smeared with smoke.
	And Ares breathes over it, raging,
	the breaker of peoples, fouling everything holy.
Chorus.
	Roaring through the town, and against the town
	a towered net closing in.
	Man goes down before man, by the spear.
	Bloody bleating,
	the wails of babies fresh from the breast,
	still at the nipple.
	And looting, blood-brother to the running to and fro.
	Loaded man runs into loaded man,
	empty man calls to empty man,
	wanting a partner in it,
	and none of them wants less, none wants an equal share.
	From this you can guess the rest.
Chorus.
	Fruit of every kind, fallen to the ground,
	hurts to find, a bitter sight
	for the housekeepers' eyes.
	Heaped, jumbled, the gifts of the earth
	are swept away on worthless waves.
	And the young slave women, new to suffering:
	a wretched bed, a prisoner's bed,
	a lucky man's, an enemy's, a stronger man's,
	that is their hope now, the coming of the night's due,
	a helper to the tears and all the grief.

(The Scout is seen coming from one side, Eteocles from the other, both in a hurry.)

First Half-Chorus. The scout from the army, I think, is bringing us some fresh news, friends. Look how fast his legs are swinging as they carry him on his errand.

Second Half-Chorus. And here is our lord himself, the son of Oedipus, just in time to hear the messenger's report. Haste is spoiling his stride, too.

Scout. I can tell you the enemy's dispositions exactly, and which gate each man drew by lot. Tydeus is already roaring at the Proetid gate. But the prophet will not let him ford the Ismenus. The sacrifices are not coming out well. And Tydeus, raving, greedy for the fight, is bellowing like a snake that hisses at midday, and lashing the wise prophet, the son of Oecles, with the insult that he fawns on death and on battle out of cowardice. Shouting things like that, he shakes the three shadowing plumes, the mane of his helmet, and under his shield bells beaten out of bronze ring out fear. On his shield he has this proud sign: a sky worked in metal, blazing with stars, and bright in the middle of the shield the full moon, eldest of the stars, the eye of night. Raving like that in his boastful armour he roars along the river bank, in love with the fight, like a horse straining at the bit and panting, waiting for the trumpet's call. Who will you set against him? Who can be trusted to stand at the Proetid gate when the bars are drawn?

Eteocles. No man's finery would make me tremble, and signs do not make wounds. Plumes and bells do not bite without a spear. And this night you speak of on his shield, glittering with the stars of heaven: perhaps his folly will turn prophet for someone. If night falls on his eyes as he dies, then for the man who carries it this arrogant sign will be rightly and justly named, and he will have prophesied his own outrage against himself. Against Tydeus I will post the trusty son of Astacus as defender of this gate, a man of very good birth, who honours the throne of Shame and hates arrogant talk. He is slow to do anything disgraceful, and he does not like to be a coward. His root springs from the sown men, the ones Ares spared, and he is very much a man of this soil. His name is Melanippus. Ares will decide the outcome with his dice. But Justice, who shares his blood, is sending him out, and rightly, to keep the enemy spear from the mother who bore him.

Chorus.
	May the gods give our champion good luck,
	since he goes up in a just cause
	to fight for the city. But I tremble
	to see the bloody deaths
	of men who die for their own.

Scout. May the gods give him that luck. Capaneus drew the Electran gate, another giant, bigger than the one I told you of, and his boasting is beyond what a man should think. He threatens terrible things against our towers, and may chance not fulfil them. He says he will sack the city whether the god wants it or not, and that not even the Quarrel of Zeus, striking the ground in front of him, would stand in his way. The lightning and the thunderbolts he compares to the heat of noon. For a sign he has a naked man carrying fire, and the torch he holds as his weapon blazes in his hands, and in gold letters he says: I will burn the city. Against a man like that, send someone. Who will stand up to him? Who will wait for that boaster without trembling?

Eteocles. Here too, gain breeds gain. When men think empty thoughts, the tongue turns out to be their true accuser. Capaneus threatens, ready to act, dishonouring the gods, and giving his mouth its exercise in idle joy he sends up to heaven, a mortal man, loud swelling words for Zeus to hear. I trust that the fire-bearing thunderbolt will come to him with justice, and that it will be nothing like the heat of the noonday sun. Against him, big-mouthed as he is, a man of burning spirit is posted: the strength of Polyphontes, a guard you can trust, with the goodwill of Artemis who stands before the gate, and of the other gods. Tell me of another, and the gate he drew.

Chorus.
	Death to the man who boasts so big against the city.
	May the thunderbolt's shaft stop him
	before he bursts into my house
	and with his arrogant spear
	drives me out of my girlhood room.

Scout. Now I will tell you of the man who drew the next gate. The third lot leaped out of the upturned bronze helmet for Eteoclus, the third man, to throw his company at the Neistan gate. He is wheeling his mares, snorting in their head-straps, wanting to fall on the gate. Their muzzles whistle in a barbarian way, filled with the breath of their proud nostrils. And his shield is not modestly decorated: an armoured man climbs the rungs of a ladder up an enemy tower, meaning to sack it. And this one too shouts, in the syllables of written letters, that not even Ares could throw him off the wall. Against this man too send someone you can trust to keep the yoke of slavery from this city.

Eteocles. I would send this man now, and with luck, too. In fact he is already sent, his boast in his hands: Megareus, Creon's seed, of the race of the sown men. He will not back away from the gate for fear of the thunder of maddened horses snorting. Either he will die and pay the land in full for his rearing, or he will take two men and the city on the shield, and dress his father's house with the spoils. Boast of another, and do not grudge me the telling.

Chorus.
	I pray for him to have good luck,
	oh, champion of my home,
	and for them, bad luck.
	As they talk their great talk against the city
	with a mind gone mad,
	so may Zeus the Punisher look on them in anger.

Scout. Another, the fourth, has the gate next to Onca Athena, and stands there with a shout: Hippomedon, the shape and huge stamp of him. He spun a great threshing floor, I mean the circle of his shield, and I shuddered. I will not say otherwise. The man who worked the sign on it was no cheap craftsman: it is Typhon, sending out of his fire-breathing mouth black smoke, the flickering sister of fire, and the rim of the hollow-bellied shield is nailed all round with coils of snakes. He himself raised the war cry, and possessed by Ares he raves for the fight like a maenad, with terror in his eyes. Guard well against the attack of a man like that. Terror is already boasting at the gate.

Eteocles. First, Onca Pallas, who lives close to the city, next to the gate, and hates outrage in a man, will keep him off like a cold snake from her nestlings. And Hyperbius, the trusty son of Oenops, is chosen to meet him man to man, willing to find out his fate in the crisis that chance brings, a man with no fault in his body, in his spirit, or in the handling of his arms. Hermes has matched them well. Man will meet man as enemy, and the gods on their shields will clash as enemies too. One has fire-breathing Typhon. On Hyperbius's shield Father Zeus sits upright with a flaming bolt in his hand. And no one has ever yet seen Zeus beaten. That is how the gods' favour lies. We are on the side of the winners, they on the side of the beaten, if Zeus is stronger than Typhon in a fight. It is likely the men will fare as their gods do, and by the reading of the sign Zeus will be Hyperbius's saviour, since Zeus happens to be on his shield.

Chorus.
	I am sure of it: the man who carries Zeus's enemy on his shield,
	the unloved body of the earth-born spirit,
	an image hated by mortals
	and by the long-lived gods,
	will fling his head down in the dust before the gate.

Scout. May it be so. Now the fifth. He is posted at the fifth gate, the North Gate, right beside the tomb of Amphion, Zeus's son. He swears by the spear in his hand, which he trusts and honours more than any god and more than his own eyes, that he will sack the city of the Cadmeans in spite of Zeus. That is what he says, this beautiful shoot of a mountain-ranging mother, this man who is half boy still. The down is only now creeping across his cheeks, the first thick hair coming up as his youth comes on. But the spirit he brings up to the gate is savage, nothing like a girl's, for all that his name says girl, and the eye in his head is a Gorgon's. And he does not stand at the gate without a boast of his own. On his shield of beaten bronze, the round wall he holds in front of his body, he was swinging the shame of our city: the Sphinx, the eater of raw flesh, fastened on with bolts, her body bright and hammered up out of the metal, and under her she carries a man, one of the Cadmeans, so that as many missiles as possible will be thrown at that man. He has not come to haggle over the fighting, it seems, or to disgrace the long road he travelled. Parthenopaeus, the Arcadian. Such is the man: a resident foreigner paying Argos back for a fine upbringing, and he threatens these towers with things I pray no god brings about.

Eteocles. If only they got from the gods exactly what they intend, they and those unholy boasts of theirs. Then they would die, all of them, utterly and badly. For this one too, the Arcadian you speak of, there is a man with no boast in him, whose hand sees what has to be done: Actor, brother of the man I named before. He will not let a tongue with no deeds behind it come flooding in through the gate and swell our troubles. He will not let a man in who carries the image of that hateful biting beast on an enemy shield. She will turn on the man who carries her, from the outside in, when she meets the thick hammering of blows under the city wall. If the gods are willing, what I say may turn out true.

Chorus.
	The words go through my chest.
	The hair on my head stands up straight
	as I hear the huge threats of these huge talkers,
	these unholy men. Oh, let the gods
	destroy them here, in our land.

Scout. The sixth I would name is the most level-headed man there and the best of them in courage, a seer: mighty Amphiaraus. He is posted at the Homoloid Gate, and he is heaping abuse on mighty Tydeus. Killer of men. Wrecker of the city. Argos's great schoolmaster in evil. The man who calls up the Fury. Slaughter's servant. Adrastus's counsellor in all this wickedness. And then he turned his eyes up to heaven and spoke to your own brother, mighty Polyneices, your mother's son, and twice, at the end, he broke the name in two and called him by it, Poly-neices, Much-Strife. This is what he said, word for word:

"Is this really a deed the gods love? Is it a fine thing to hear about, a fine thing to tell the men who come after us, that you sacked the city of your fathers and the gods of your own blood, and threw a foreign army at them? What justice is there in drying up the spring that is your mother? Your fatherland, taken by the spear because you were in a hurry: how is it ever going to be your ally? As for me, I will fatten this soil, a seer buried under enemy ground. Let us fight. I do not expect a shameful death."

That is what the seer said, calmly, holding his shield of solid bronze, and there was no device on its circle at all. He does not want to seem the best. He wants to be it, and he reaps the deep furrow that runs through his mind, where the good plans grow. Send wise and brave men to row against that one, that is my advice. A man who reveres the gods is a man to be afraid of.

Eteocles. Oh, the bad luck of it, the omen that yokes a just man to the most godless of men. In every business there is nothing worse than bad company. Its crop is not worth carrying home: the field of ruin grows death for its harvest. A god-fearing man boards a ship with a crew of hotheads bent on some crime, and he goes down with that race of men the gods spit on. Or he is a just man among fellow citizens who hate strangers and have forgotten the gods, and he is caught in the same net as them, though he does not deserve it, and struck down by the god's whip that falls on everyone alike. So it is with the seer, the son of Oecles: a level-headed, just, good, god-fearing man, a great prophet, mixed in with godless, loud-mouthed men against his own judgement, men marching out in a long procession that is a long way back. If Zeus is willing, he will be dragged down with them. My own guess is that he will not even attack the gate. Not because he has no heart or a weak will, but because he knows he has to die in this battle, if the oracles of Apollo Loxias are to bear fruit. And that god likes either to say nothing or to say the thing that counts. Even so, I will post a man against him: mighty Lasthenes, a gatekeeper who hates strangers. He has an old man's mind in a body in its prime, a quick-footed eye, and a hand that is not slow to drive its spear into whatever shows past the edge of a shield. But it is the gift of a god when mortals succeed.

Chorus.
	Hear our just prayers, gods, and bring them to pass.
	Let the city come through this safe.
	Turn the hard work of the spear back
	on the men who have invaded our land.
	Let Zeus strike them outside the walls
	with his thunderbolt and kill them.

Scout. Now the seventh, at the seventh gate: your own brother, and the fate he prays for and calls down on this city. He prays that he will climb our towers and be proclaimed king over the land, shout the war-cry of victory over a captured city, and then meet you hand to hand. And having killed you, he prays to die beside you. Or, if you live, to pay you back for dishonouring him, for driving him out, with the same coin: exile. That is what mighty Polyneices is shouting, and he calls on the gods of his birth, the gods of his fathers' land, to look down on his prayers and see every one of them granted. He carries a newly made shield, a perfect circle, with a double device cleverly fastened on it. A man in armour, worked in beaten gold, is led along by a woman walking ahead of him with modest steps. She says she is Justice, and the letters on the shield say it too: "I will bring this man home, and he will have his city and the run of his father's house again." Those are the inventions on their shields. Now decide for yourself, quickly, whom you think to send. You will never be able to fault this man for what he reported. What you must decide for yourself is how to steer the city.

(The Scout goes out.)

Eteocles. Oh, my family, driven mad by the gods and hated by them, house of Oedipus, all tears. Oh no. Now my father's curses come true. But it is not right for me to weep or to wail, in case an even harder grief is born out of this one. As for the man whose name fits him so well, Polyneices, I mean, we will soon know where that device on his shield ends up, whether golden letters babbling on a shield, with a wandering mind behind them, are going to bring him home. If Justice, Zeus's unmarried daughter, had been with him in what he did and what he thought, that might have happened. But not when he came out of the darkness of his mother's womb, not while he was nursed, not when he was a boy growing up, not when the beard first gathered on his chin, not once did Justice look at him and judge him worth her while. And I do not think she is standing beside him now, while he does his own fatherland this violence. She would be false to her own name, and justly so, if she kept company with a man who would dare anything. Trusting in that, I will go and stand against him. I myself. Who has a better right? Commander against commander, brother against brother, enemy against enemy, I will take my stand. Quick, bring my greaves, my armour against spear and stone.
