Chorus. No, son of Oedipus, dearest of men, do not become the twin in temper of the man with the worst of names. It is enough that Cadmeans come to grips with Argives. That blood can be washed clean. But two brothers of one blood killing each other: that is a pollution that never grows old.

Eteocles. If a man has to suffer something bad, let it at least be without shame. That is the only profit there is among the dead. But when a thing is bad and shameful together, you will find no glory in it to speak of.

Chorus.
	What are you so hungry for, child?
	Do not let the spear-mad ruin that fills the heart
	carry you away. Throw out this evil desire
	while it is still a beginning.

Eteocles. Since the god is driving the business so hard, let the whole race of Laius, which Phoebus hates, go down the wind, its lot the wave of Cocytus.

Chorus.
	A raw-biting desire is goading you too far,
	to bring off a killing of men
	whose fruit is bitter: blood the gods forbid.

Eteocles. Yes, my own dear father's curse, hateful, wretched, sits on my dry, unweeping eyes and says: the gain comes first, and then the death.

Chorus.
	But do not let it drive you. No one will call you a coward
	for coming out of this with your life and your honour.
	Will the Fury in her black goat-skin not leave the house
	when the gods take a sacrifice from your hands?

Eteocles. The gods have already stopped caring about us, somehow. The only gift of ours they admire is our destruction. So why should I go on fawning on a deadly fate?

Chorus.
	Now, while it stands right beside you.
	Because the spirit may turn, in time,
	with a change of heart, and come on a gentler wind.
	Right now it is still boiling.

Eteocles. Yes, it boiled over: the curses of Oedipus. And the visions that came to me in my sleep were too true, dividing up my father's property.

Chorus. Listen to women, even though you do not like to.

Eteocles. Say something that can be done. And do not make it long.

Chorus. Do not go yourself, not on that road to the seventh gate.

Eteocles. I am whetted sharp. You will not blunt me with words.

Chorus. And yet a victory, even a cowardly one, is honoured by god.

Eteocles. A man in armour has no business hugging that saying to himself.

Chorus. But do you want to reap your own brother's blood?

Eteocles. When the gods give evils, you cannot run away from them.

(Eteocles goes out to the seventh gate.)

Chorus.
	I shudder at the goddess who wrecks a house,
	the goddess who is not like other gods,
	the prophet of evil who is never wrong,
	the Fury a father prayed for:
	that she will carry out the raging curses
	of Oedipus, whose mind was hurt.
	This strife that kills his sons is driving her on.
Chorus.
	A stranger is dealing out their inheritance,
	a Chalybian settler out of Scythia,
	a bitter divider of goods, savage-hearted iron.
	He has shaken the lots for the land they will live in,
	as much as the dead can hold,
	and no share in the wide plains.
Chorus.
	And when they have died, each by the other's hand,
	each cutting the other down,
	and the dust of the earth has drunk
	their black clotted blood,
	who could find a cleansing for them?
	Who could wash them? Oh,
	new troubles of this house
	mixed in with the old evils.
Chorus.
	I am speaking of the old crime,
	the crime whose punishment came fast
	and has stayed on into the third generation:
	when Laius, against Apollo,
	who told him three times
	at his Pythian oracle at the navel of the earth
	that he could save the city
	if he died without children,
Chorus.
	was overpowered by his own foolish longing
	and fathered his own death,
	Oedipus, who killed his father,
	who sowed his mother's holy field,
	the field where he himself was grown,
	and endured a root of blood.
	Madness brought that pair together,
	a bride and groom out of their minds.
Chorus.
	It is like a sea of evils driving its wave on.
	One wave falls, and the sea lifts another,
	three-crested, and it crashes and boils
	around the stern of the city.
	In between, the defence stretches thin,
	no wider than a wall.
	I am afraid the city will go down
	along with its kings.
Chorus.
	Because when a curse spoken long ago comes true,
	the settling of accounts is heavy,
	and once the deadly thing exists, it does not pass by.
	When the wealth of men who live by their gain
	grows too fat,
	it has to go overboard, from the stern.
Chorus.
	What man did the gods ever admire so much,
	the gods, and the people who share the city's hearth,
	and the crowded gathering of mortals,
	as they honoured Oedipus then,
	when he took out of our land
	the plague that snatched men away?
Chorus.
	But when he came to himself
	and understood, poor wretch, his terrible marriage,
	he could not bear the pain. With a maddened heart
	he did two evil things at once.
	With the hand that killed his father
	he struck out his own eyes,
	which he loved more than his children.
Chorus.
	And on his sons he loosed savage words,
	angry at the way they kept him, aiai,
	curses from a bitter tongue:
	that one day they would divide his goods
	with iron in their hands.
	And now I tremble
	that the Fury, on her quick feet, is bringing it about.

(The Scout comes back from the walls.)

Scout. Take heart, you daughters your mothers raised. This city has escaped the yoke of slavery. The boasts of those mighty men have fallen to the ground. The city is in fair weather. Under all the blows of the waves she has not taken on a drop of water. The wall holds, and we barred the gates with champions we could trust, one man against one man. At six of the gates all is well. But the seventh: the holy lord Apollo, the leader of sevens, took the seventh gate for himself, and on the children of Oedipus he finished the old folly of Laius.

Chorus. What new thing has happened to the city, on top of all the rest?

Scout. The city is saved. But the kings, born of the same seed—

Chorus. Who? What are you saying? Fear of your news is driving me out of my mind.

Scout. Be sensible now, and listen. The sons of Oedipus—

Chorus. Oh, I am wretched. I am a prophet of these evils.

Scout. Beyond any argument, beaten down into the dust—

Chorus. They are lying out there? It is heavy. But tell it anyway.

Scout. The men are dead, by their own hands, each killing the other.

Chorus. So with brothers' hands they slaughtered each other, both at once?

Scout. Yes, so alike was the fate that took them both, too much alike. It is that fate, all by itself, that is using up this ill-starred family. There is cause here for joy and for tears. Joy, because the city is doing well. Tears, because its two masters, the two generals, have divided up the whole of their inheritance with hammered Scythian iron. They will own exactly as much land as they take when they are buried, swept away in misery, just as their father's curses said. The city is saved. But the earth has drunk the blood of the two kings born of the same seed, each murdered by the other.

(The Scout goes out.)

Chorus.
	Great Zeus, and you gods who hold the city,
	you who truly guard these towers of Cadmus,
	which do I do?
	Shout for joy and raise the women's cry
	because the city is safe and unharmed,
	or weep for the war-lords,
	miserable, ill-starred, and childless,
	who lived up to their names,
	men of much strife,
	and died of their godless intent?
Chorus.
	Black curse on the race of Oedipus,
	curse that has come to its end,
	a cold evil is falling round my heart.
	I have made a song for the tomb,
	like a wild woman of Dionysus,
	hearing of corpses dripping blood,
	dead by a miserable fate.
	It is a bird of bad omen,
	this music played to the spear.
Chorus.
	It has done its work, it did not fall short,
	the prayer a father spoke.
	The plans of Laius, made in disbelief, held their force.
	And I am anxious for the city:
	what the gods have said does not go blunt.
	Oh, men of many groans,
	you have done a thing past belief.
	Sorrows to scream at have come, and not in words.

(The bodies of the two brothers are carried in.)

Chorus.
	It is plain to see. The Scout's report is here in front of us.
	Double troubles, twin men killed by each other,
	a double share of suffering, complete.
	What can I say?
	What else but this: pain on pain
	sits at this house's hearth.
	Come, friends, on the wind of grief,
	row with your hands about your heads,
	the stroke that sends the dead on their way,
	the stroke that always carries across Acheron
	the black-sailed ship of pilgrims with no sail spread,
	to the shore where Apollo never walks,
	the sunless shore that takes in everyone,
	the land no one can see.
	But here they come to their bitter task,
	Antigone and Ismene,
	to keen for their two brothers.
	There is no doubt in my mind that from their lovely,
	deep-folded breasts they will send up
	a grief worthy of the dead.
	But it is right that we, before their voices,
	shriek out the ugly hymn of the Fury
	and then sing the victory-song
	that Hades loves and we hate.
	Oh, unluckiest in your brothers of all women
	who fasten a belt about their dress,
	I cry, I groan, and there is no pretence in it:
	this shrill lament comes straight from my heart.
Chorus.
	Oh. Oh. Men of wrong minds,
	whom your own people could not persuade
	and no evil could wear down,
	you captured your father's house
	with the spear, in your misery.
	Misery, yes. They found miserable deaths,
	and outraged their house to find them.
Chorus.
	Oh. Oh. You who were set to knock down
	the walls of your own home,
	who had eyes for one bitter thing, sole rule,
	you are reconciled now,
	with iron.
	And all too truly the queenly Fury
	of your father Oedipus has brought it to pass.
Chorus.
	Struck through the left side,
	struck, yes,
	through the flanks that grew in one womb.
	Aiai, the god in it.
	Aiai, the curses,
	death answering death.
	Struck clean through, you say,
	in their bodies and in their house,
	by a rage that has no voice,
	by the doom their father's curse called down,
	a doom that did not divide them.
Chorus.
	And the groaning runs right through the city.
	The towers groan.
	The ground that loved these men groans.
	But the property stays, for the ones who come after,
	the property through which these ill-fated men,
	through which the quarrel came,
	and death, the end of it.
	Quick-tempered, they shared out the goods
	so that each got an equal share.
	The one who reconciled them is not blameless
	to those who loved them,
	and there is no grace in Ares.
Chorus.
	Struck down by iron, this is how they are,
	and struck down by iron, what waits for them?
	Someone might ask that. What waits for them
	is their share of their father's tomb.
	Our loud, shrieking, tearing grief
	sends them on their way from this house,
	a groan for our own selves, a pain of our own,
	sick at heart, with no joy in it,
	weeping real tears from the heart,
	the heart that wastes away as I weep
	for these two lords.
Chorus.
	And it can be said over these wretched men
	that they did much to the citizens,
	and much to the ranks of all the foreigners
	who died by the crowd in the fighting.
	Ill-starred, beyond every woman
	who is called a mother, is the woman who bore them.
	She took her own child for her husband
	and bore these two,
	and this is how they ended,
	killing each other with hands
	born of the same seed.
Chorus.
	Of the same seed, yes, and both destroyed,
	in a parting with no love in it,
	in strife gone mad,
	at the end of their quarrel.
	Their hatred has stopped.
	Their lives are mixed together
	in the earth that runs with their blood.
	Now they truly are of one blood.
	Bitter, the settler of their quarrels,
	the stranger from across the sea,
	the iron whetted sharp and rushed out of the fire.
	Bitter too was Ares, the wicked divider of goods,
	who made their father's curse come true.
Chorus.
	They have their share, poor wretches,
	their allotment of the griefs the gods gave.
	Under their bodies
	the wealth of earth will have no bottom.
	Oh, you have crowned your family
	with troubles, like a garland.
	At the last the Curses have raised their war-cry,
	the shrill tune, over a family
	routed and put to flight on every side.
	A trophy to Ruin stands at the gate
	where they struck each other down,
	and the god, having mastered them both,
	stopped there.

(Antigone stands by the body of Polyneices, Ismene by the body of Eteocles. The two sisters sing in answer to each other.)

Antigone. You struck, and were struck.

Ismene. You killed, and died.

Antigone. With the spear you killed—

Ismene. With the spear you died—

Antigone. Misery in the doing.

Ismene. Misery in the suffering.

Antigone. Let the wailing come.

Ismene. Let the tears come.

Antigone. You lie there dead—

Ismene. Who did the killing.

Antigone.
	Ah.
Ismene.
	Ah.
Antigone.
	My mind is mad with grief.
Ismene.
	The heart groans inside me.
Antigone.
	Oh. Oh. You, whom I mourn for everything.
Ismene.
	And you, wretched in everything.
Antigone.
	By one of your own you were destroyed.
Ismene.
	And one of your own you killed.
Antigone.
	Double to tell—
Ismene.
	Double to see—
Antigone.
	these griefs, brother beside brother.
Ismene.
	Brothers' sorrows beside brothers'.
Chorus.
	Oh, Fate, hard giver of hard gifts,
	and you, great shadow of Oedipus,
	black Fury, how strong you are.
Antigone.
	Ah.
Ismene.
	Ah.
Antigone.
	Sorrows hard to look at—
Ismene.
	he showed me, coming home from exile.
Antigone.
	And he never got home, once he had killed.
Ismene.
	He was saved, and lost his breath.
Antigone.
	He lost it, yes, and more.
Ismene.
	And robbed this one of his.
Antigone.
	Wretched family.
Ismene.
	Wretched suffering.
Antigone.
	Griefs of one blood, full of groans.
Ismene.
	Drenched with sorrows three times over.
Chorus.
	Oh, Fate, hard giver of hard gifts,
	and you, great shadow of Oedipus,
	black Fury, how strong you are.
Antigone.
	You know her now. You crossed through her—
Ismene.
	And you learned no later than he—
Antigone.
	when you came back to the city,
Ismene.
	yes, to row against this one with your spear.
Antigone.
	Deadly to tell.
Ismene.
	Deadly to see.
Antigone.
	Oh, the pain—
Ismene.
	Oh, the evils—
Antigone.
	for house and land.
Ismene.
	And above all for me.
Antigone.
	And further still, for me.
Ismene.
	Oh. Oh. The groaning evils, my king.
Antigone.
	Oh, most mourned of all men.
Ismene.
	Oh. Oh. Possessed by Ruin.
Antigone.
	Oh. Oh. Where in the earth shall we lay them?
Ismene.
	Oh. Where the honour is greatest.
Antigone.
	Oh. Oh. A grief to lie beside their father.

(A Herald comes from the city.)

Herald. I am to announce what the people's councillors of this city of Cadmus have resolved and decreed. Eteocles here, for his loyalty to this land, is to be buried in a kindly grave dug in its soil. He hated the enemy and chose to die in the city. He kept faith with the shrines of his fathers, and he died without a stain on him, in the place where it is a fine thing for the young to die. That is what I was ordered to say about him. But his brother, this corpse of Polyneices, is to be thrown outside the city unburied, for the dogs to tear. He would have been the destroyer of the Cadmeans' land, if one of the gods had not stood in the way of him with his brother's spear. Even dead, he will carry the guilt of the gods of his fathers, whom he dishonoured when he threw a foreign army at this city to take it. So this is his reward, so the council rules: to be buried without honour, under the birds of the air. No hands are to heap a mound over him, no one is to pay him the respect of shrill wailing, and he is to be denied the honour of being carried to the grave by the people who loved him. That is what the Cadmeans' council has decreed.

Antigone. And I have something to say to the rulers of the Cadmeans. If no one else is willing to help me bury this man, I will bury him myself, and take the risk of burying my own brother, and I am not ashamed of this defiance, this refusal to obey the city. There is something to be feared in the womb we shared, born as we were from a wretched mother and an unlucky father. So, my soul, share his evils willingly, though he had no will in it. Be alive with the dead, one heart with my brother. No hollow-bellied wolf is going to tear the flesh off this man. Let no one imagine it. I will find him a grave and a burial, even though I am a woman, carrying the earth in the fold of my linen dress, and I will cover him with my own hands. Let no one decree otherwise. Take heart. I will find a way to get it done.

Herald. I order you not to force the city's hand like this.

Antigone. I order you not to waste your proclamations on me.

Herald. A people that has just escaped disaster can be harsh.

Antigone. Be harsh, then. This man will not go unburied.

Herald. What? A man the city hates, and you will honour him with a grave?

Antigone. The gods finished settling their account with him long ago.

Herald. No. Not until he put this land in danger.

Antigone. He was treated badly, and he gave back bad for bad.

Herald. But he did it to all of us, not to one man.

Antigone. Strife is the last of the gods to end an argument. I am going to bury him. Stop making speeches.

Herald. Follow your own counsel, then. But I forbid it.

(The Herald goes out.)

Chorus.
	Oh. Oh.
	Great boasters, wreckers of families,
	you Fates, you Furies, who have destroyed
	the race of Oedipus like this, from the root,
	what is to become of me? What do I do? What plan do I make?
	How can I bring myself neither to weep for you
	nor to walk beside you to your tomb?
	But I am afraid. I turn away
	in terror of the citizens.
	You, at least, will have many mourners.
	But he, poor wretch, goes off unwept,
	with one sister's song for his whole dirge.
	Who could go along with that?
First Half-Chorus.
	Let the city do what it likes, or not,
	to those who weep for Polyneices.
	We will go and bury him with her,
	we will walk beside his body.
	This grief belongs to the whole race,
	and what the city praises as just
	is one thing one day and another the next.
Second Half-Chorus.
	And we will go with this one,
	as the city and justice together approve.
	Because after the blessed gods and the strength of Zeus,
	it was this man, more than any other,
	who kept the city of the Cadmeans from capsizing,
	from going under
	in a wave of foreign men.

(All go out, the two processions taking the two bodies in opposite directions.)

The Suppliants (c. 463 BC)

The Suppliants

(The shore of Argos, near a mound where the images of the city's gods stand together. Fifty young women come in from the sea, dark-skinned and dressed in Egyptian linen, each carrying a branch wound with white wool. Their father Danaus is with them.)

Chorus.
	Zeus, god of the suppliant, look kindly
	on our company, that put to sea
	from the fine-sanded mouths of the Nile.
	We have left the land of Zeus,
	whose pastures run up against Syria, and we are fugitives,
	not because any city voted to banish us
	for blood on our hands,
	but by our own choice. We are running from men.
	We hate the marriage the sons of Aegyptus want,
	and we call it what it is: impious.
	Danaus, our father, head of our councils,
	head of our revolt, moved the pieces on the board
	and picked, out of all the griefs on offer,
	the one with most honour in it:
	to run, headlong, over the salt waves,
	and put in at the land of Argos.
	For that is where our race began, and we are proud of it:
	from the heifer the gadfly tormented,
	from the touch and the breath of Zeus.
	What country could we come to
	kinder than this one,
	with these in our hands, the suppliant's only weapons,
	branches wreathed in wool?
	City, land, clear water,
	gods on high, and you gods below,
	heavy in your punishments, who keep the graves,
	and Zeus the Saviour, called on third,
	who guards the houses of righteous men:
	receive this company of women as your suppliants,
	and let the land breathe respect on us.
	But that swarm of men, that crowd of outrage born of Aegyptus,
	before they set one foot on this marshy shore,
	drive them out to sea, them and their fast-oared ship,
	and there let them meet the whirlwind,
	the storm that beats down, the thunder and lightning,
	the rain-heavy winds on a wild sea.
	Let them die out there,
	before they ever climb into beds that Right forbids them,
	before they take their uncle's daughters, us,
	against our will, and call us theirs.
Chorus.
	Now I call on the calf of Zeus,
	our champion from across the sea,
	the son of the cow our ancestress,
	the cow who grazed among the flowers,
	the child of the breath of Zeus.
	Zeus touched her. Her time came to term
	and gave the child his name, and the name was right:
	she bore Epaphus, the Touched One.
Chorus.
	I call on him.
	And now, here in the grassy places
	where our ancient mother pastured,
	I will remember what she suffered long ago,
	and I will show the people of this land
	sure proofs of it.
	Some of it they will not expect, but it will be seen to be true.
	Anyone who hears the whole story through
	will know it for the truth.
Chorus.
	And if there is someone near, a native of this place,
	who reads the cries of birds,
	when he hears our pitiful lament
	he will think he is hearing the voice
	of Metis, Tereus' wife, the pitiable one,
	the nightingale the hawk drives before it,
Chorus.
	who, shut out from her green leaves,
	grieves and cries for the haunts she loved,
	and puts together the song of her child's death:
	how he died by her own hand,
	killed by his own mother,
	when he met her unnatural rage.
Chorus.
	So I too, who love to lament,
	in the Ionian way,
	tear at my soft cheek
	that the Nile's sun has ripened,
	and at a heart that has never learned to weep.
	I gather the flowers of grief,
	afraid, wondering whether we have friends here at all,
	whether there is one person who will take up the cause
	of this flight of ours from the land of mists.
Chorus.
	But gods of our race, hear us.
	Look well at what is just.
	Do not let the young have all they want
	against what is due. Hate outrage, be quick to hate it,
	and you will be just toward marriage.
	Even for men beaten in war and running from it
	there is an altar, a defence against harm,
	the awe that the gods command.
Chorus.
	May Zeus make it come out well, truly well.
	The desire of Zeus is not easy to hunt down.
	It blazes everywhere,
	even in the dark, with black fortune,
	for the peoples of the earth.
Chorus.
	It lands safe, and not on its back,
	whatever thing the head of Zeus has nodded into being.
	The paths of his mind
	run thick and shadowed,
	and no eye can follow them.
Chorus.
	He hurls men down from the high towers of their hopes
	into utter destruction.
	He arms no violence against them.
	Everything the gods do is done without effort.
	Seated, he thinks his thought,
	and from where he sits, somehow, it is done,
	all of it, there on his holy throne.
Chorus.
	So let him look at human outrage
	and see how it grows young again,
	an old stock sprouting new shoots
	because of this marriage they want with us,
	with minds that no advice can turn,
	a mad purpose for its goad
	that nothing can escape,
	and ruin waiting, which it will know too late, when it is deceived.
Chorus.
	Such sufferings I cry out in my grief,
	shrill and deep, and the tears fall with them,
	ieh, ieh,
	the wailing that belongs at funerals.
	Alive, I sing my own dirge.
Chorus.
	I call on the hill country of Apia to be kind to me.
	Land, you understand my foreign speech well enough.
	Again and again I fall on my Sidonian veil
	and tear it, and the linen rips to rags.
Chorus.
	When things go well, and death keeps its distance,
	the gods get their sacrifices, freely and in full.
	Oh, oh,
	oh, troubles too tangled to read.
	Where will this wave carry me?
Chorus.
	I call on the hill country of Apia to be kind to me.
	Land, you understand my foreign speech well enough.
	Again and again I fall on my Sidonian veil
	and tear it, and the linen rips to rags.
Chorus.
	The oar and the house of timber
	stitched with linen rope, that kept the sea out,
	carried me here on the winds and no storm touched me.
	I have no complaint.
	But in time to come let the Father
	who sees everything
	make the ending kind:
Chorus.
	that the great seed of our honoured mother
	escape the beds of men, ah, ah,
	unmarried, untamed.
Chorus.
	And let the pure daughter of Zeus,
	who keeps her holy face steady and safe,
	look on me, willing, as I am willing.
	Angry at this hunt, with all her strength,
	a virgin, let her come to a virgin's rescue,
	and set me free:
Chorus.
	that the great seed of our honoured mother
	escape the beds of men, ah, ah,
	unmarried, untamed.
Chorus.
	And if she will not, then we,
	a race burned black by the sun,
	will go with our branches
	to the Zeus of the earth below,
	the Zeus who hosts more guests than any,
	the Zeus of the dead,
	and we will get there by the noose,
	if the gods of Olympus will not hear us.
Chorus.
	Ah, Zeus. It is Io's poison,
	a wrath from the gods that hunts us down.
	I know your wife's spite. It conquered heaven.
	After a harsh wind comes the storm.
Chorus.
	And then will Zeus not be caught
	by charges he cannot answer,
	for dishonouring the child of the cow,
	the child he made himself, long ago,
	and turning his face away
	from our prayers now?
	Let him hear, from high up, when we call him.
Chorus.
	Ah, Zeus. It is Io's poison,
	a wrath from the gods that hunts us down.
	I know your wife's spite. It conquered heaven.
	After a harsh wind comes the storm.

Danaus. Children, you have to keep your heads. You came here with a father who keeps his: an old man you can trust, and the captain of your ship. Now that we are on land I have thought ahead, and I want you to write what I say on the tablets of your minds and keep it there.

I see dust, an army's messenger that makes no sound. The axles are turning in their sockets, and they are not quiet. I can make out a crowd of men with shields and spears, and horses, and curved chariots. Most likely the rulers of this land have heard from their scouts and are coming to look at us. Whether the man who set this expedition moving means no harm, or has been sharpened to a cruel rage, it is better in every way, girls, to sit down at this mound, where the gods of the land stand gathered together. An altar is stronger than a fortress wall. It is a shield that cannot be broken.

So go, as fast as you can. Hold your branches wreathed in white wool, the emblems of Zeus who respects the suppliant, solemnly, in your left hands. Answer the strangers in the language of respect, of grief, of sheer need, as newcomers should, and tell them plainly that this flight of yours has no blood on it. Above all, let there be nothing bold in your voices. Let nothing shameless come from your faces: modest brows, quiet eyes. Do not run ahead of them in talk and do not drag behind. The people here are very easily offended. And remember to give way. You are a stranger in need, a fugitive. Bold tongues do not suit the weak.

Chorus. Father, you speak good sense, and you are speaking to daughters who have some. I will take care to remember these wise orders of yours. And may Zeus, the father of our race, look on us.

Danaus. May he look, indeed, and with a kind eye.

Chorus. I would like to be sitting close beside you now.

Danaus. Then do not waste time. Let the plan become action.

Chorus. Zeus, pity our labours. Do not let us be destroyed.

Danaus. If he wills it, all this will end well.

Danaus. And now call on this bird of Zeus here too.

Chorus. We call on the saving rays of the sun.

Danaus. And on pure Apollo, a god who was once himself an exile from heaven.

Chorus. He knows what that fate is. He may feel for mortals.

Danaus. May he feel for us indeed, and stand beside us, ready to help.

Chorus. Which of these gods should I call on next?

Danaus. I see a trident here, the sign of its god.

Chorus. He sent us here safely. Let him receive us safely on his land.

Danaus. And here is Hermes, another god, as the Greeks set him up.

Chorus. Then let him bring good news to free women.

Danaus. Honour the altar that all these lords share. Sit down on holy ground like a flock of doves in terror of hawks, birds of the same feather, kin and enemies at once, who would defile their own blood. If a bird eats a bird, how can it be pure? And if a man marries a woman who does not want him, taking her from a father who does not want to give her, how can he be pure? Not even in Hades, when he is dead, will a man who has done that escape the charge of outrage. There too, so people say, another Zeus sits in judgement on wrongdoing among the dead, and passes the last sentence. Watch carefully, and answer in the way I told you, so that this business comes out a victory for you.

(The King of Argos comes in with armed men.)

King. Where has this crowd come from, that we are speaking to? There is nothing Greek in the way you dress: you lounge in barbarian robes and thick, close-woven headbands. This is not the clothing of the women of Argos, or of anywhere in Greece. And how you dared to come to this country without fear, with no heralds to announce you, no one here to sponsor you, and no guides, that is a wonder to me. And yet I see branches laid beside you in front of the gathered gods, the kind that suppliants carry by custom. On that one point alone Greece can make a confident guess. About everything else it would be fair to go on guessing, if there were not a voice here present to tell me.

Chorus. About our clothes you have spoken nothing but the truth. But I, how am I to address you? As a private citizen, as a spokesman carrying the sacred staff, or as the ruler of the city?

King. On that point, answer me and speak without fear. I am Pelasgus, son of Palaechthon, whom the earth itself brought forth, and I rule this land. The race of the Pelasgians, who farm this ground, takes its name from me, its king, as is right. All the country that the pure Strymon runs through, on the side towards the setting sun, is mine. I set my borders at the land of the Perrhaebians, at the parts beyond Pindus near the Paeonians, and at the mountains of Dodona. The edge of the wet sea cuts my land short. Everything on this side of those lines, I rule.

And the ground itself, this plain of Apia, has carried that name from long ago in honour of a healer. Apis, healer and prophet, son of Apollo, came from Naupactus on the far shore and cleaned this land of monsters that were killing men, creatures that the earth itself, stained with the pollution of old bloodshed, had sent up in her anger: a colony of swarming snakes, a hateful thing to live beside. Apis cut those plagues out and set us free of them, and did it without a fault, and the land of Argos paid him back by remembering him in its prayers.

Now you have my account of myself. Declare your own ancestry and go on from there. But our city does not care for long speeches.

Chorus. Our story is short and clear. We claim to be Argives by birth, the seed of a cow blessed in her child. And every word of it is true. I will fix it to the story, point by point.

King. Strangers, what you are telling me is past believing: that your race is from Argos. You look far more like the women of Libya, and nothing at all like the women born here. The Nile might raise a plant like you. And there is the Cyprian stamp, struck on the female form by male craftsmen, and it looks like yours. I hear too that there are nomad women who ride camels as if they were horses, on padded saddles, in a country next door to the Ethiopians. And if you were armed with bows I would certainly have taken you for Amazons, the women who live without men and eat raw meat. But teach me, and I will understand better how your birth and your seed come from Argos.

Chorus. Is there a story that once, here in the land of Argos, Io kept the keys of Hera's house?

King. There certainly is. The tradition is very strong.

Chorus. And is there some story too that Zeus lay with a mortal woman?

King. There is. And that embrace was no secret from Hera.

Chorus. How did this quarrel between the royal pair end?

King. The goddess of Argos turned the woman into a cow.

Chorus. And while she was a cow with horns, did Zeus not come near her?

King. So they say, in the shape of a bull that mounts cows.

Chorus. And what did the strong wife of Zeus do about that?

King. She set the all-seeing one to watch the cow.

Chorus. What all-seeing herdsman with a single cow do you mean?

King. Argus, the son of Earth. Hermes killed him.

Chorus. What else did she devise against the unlucky cow?

King. A sting that drives cattle and keeps them moving.

Chorus. The people by the Nile call it the gadfly.

King. So it drove her out of the land on a long, long run.

Chorus. Everything you have said fits my story exactly.

King. And so she came to Canobus, and to Memphis.

Chorus. And Zeus laid his hand on her, and fathered a son by the touch.

King. Then who claims to be the calf of Zeus, born of the cow?

Chorus. Epaphus, and the name is true: he is called after the touch that set her free.

Chorus. Libya, who reaps the largest portion of the earth.

King. And what other offspring of hers can you name?

Chorus. Belus, who had two sons and was the father of my father here.

King. Now tell me this very wise name of his.

Chorus. Danaus. And he has a brother with fifty sons.

King. Open up his name too. Do not hold it back.

Chorus. Aegyptus. Now you know our ancient line. Act on it, and raise up this company, which is Argive.

King. I think you do have a share in this land from long ago. But how did you bring yourselves to leave your father's house? What stroke of fortune came down on you?

Chorus. Lord of the Pelasgians, the troubles of human beings come in many colours. You will not find two feathers of pain the same. Who would have guessed that a family related to you from old times would run here, on a flight no one expected, and put in at Argos in terror, because it loathed a marriage bed?

King. Why do you say you have come as suppliants to these gods gathered here, with those fresh-cut branches wreathed in white?

Chorus. So that I do not become a slave to the sons of Aegyptus.

King. Because you hate them? Or are you saying it would be against the law?

Chorus. Who would buy their masters from among their own kin?

King. That is how families grow their power.

Chorus. Yes, and when things go badly it is easy to get rid of a wife.

King. Then how am I to do my duty by the gods where you are concerned?

Chorus. By not handing us back to the sons of Aegyptus when they ask for us.

King. That is a heavy thing you ask: to take on a new war.

Chorus. But Justice stands over the men who fight beside her.

King. Yes, if she had a share in the business from the beginning.

Chorus. Then respect the city's stern, the altar, wreathed as you see it with our branches.

King. I shudder, looking at these seats in the shadow of the branches.

Chorus. But the anger of Zeus, god of suppliants, is heavy.

Chorus.
	Son of Palaechthon, lord of the Pelasgians,
	hear me with a kind heart.
	Look at me, your suppliant, a fugitive, running in circles,
	like a heifer that wolves are chasing over sheer rocks,
	where she trusts the herdsman's strength
	and lows to tell him her trouble.

King. I see this company of the gathered gods nodding in the shade of fresh-cut branches. Let this business of our city's guests bring no harm. Let no quarrel come on the city out of nowhere, unforeseen and unprepared for. The city has no need of that.

Chorus.
	Let Right, the suppliant's goddess, daughter of Zeus who portions out,
	look on this flight and see that it brings no harm.
	And you, old in wisdom, learn from someone younger:
	if you respect the one who turns to you for help,
	you will not go short. The gods accept offerings
	from a man who is pure.

King. It is not at the hearth of my own house that you sit. If the city is polluted as a whole, let the people as a whole work out the cure. I will make no promise before I have shared all this with every citizen.

Chorus.
	You are the city. You are the people.
	You are a ruler no one judges.
	You rule the altar, the hearth of the land,
	with the single vote of your nod.
	On a throne with a single sceptre
	you decide every matter. Guard against pollution.

King. Pollution on my enemies. But I cannot help you without harm. And it is not wise, either, to dishonour these prayers. I have no way out. Fear holds my mind: to act, or not to act, and take what fortune gives.

Chorus.
	Look up at the one who watches from above,
	the guardian of mortals worn out with trouble,
	who sit at their neighbours' doors
	and do not get the justice the law owes them.
	The anger of Zeus, god of suppliants, stays,
	and the sufferer's cries will not soften it.

King. If the sons of Aegyptus have power over you by the law of your own city, claiming to be your nearest kin, who would want to stand against them? You must plead by the laws of the home you fled that they have no authority over you at all.

Chorus.
	Never, never let me fall into the hands
	of men and their power. I steer by the stars
	and I mark out my escape from a marriage
	that sickens my heart: flight. Take Justice for your ally
	and give the judgement that reveres the gods.

King. The judgement is not easy. Do not make me the judge. I have said it already: even though I rule, I will not do this without the people, or one day, if something goes wrong, they will say, "You honoured foreigners, and you destroyed your city."

Chorus.
	Zeus, kin to both sides, looks down on this,
	tipping his scale to one side or the other,
	giving, as is right, wrong to the wicked
	and holy things to those who keep the law.
	When the weights are balanced so evenly,
	why are you sorry to do what is just?

King. We need deep thought here, thought that saves, an eye that sees clearly and is not too drunk with wine, to go down like a diver into the depths, so that this comes out without harm, first for the city and then for ourselves, and ends well; so that no fight over hostages fastens on us, and so that we do not hand you over, settled as you are in the seats of the gods, and bring in to live with us the god who destroys everything, the heavy avenger, who does not set a man free even in Hades, even dead. Do you really think there is no need of thought that saves?

Chorus.
	Think, and be,
	with all justice, our reverent
	protector. Do not betray the fugitive,
	driven from far away
	by a godless expulsion.
Chorus.
	Do not see me dragged
	from these seats of many gods
	as a prize, you who hold all the power in the land.
	Recognise the outrage of these men
	and guard against the anger.
Chorus.
	Do not bring yourself to see your suppliant
	hauled from the images of the gods by force,
	against justice, like a horse by the headband,
	with hands seizing my robes
	of many-coloured weave.
Chorus.
	Know this: whichever way you settle it,
	your children and your house
	are left to pay the debt
	in full, measure for measure.
	Think about this. The power of Zeus is just.

King. I have thought about it. And this is where I run aground. Against one side or the other I am forced to take up a great war. There is no way round it. The hull is bolted tight, as if the shipwrights' windlasses had drawn it in. There is no ending anywhere without pain.

When property is plundered from a house, more can come, by the grace of Zeus who guards a man's goods, a fresh cargo bigger than the loss. And when a tongue has shot its arrows wide of the mark, one speech can heal the wound another made, a soothing word for the bitter pain that stirs the heart. But to keep kindred blood from being shed, there must be sacrifice, and many victims must fall to many gods, to heal the harm before it comes. Truly, I have come deep into this quarrel, and I am the loser by it. I would rather be ignorant of trouble than an expert in it. But let it go well, against my expectation.

Chorus. Hear now the last of my many respectful words.

King. I hear you. Speak. It will not escape me.

Chorus. I have breast-bands and belts to gather up my robes.

King. Such things go well with women, no doubt.

Chorus. Out of these, then, understand me, there is a fine device.

King. Tell me what words you are about to utter.

Chorus. If you will not give this company some pledge it can trust—

King. What does your device of belts accomplish for you?

Chorus. To decorate these images with new tablets.

King. That is a riddle. Say it plainly.

Chorus. To hang ourselves from these gods, as fast as we can.

King. I have heard a word that is a whip across my heart.

Chorus. You understand. I have given you eyes to see it clearly.

King. Yes, and on every side there are things hard to wrestle down. A mass of troubles is coming at me like a river. I have put out onto a bottomless sea of ruin with no easy crossing, and there is no harbour from trouble anywhere. If I do not pay you this debt, the pollution you speak of is beyond any arrow's range. And if I stand in front of my walls against your own blood, the sons of Aegyptus, and fight it out to the end, how is that not a bitter price to pay: men's blood staining the ground for the sake of women? And yet I have to respect the anger of Zeus who guards the suppliant. Among mortals there is no fear higher than that.

You, old father of these girls, take these branches in your arms at once and lay them on the other altars of our country's gods, so that every citizen sees the sign that you have come as suppliants, and no word of blame is thrown at me. The people love to find fault with those in charge. And it may be that someone, seeing this and pitying it, will hate the outrage of that male expedition, and the people will be better disposed to you. Everyone feels goodwill towards the weaker side.

Danaus. This is worth a great deal to us: to have found a protector who respects us. But send escorts and guides with me, natives of the country, so that we can find the altars in front of the temples of the gods who keep your city, and their seats, and go through the town in safety. Our shape is not like yours. The Nile does not raise the same stock as the Inachus. Take care that boldness does not give birth to fear. Men have killed a friend before now, not knowing him.

King. Go with him, men. The stranger speaks well. Lead him to the city's altars, the seats of the gods. And do not talk at length with anyone you meet on the way, while you are bringing this seafarer to sit at the hearths of the gods.

(Danaus goes out with the King's men.)

Chorus. You have told him what to do, and let him go with his orders. But what about me? What am I to do? Where do you set my safety?

King. Leave your branches here, the sign of your trouble.

Chorus. There. I leave them, at your word and at your hand's signal.

King. Now move down into this open grove around the sanctuary.

Chorus. But how can a grove that is not holy ground protect me?

King. We will not hand you over to be snatched away by birds of prey.

Chorus. But what if it is to men more hateful than cruel snakes?

King. Speak well of what may come, when you are spoken to so well.

Chorus. It is no wonder if fear in the mind makes me hard to bear with.

King. Fear that goes too far is always beyond control.

Chorus. Then cheer my heart, with deeds as well as words.

King. Your father will not leave you alone for long. I am going now to call together the people of this land and make the community friendly to you, and I will teach your father what he ought to say. So stay here, and pray to the gods of this country for what your heart longs for, while I go to arrange it. May persuasion go with me, and the luck that gets things done.

(The King goes out with his attendants.)

Chorus.
	Lord of lords, most blessed of the blessed,
	power most perfect of all perfect powers,
	Zeus, the fortunate one: hear us.
	Keep the outrage of these men away from your own blood.
	Hate it well, and keep it far off.
	Throw their ruin, their black-benched ships,
	into the purple sea.
Chorus.
	Look at the women's side of this.
	Look at our race, old in the story,
	and at the woman you loved, our ancestor,
	and renew the kind old tale.
	Remember it all, you who laid your hand on Io.
	We say we are the children of Zeus,
	settlers who went out from this land.
Chorus.
	I have come back to the old tracks,
	to the meadow where my mother was watched
	as she grazed among the flowers,
	the pasture rich for cattle, where Io,
	stung to madness by the gadfly,
	ran with her wits gone,
	passing through tribe after tribe of men,
	and, as fate had cut it, split the surging strait in two
	and marked out the land on the other shore.
Chorus.
	She races on through the land of Asia,
	straight through Phrygia, where the sheep graze,
	past the Mysian city of Teuthras,
	through the hollows of Lydia,
	over the mountains of the Cilicians
	and the tribes of the Pamphylians,
	over rivers that never run dry,
	over deep, rich earth,
	and the land of Aphrodite, heavy with wheat.
Chorus.
	Driven on by the sting of the winged herdsman,
	she comes at last to the holy grove of Zeus
	that feeds every living thing,
	to the meadow fed by snow that Typhon's fury attacks,
	to the water of the Nile that no sickness can touch,
	maddened by torments no one should have to suffer
	and by the pain of the goad that pierced her,
	Hera's frenzied dancer.
Chorus.
	And the mortals who lived in that land then
	shook with green fear in their hearts
	at a sight they had never seen,
	a creature hard to look at, half beast and half human,
	one part cow
	and one part woman. They stared at the monster.
	And then, who was it who soothed her,
	the far wanderer, the wretched one,
	Io, driven by the gadfly?
Chorus.
	Zeus, who rules through endless time.
	Zeus.
	By the strength of his hand, which does no harm,
	and by his divine breath,
	she was stilled, and she wept out
	the grief and the shame in tears.
	She took Zeus into herself, and the story is true:
	she bore a child without a fault,
Chorus.
	blessed through the long ages.
	And so the whole earth shouts,
	This truly is the child of Zeus,
	who gives life to everything.
	For who else could have stopped
	the sickness Hera plotted against her?
	Say it was the work of Zeus, say this race
	comes from Epaphus, and you have the truth.
Chorus.
	Which of the gods has done deeds
	that give me a better right to call on him?
	The father himself, the planter, who made us with his own hand,
	the lord, the great, old-minded builder of our race,
	who devises everything, Zeus, the fair wind behind us.
Chorus.
	He does not sit under anyone's command,
	a lesser power ruling at a greater one's pleasure.
	No one is enthroned above him whose strength he bows to.
	His deed is as quick as his word.
	Whatever his counselling mind carries, he hurries to do it.

(Danaus comes back from the city.)

Danaus. Take heart, children. All is well with the people of this land. The people have voted, and the decrees they passed carry full authority.

Chorus. Welcome, old man, welcome, dearest of all messengers to me. Tell us how it was settled, how the people's ruling hand fell, which way the weight of the vote came down.

Danaus. The Argives decided, and not by a split vote. They decided in a way that made my old heart young again. The air bristled with right hands, the whole people at once, as they ratified this proposal into law: that we are to live in this land as settlers, free, not to be seized, and safe from any man's violence; that nobody, native or foreigner, may carry us off; and that if anyone uses force, any landholder who fails to come to our help loses his rights and is driven out of the country by the people.

That is the speech the King of the Pelasgians made on our behalf, and it persuaded them. He warned them of the great anger of Zeus, the protector of suppliants, and said the city must never, in time to come, fatten that anger. He told them that a double pollution, from strangers and from citizens both at once, appearing before the city, would be a feeding-ground for grief past all cure. When the Argive people heard that, they decided by a show of hands, without waiting for the crier's call, that so it should be. The Pelasgian people listened to the windings of his speech and were easily persuaded. But it was Zeus who brought it to its end.

Chorus.
	Come now, let us speak good prayers
	over the Argives, good returned for good.
	And may Zeus, god of strangers, look on
	this thanks from a stranger's mouth
	and see it true, and see it reach its goal with nothing lacking.
Chorus.
	Now, gods born of Zeus,
	hear me as I pour out
	prayers for our kin.
	Never may the Pelasgian land,
	this city, be gutted by fire
	at the hands of Ares, who never has enough of the war-cry,
	the lecher of battle,
	who reaps his harvest of men
	in fields other men have ploughed.
	Because they pitied us
	and cast a kind vote,
	and respect the suppliants of Zeus,
	this poor flock of ours.
Chorus.
	They did not cast their votes
	with the males and trample down
	the women's quarrel.
	They looked to Zeus,
	the avenger, the watcher
	no one can fight, whom no house
	would want to have
	settling on its roof
	and fouling it. He sits heavy.
	They honour their own blood,
	suppliants of holy Zeus.
	And so, with clean altars,
	they will please the gods.
Chorus.
	So let a prayer fly up,
	eager to honour them,
	out of mouths in the shadow of these branches.
	Never may plague
	empty this city of its men.
	Never may civil strife
	bloody the ground of the land with the bodies of its own people.
	Let the flower of its youth
	stand unplucked. Let Ares,
	who shares Aphrodite's bed and kills men,
	never cut off its bloom.
Chorus.
	And let the altars where the old men gather
	be crowded with old men
	and blaze with their offerings.
	So may the city be well governed,
	its people honouring great Zeus,
	and above all Zeus, god of strangers,
	who by his grey old law sets destiny straight.
	We pray that guardians of the land
	will always be born to take the others' place,
	and that Artemis, who is Hecate too,
	will watch over the women when they give birth.
Chorus.
	Let no man-killing slaughter
	come on this city
	to tear it apart,
	arming Ares, who has no dance and no lyre
	and is the father of tears,
	and arming the shout of civil war.
	Let the joyless swarm of diseases
	settle far from the heads of its people.
	And may Apollo the Wolf God be kind
	to all the young.
Chorus.
	May Zeus bring the earth to its full fruit,
	with the produce of every season.
	May the grazing herds in the fields
	breed and multiply.
	May they have everything from the gods.
	At the altars, let singers set
	a music of good omen,
	and out of clean mouths let the song
	that loves the lyre go up.
Chorus.
	May the people, who hold the city's power,
	guard its rights without fear,
	a government that thinks ahead and counsels for the common good.
	And to strangers, before they arm Ares,
	may they give fair terms of settlement,
	justice without grief.
Chorus.
	And the gods who hold this land forever,
	may they always honour them, in the way of their fathers,
	with the laurel branch carried high and the ox slaughtered.
	For respect for parents
	stands written third
	among the laws of Justice, who is honoured above all.

Danaus. I approve these prayers, dear children; they are sensible. But do not be frightened when you hear from your father news you did not expect, and news that is new. From this look-out, here on the shrine that shelters suppliants, I can see the ship. It is well marked, and there is no mistaking it: the trimming of the sail, the leather screens along the sides, the prow whose painted eyes look out on the road ahead, obeying the steering-oar at the stern all too well, for those who are not its friends. The men on board are plain to see, their black limbs against their white linen clothes. And the other ships, and the whole supporting fleet, are in clear view. The lead ship herself has furled her sail close under the land and is rowing in with every oar beating.

But you must look at this calmly and with your wits about you, and not forget these gods. I will go, and come back with helpers and advocates for our case. Perhaps some herald or embassy will come, wanting to take you away, to lay hands on you as if you were their property. None of that will happen. Do not be afraid of them. Still, in case we are slow to answer your cry, it is better never for a moment to forget the help you have right here. Take courage. In time, on the appointed day, the man who dishonours the gods pays the penalty.

Chorus. Father, I am afraid. The ships come on such quick wings. There is no time left in between.

Chorus.
	Fear has me all round. Terror. So it is true.
	Was all my long running any use to me at all?
	Father, I am dying of fright.

Danaus. The Argives' vote is final, children. Take heart. They will fight for you. I know it for certain.

Chorus. The lustful sons of Aegyptus are a race that should be wiped out, and they never have enough of a fight. I am telling a man who knows.

Chorus.
	In ships bolted tight with timber, dark-eyed at the prow,
	they have sailed here, and their anger has found its mark,
	with a great dark army at their backs.

Danaus. And they will find plenty of men here whose arms are filed hard by the noon heat.

Chorus. Do not leave me alone. I beg you, father. A woman alone is nothing, and there is no Ares in her.

Chorus.
	They are murderous-minded and full of tricks,
	unclean in their hearts, like ravens,
	caring nothing for altars.

Danaus. That would work out well for us, children, if the gods hated them as much as you do.

Chorus. No, father. No fear of these tridents or the holy things of the gods will ever keep their hands off us.

Chorus.
	They are too proud, mad with an unholy rage,
	shameless as dogs, and they hear
	nothing the gods say.

Danaus. But there is a saying that wolves are stronger than dogs. And the fruit of the papyrus does not beat the ear of wheat.

Chorus. They have the tempers of lewd and godless beasts. We must guard against them, and fast.

Danaus. A fleet is not quick to get itself ashore, nor quick to moor, when the cables that keep it safe have to be carried up onto the land. Even at anchor, the shepherds of ships do not feel safe straight away, especially when they have come to a coast with no harbour and the sun is going down into night. Night tends to breed worry in a careful pilot. And an army cannot be landed properly before the ship trusts her mooring. You keep your mind on this: for all your fear, do not neglect the gods. I will get help and come back. The city will not fault its messenger for being old, when his mind and his tongue are young.

(Danaus goes out toward the city.)

Chorus.
	Oh, land of hills, land we rightly worship,
	what will become of us? Where in this land of Apis can we run,
	if there is any dark hiding-hole anywhere?
	Let me turn into black smoke
	and neighbour the clouds of Zeus.
	Let me lift, without wings, and vanish altogether,
	unseen, like dust,
	and be gone.
Chorus.
	There is no escaping the evil now.
	My heart is black and it shudders.
	My father's look-out has caught me. I am going out of my mind with fear.
	I would rather find my death
	in a noose, in the knotted rope,
	before a man I loathe
	comes near this skin of mine.
	Let Hades be my master. I will die first.
Chorus.
	Where could I find a seat high in the air,
	where the wet clouds turn into snow,
	or some smooth cliff no goat can climb,
	out of everyone's sight, brooding alone,
	overhanging, a haunt of vultures,
	to witness my long fall,
	before a marriage that would tear my heart
	is forced on me?
Chorus.
	After that, I would not refuse to be
	carrion for dogs and a feast for the birds of this country.
	Death sets you free
	from evils that love a wail. Let it come,
	let death come and find me
	before the marriage bed.
	What other road of escape can I cut,
	one that frees me from marriage?
Chorus.
	Shriek it to the sky,
	songs, sad songs of prayer to the gods.
	Father, see that somehow they reach their end
	and set me free, and set me at peace.
	Look on this violence with no kindness in your eyes,
	your just eyes.
	Respect your suppliants,
	Zeus, who holds the earth and has all the power.
Chorus.
	For the race of Aegyptus, the male race,
	unbearable in their outrage,
	are running me down, a fugitive,
	with their loud, shouting lust,
	and they want to take me by force.
	But yours, and only yours, is the beam of the balance.
	Without you, what is ever brought to its end
	for mortals?

(An Egyptian ship has landed. The Herald of the sons of Aegyptus comes up from the shore with armed men behind him.)

Chorus.
	Oh, oh, oh. Ah, ah, ah.
	Here he is, the snatcher, off the ship and onto the land.
	Before that, snatcher, may you drop dead.
	I cry out, I cry out the pain.
	I see the start of it, the violence I am going to suffer.
	Eh, eh.
	Run for shelter, run.
	He is savage in his heart and he swaggers,
	unbearable on ship and on land.
	Lord of the land, stand in front of us.
Herald.
	Move, move, to the boat,
	as fast as your feet can carry you.
	Or else, or else,
	hair torn out, hair torn out, and the goad,
	and bloody, a bloody
	cutting off of heads.
	Move, move, damn you, damn you, to the boat.
Chorus.
	I wish you had drowned on the wide salt road
	with all your masters' outrage
	and your ship bolted with nails.
	You men of blood, with your boat, your boat.
Herald.
	I order you to stop your shrieking.
	Give up the madness in your heart.
	Iou, iou.
	Leave your seats. Go to the ship.
	You have no honour and no city, and I do not respect you.
Chorus.
	Never again let me see
	the water that fattens the cattle,
	where the blood that gives life to men
	grows and thrives.
	I belong to this land, I am of old, deep stock here,
	old man, of the deep-rooted stock.
Herald.
	You will go on board, on board,
	fast, whether you want to or not,
	by force, by force, and plenty of it.
	Go, before you suffer the worst of it
	and die under our hands.
Chorus.
	Aiai, aiai.
	May you die past all help,
	driven off course across the sea's salt grove,
	along the sandy mound of Sarpedon's tomb,
	wandering under the east winds.
Herald.
	Shriek and scream and call on the gods.
	You will not jump clear of the Egyptian boat.
	Shriek and shout.
	Pour out a song more bitter than grief.
Chorus.
	Oioi, oioi,
	the outrage. You bark ahead of the land
	and swagger over the sea, you crocodile.
	May the great Nile, who watches you,
	turn you back from your outrage,
	you and your unseen violence, and blot you out.

Herald. I order you to the boat that turns both ways. As fast as you can. Let no one hang back. Dragging has no respect for a head of hair.

Chorus.
	Oioi, father. The help of the holy image
	is ruining me. He is dragging me to the sea,
	step by step, like a spider.
	A dream, a black dream.
	Otototoi.
	Mother Earth, Mother Earth, turn away
	his terrible cry.
	Oh father, son of Earth, Zeus.

Herald. I am not afraid of the gods of this place. They did not rear me. They never fed me into old age.

Chorus.
	He is close, he is raging, the two-footed snake.
	Like a viper he has me,
	he has bitten my foot and holds on.
	Otototoi.
	Mother Earth, Mother Earth, turn away
	his terrible cry.
	Oh father, son of Earth, Zeus.

Herald. If none of you agrees to this and goes to the ship, the tearing will have no pity on the cloth of your dresses.

Chorus. We are lost. King, we are suffering things no god allows...

Herald. Kings? You will soon see plenty of kings, the sons of Aegyptus. Take heart. You will not be able to complain that no one is in charge.

Chorus. Io, leaders of the city, chiefs, I am being overpowered!

Herald. It looks as if I will have to grab you by the hair and drag you, since you do not listen sharply to what I say.

(The King comes in with armed men.)

King. You there. What do you think you are doing? What kind of arrogance made you dishonour this land of Pelasgian men? Do you think you have come to a city of women? For a barbarian dealing with Greeks you take far too many liberties. You have got a great deal wrong and nothing right.

Herald. And what have I done wrong here, that goes against my rights?

King. First of all, you do not know how to be a stranger.

Herald. How do I not? I found what I had lost and I am taking it.

King. Which of our citizens did you tell, whose duty it is to look after strangers?

Herald. Hermes the Searcher, greatest of all who look after strangers.

King. You name the gods and you show them no respect.

Herald. The gods I respect are the gods by the Nile.

King. And ours are nothing, from what I hear you say?

Herald. I will take these women, unless someone tears them out of my hands.

King. You will be sorry if you touch them, and not in the long run either.

Herald. I hear you. And what you say is no way to treat a guest.

King. No. I do not host men who rob the gods.

Herald. I will go and tell this to the sons of Aegyptus.

King. That is a thought my pride will not graze on.

Herald. But so that I can know it and report it more clearly, since it is a herald's duty to report every detail exactly, what am I to say? Who am I to say took this band of women, their own cousins, away from me, when I come back? Ares does not judge a case like this by calling witnesses. He does not settle the quarrel by taking silver. Before it is over, many men fall and kick their lives away.

King. Why should I tell you my name? You will learn it in time, you and your shipmates. As for these women: if they are willing, if their hearts are in it, you may take them, if a god-fearing argument persuades them. But the people of this city have passed a single decree, all of one mind, never to give up this company of women to force. The bolt is driven clean through it, to hold and stay fixed. This is not written on tablets or sealed up in the folds of a book. You hear it plainly from a tongue that speaks free. Now get out of my sight, and fast.

Herald. It seems we are about to start a new war. May victory and power go to the males.

King. Males? You will find that the men who live in this land are males too, and they do not drink beer brewed from barley.

(The Herald goes back down to the ships.)

But you, all of you, with your dear serving women, take heart and go into our well-walled city, closed in by a deep engineering of towers. There are plenty of public houses, and I am not housed with a small hand myself. There you can live in well-prepared rooms with many others, or, if it pleases you more, you may live in houses of your own. Choose the best of these, whatever pleases your hearts most. I am your protector, I and all the citizens, whose vote this is that is now taking effect. Why wait for anyone with more authority than that?

Chorus.
	Good things for good things. May you overflow with them,
	godlike son of the Pelasgians.
	And be kind, and send our father here,
	brave Danaus, who thinks ahead
	and leads our counsels. It is for him first
	to plan where we should live
	and what quarter is friendly. Everyone is quick
	to find fault with people who speak a foreign tongue.
	May the best come of it.
	With a good name, and with no angry word
	from the people of this land,
	take your places, dear slaves, just as
	Danaus allotted to each of you
	her share of service, as her dowry.

(The King goes out toward the city. Danaus comes back with a bodyguard of Argive spearmen.)

Danaus. Children, we should pray to the Argives, and sacrifice to them, and pour libations to them as if to Olympian gods, because they are our saviours, and not by a split vote. They heard from me what happened, what your cousins did to their own kin, and it made them bitter against them. And they assigned me these spearmen as an escort, so that I should have rank and honour, and should not be killed unawares by a spear from an ambush and become a burden on the land that never dies. When people receive favours like these, gratitude has to be honoured all the more, from the bottom of the heart.

And write this too, beside the many other wise rules of your father already written in your minds: an unknown company is tested by time. Everyone has an evil tongue ready for an immigrant, and it is easy to say something filthy about him. So I urge you: do not shame me, now that you have in you the ripeness that turns men's heads. Tender summer fruit is never easy to guard. Beasts spoil it, and men, why not, and creatures that fly and creatures that walk the earth. Aphrodite cries out the news of fruit that drips with ripeness, and every man who passes by, overcome by desire, shoots the eye's bewitching arrow at the soft beauty of virgins. So let us not suffer the very thing we took so much trouble to avoid, ploughing so much sea with our ship, and let us not bring shame on ourselves and pleasure to my enemies. We have two kinds of housing offered, one from Pelasgus and one from the city, to live in and pay nothing. These terms are easy. Only keep your father's instructions, and value your chastity above your life.

Chorus. For all the rest, may the Olympian gods give us good luck. As for the ripeness of my body, father, be easy. Unless the gods have planned something new, I will not turn my mind off the track it has followed.

Danaids.
	Come now, go on, and glorify
	the blessed gods who rule the city and keep it,
	and those who live around
	the ancient stream of Erasinus.
	And you, our attendants, take up the song.
	Let our praise be for this city of the Pelasgians,
	and let us no longer honour with our hymns
	the mouths of the Nile,
Danaids.
	but the rivers that pour their gentle drink
	through this land and give it many children,
	soothing its soil
	with their fat streams.
	May pure Artemis look on this company
	with pity, and may marriage never come
	by Aphrodite's compulsion.
	Let that prize go to the people I hate.
Serving Women.
	But this kindly song of ours does not neglect Aphrodite,
	for with Hera she has the power nearest to Zeus,
	and the goddess of many wiles is honoured
	for her solemn work.
	At their dear mother's side stand her partners,
	Desire, and the one who is refused nothing,
	Persuasion, who enchants.
	And Harmony has her share of Aphrodite,
	and so do the whispering paths of the Loves.
Serving Women.
	But for the fugitives I am afraid of what is blowing in:
	bitter pain and bloody wars.
	Why did they have such fair sailing,
	with the pursuit so quick behind them?
	What is fated will happen.
	The great mind of Zeus cannot be stepped over.
	It has no bounds.
	Many women before you have ended
	in marriage. May it be so for you.
A Danaid.
	May great Zeus keep marriage
	with the sons of Aegyptus away from me.
A Serving Woman.
	That would be best.
A Danaid.
	You would charm what cannot be charmed.
A Serving Woman.
	And you do not know what is to come.
A Danaid.
	How could I look into the mind of Zeus,
	a sight with no bottom to it?
A Serving Woman.
	Then pray in measured words.
A Danaid.
	What is this measure you are teaching me?
A Serving Woman.
	Not to ask too much of the gods.
Chorus.
	May Zeus the lord keep from me
	a marriage to a man I hate,
	a deadly marriage: Zeus, who set Io free
	from her pain, holding her still
	with his healing hand,
	making his force a kindness.
Chorus.
	And may he give the victory to the women.
	I praise what is better than evil,
	two parts good to one part bad,
	and I praise this: that by my prayers, justice
	should follow justice, by the means of release
	that come from a god.

(They all go out together toward the city.)

The Oresteia (458 BC)

Agamemnon

(Argos, at night, before the palace of the sons of Atreus. A Watchman lies on the flat roof, looking out towards the hills.)

Watchman. I ask the gods for release from this work, this watch that has run a whole year. All that time I have lain up here on the roof of the sons of Atreus, propped on my elbow like a dog, and I have come to know the whole gathering of the night stars, and the bright lords that bring winter and summer to men, the stars that stand out in the sky, when they set and when they rise. And now I am watching for the signal of the torch, the gleam of fire that will carry word from Troy, the news that the city has fallen. That is the order of a woman whose heart hopes like a man's and plans like a man's.

So I keep this bed of mine, a bed that wanders through the night, wet with dew, a bed no dreams come to, because fear stands beside it instead of sleep, and I cannot shut my eyes properly and sleep. And whenever I think I will sing, or hum a tune, cutting myself a dose of song as a cure for sleep, then I weep instead, groaning for the luck of this house, which is not run as well as it used to be. But tonight, let there be a lucky release from these labours. Let the fire of good news show out of the dark.

(A fire flares up on the far hills.)

Oh, welcome, lamp of the night, showing a light like day, and the setting up of many dances in Argos to give thanks for this stroke of luck. Iou, iou! I give the sign, loud and clear, to Agamemnon's wife: up from her bed, and as fast as she can, raise the cry of joy through the house to welcome this torch, if the city of Ilium is truly taken, as that beacon blazing there announces. And I will dance the opening steps myself. My masters' dice have fallen well, and I am counting it as my own throw: this beacon-watch has thrown me three sixes.

Well, may it happen that the master of the house comes home, and that I hold his dear hand in this hand of mine. The rest I keep quiet about. A great ox has stepped on my tongue. The house itself, if it could find a voice, would tell the story plainest. As for me, I choose to speak to those who know. For those who do not know, I have forgotten.

(The Watchman goes down into the house. The old men of Argos, the Chorus, come in below.)

Chorus.
	This is the tenth year since Priam's great opponent at law,
	King Menelaus, and Agamemnon with him,
	the strong yoked pair of Atreus' sons,
	honoured by Zeus with two thrones and two sceptres,
	put out from this land
	with a thousand ships full of Argives,
	an army to back their claim,
	screaming a great war-cry out of their hearts
	the way vultures do
	when, in wild grief for their young,
	they wheel high above the nest,
	rowing with the oars of their wings,
	the long labour of guarding the chicks
	lost, all of it.
	And someone on high, Apollo,
	or Pan, or Zeus, hears the shrill, sharp
	bird-cry of these lodgers in his sky
	and sends a Fury on the ones who broke the law,
	late, but she comes.
	Just so the stronger one, Zeus, god of guest and host,
	sends the sons of Atreus against Paris,
	all for a woman with many husbands,
	and he will lay many wrestling-bouts, limb-heavy,
	the knee driven into the dust,
	the spear-shaft snapped in the first rites,
	on Danaans and Trojans alike.
	It is where it is now.
	It is being brought to its appointed end.
	Not by burning under, not by pouring under,
	not by tears will a man charm away
	the stiff anger of sacrifices that will not take fire.
	But we, who cannot pay our way with old flesh,
	left behind when that army went to help,
	stay here, steering a strength like a child's
	on our walking sticks.
	For the young marrow, king inside the breast,
	is like an old man's: Ares is not in his place.
	And great old age, its leaves already drying,
	walks its three-footed roads,
	no stronger than a child,
	a dream that wanders about in daylight.
	But you, Tyndareus' daughter,
	Queen Clytemnestra,
	what is it? What is new? What have you heard,
	what message has persuaded you
	to send round the order for sacrifice?
	All the gods that hold the city,
	the high gods, the gods under the earth,
	the gods of the sky and the gods of the marketplace,
	their altars are blazing with gifts,
	and here and there and everywhere a flame
	rises high as the sky,
	drugged with the soft, honest coaxing
	of holy oil, the royal offering
	brought up from the palace's inner store.
	Tell us what you can of this,
	and what is lawful to tell,
	and be the healer of this worry,
	which now turns to dark thoughts,
	and now the hope that shines gentle from the sacrifices
	pushes back the care that never gets its fill,
	the grief that eats the heart.
Chorus.
	I have the right to sing of the sign that met the men on the road,
	the lucky sign of power, of grown men in their strength,
	for still the gods breathe into me
	persuasion, the strength of song, the age that grew up with me:
	how the twin-throned power of the Achaeans,
	the two commanders of one mind over the young men of Greece,
	was sent with spear and avenging hand
	against the Trojan land by the rushing bird,
	the king of birds to the kings of the ships,
	one black, one white behind,
	appearing near the palace on the spear-hand side,
	perched where everyone could see them,
	feeding on a hare, heavy with the young inside her,
	caught in her last run.
	Sing sorrow, sing sorrow, but let the good win out.
Chorus.
	And the army's trusted prophet, seeing the two sons of Atreus,
	two in temper as well, knew the eaters of the hare
	for the leaders who sent the army out, and read it like this:
	"In time this march takes Priam's city,
	and all the herds the people own before its towers
	Fate will plunder by force.
	Only let no jealousy from the gods darken
	the great bit forged for the mouth of Troy, the army,
	and strike it down before its time.
	For holy Artemis pities, and hates
	the winged hounds of her father
	that sacrificed the poor cowering thing
	with her young, before the birth.
	She loathes the eagles' feast."
	Sing sorrow, sing sorrow, but let the good win out.
Chorus.
	"Lovely goddess, so kind
	to the dewy cubs of savage lions,
	so delighted in the suckling young
	of every beast that roams the fields,
	grant that what these signs mean comes true,
	the omen of the birds, lucky on the right hand, but with a fault in it.
	And I call on Apollo the Healer:
	let her not raise against the Danaans
	long winds that hold the ships and will not let them sail,
	pressing for another sacrifice, one with no law, one no one may eat,
	a builder of quarrels born into the family,
	a thing that fears no husband. For there waits at home,
	terrible, rising up again, a keeper of the house full of tricks,
	a Wrath that remembers, that avenges a child."
	Such were the fates Calchas screamed out, along with great good,
	from the birds on the road, for the royal house.
	And in tune with it,
	Sing sorrow, sing sorrow, but let the good win out.
Chorus.
	Zeus, whoever he is,
	if that is the name he likes to be called,
	that is what I call him.
	I have weighed everything
	and I have nothing to set beside him,
	nothing but Zeus, if I am truly to throw
	this useless weight off my mind.
Chorus.
	Not the one who was great before,
	swollen with a daring that would fight anyone:
	he will not even be spoken of. He was, and is gone.
	And the one who came after him
	met his thrower, three falls, and is gone.
	But whoever gladly shouts the victory song for Zeus
	will hit the mark of understanding, all of it.
Chorus.
	Zeus, who set mortals on the road to thinking,
	who made it the law that stands:
	learning through suffering.
	In sleep, in front of the heart,
	the pain that remembers drips,
	and against their will men come to their senses.
	It is a violent kindness, somehow, from the gods
	who sit at the helm on their solemn bench.
Chorus.
	And so, at that time, the older leader of the Achaean ships,
	blaming no prophet,
	bending with the winds of luck that beat on him,
	when the Achaean people were weighed down
	by no sailing and empty bellies,
	holding the shore across from Chalcis
	at Aulis, where the tide roars back and forth,
Chorus.
	the winds came down from the Strymon,
	winds of bad idleness, of hunger, of bad anchorage,
	that set men wandering, that spared neither ships nor cables,
	and doubled the length of the waiting
	and wore the flower of the Argives down by rubbing.
	And when the prophet screamed out to the chiefs
	a cure for the bitter storm
	heavier still than the storm, and named Artemis as its cause,
	so that the sons of Atreus struck the ground with their sticks
	and could not hold back their tears,
Chorus.
	then the older king spoke and said:
	"It is a heavy fate not to obey,
	and heavy if I cut down my child, the glory of my house,
	staining a father's hands at the altar
	with streams of a slaughtered girl's blood.
	Which of these is without evil?
	How can I desert the fleet
	and fail my allies?
	For them to want, with passion beyond passion,
	a sacrifice to stop the winds, a virgin's blood,
	is their right. May it turn out well."
Chorus.
	When he had put on the yoke-strap of Necessity,
	a wind shifting in his mind,
	impious, unclean, unholy,
	from that moment he changed, and would think anything, dare anything.
	For men are made bold by the wretched madness
	that plans shame and is the first cause of pain.
	He steeled himself, then, to be the sacrificer of his daughter,
	to help a war fought to avenge a woman,
	a first rite for the ships.
Chorus.
	Her prayers, her cries of "Father,"
	her girl's life: the judges who loved war
	counted them as nothing.
	Her father said a prayer and told the servants
	to lift her over the altar like a goat,
	as she fell forward with all her heart into her robes,
	to lift her up bodily, and to hold her lovely mouth
	with a guard against any word
	that might curse the house,
Chorus.
	by force, by the bit, by a strength that gave her no voice.
	Her saffron dress poured onto the ground,
	and she struck each of the men who were sacrificing her
	with a shot from her eyes that begged for pity,
	standing out like a figure in a painting, wanting to speak,
	because many times, in her father's hall at the good tables,
	she had sung, and with a pure voice, untouched by any man,
	lovingly she had honoured her dear father's hymn
	at the third libation, the prayer for good luck,
Chorus.
	What came after that I did not see, and I do not tell.
	The arts of Calchas do not go unfulfilled.
	Justice tips her scales so that those who suffer learn.
	The future you will hear when it happens. Until then, let it go.
	It comes to the same as grieving ahead of time.
	It will come clear with the light of dawn.
	And for what follows, may the outcome be good,
	as she wishes it, the one who stands nearest to our lord,
	the single guard and wall of this Apian land.

(Clytemnestra comes out of the palace.)

Chorus. I have come, Clytemnestra, out of respect for your power. It is right to honour the wife of the ruling man when the man's throne stands empty. Whether you have heard something good, or heard nothing and are sacrificing on hopes of good news, I would gladly hear it. And if you keep silent, no one will blame you.

Clytemnestra. Good news, as the proverb has it: may Dawn be born from her mother Night. You will hear of a joy greater than any you hoped to hear. The Argives have taken Priam's city.

Chorus. What did you say? The word ran past me. I could not believe it.

Clytemnestra. Troy belongs to the Achaeans. Am I speaking clearly?

Chorus. Joy is creeping over me, and it is calling out my tears.

Clytemnestra. Yes. Your eye gives you away. You mean well by us.

Chorus. But what is the proof? Do you have evidence of this?

Clytemnestra. I do. Why would I not? Unless a god has cheated me.

Chorus. Is it the persuasive visions of dreams you are honouring?

Clytemnestra. I would not take the fancies of a dozing mind for fact.

Chorus. Then has some rumour without wings fattened your hopes?

Clytemnestra. You are finding fault with my wits as if I were a little girl.

Chorus. And how long ago was the city sacked?
