Clytemnestra. In the night, I tell you, that has just now given birth to this light.

Chorus. And what messenger could get here so fast?

Clytemnestra. Hephaestus, sending out a bright blaze from Ida. Beacon sent beacon here, fire relayed by courier fire. Ida to the Hermes crag on Lemnos. Third from the island, the great torch was taken up by the peak of Athos, the mountain of Zeus; and rising high, high enough to ride on the back of the sea, the strength of the travelling beacon went on joyfully, the pine-wood shining gold like a sun, and passed the blaze on to the lookouts of Macistus. And the man there did not delay, was not beaten stupidly by sleep, and did not neglect his share of the message: far off, the beacon's light came to the streams of Euripus and signalled to the watchmen on Messapion. They answered with a blaze and passed it on, setting fire to a heap of old heather. The lamp, strong, not dimmed yet, leaped the plain of Asopus like a bright moon to the crag of Cithaeron and woke another relay of the courier fire. The watch there did not refuse the far-sent light but burned more than they were told; the light shot across the Gorgon-eyed lake, and reaching Mount Aegiplanctus it urged on the ordinance of fire: no delay. They light it with unstinting force and send up a great beard of flame, so that it crossed, blazing, even the headland that looks down on the Saronic strait. Then it swooped, and came to the peak of Arachnaeus, the lookout next door to our city. And then it lands on this roof of the sons of Atreus, this light, whose grandfather is the fire on Ida.

Those were my rules for the beacon-runners, one runner taking the light from the next, like a torch-race, and the winner is the one who ran first and last. That is the proof and the sign I give you: my husband sent me word from Troy.

Chorus. Lady, I will pray to the gods again in a moment. But I would like to hear this story straight through from beginning to end and marvel at it, so tell it once more.

Clytemnestra. The Achaeans hold Troy today. I think a cry that will not mix is rising in the city. Pour vinegar and oil into the same jar and you would say they stand apart, no friends. So you can hear the voices of the taken and the takers, two separate fortunes. Some are thrown over the bodies of husbands and brothers, children over the old fathers who gave them life, and they wail for the death of their dearest from a throat that is no longer free. And the others: the night's wandering labour after battle sets them down hungry at whatever breakfast the city has, with no order of rank about who goes where, but as each man drew his lot from luck. They are already living in the captured houses of Troy, out of the frosts and dews of the open sky, and like happy men they will sleep the whole night through with no guard.

If they honour properly the gods who hold the city, the gods of the conquered land, and the seats of the gods, then the takers will not be taken in their turn. But let no lust fall on the army first, to plunder what they must not, beaten by greed. They still have to make the turn for home, run the second leg of the double course, and get safely back. And if the army comes home without any offence to the gods, the pain of the dead might still wake up, if no sudden evil strikes first. That is what you hear from me, a woman. But may the good win out, with no one in two minds about seeing it. Of many blessings, I have chosen to enjoy this one.

Chorus. Lady, you speak with good sense, like a sensible man. Now that I have heard your trustworthy proofs I am getting ready to address the gods properly. The labour has earned a thanks worth the giving.

Chorus.
	Zeus the king, and friendly Night,
	who won us great glory,
	you who threw over the towers of Troy
	a net with no gaps in it, so that no one, grown man
	or child, could leap over
	the great dragnet of slavery,
	the ruin that catches everything.
	Great Zeus, god of guest and host, I stand in awe of him,
	who did this, who kept his bow aimed at Paris
	so long, so that the arrow should not fall short
	nor fly past the stars
	for nothing.
Chorus.
	"The stroke of Zeus": they can say it.
	This much you can track down:
	he did as he decided. Someone said
	the gods do not bother to care about men
	who trample the grace of untouchable things.
	That man was impious.
	It is shown now, what comes of daring what must not be dared,
	when men breathe bigger than is just,
	and their houses swell past all reason,
	past the best measure. Let there be enough,
	and no harm in it, so that a man
	with good sense can be satisfied.
	For there is no wall in wealth
	to protect a man who, glutted,
	has kicked the great altar of Justice
	out of sight.
Chorus.
	Wretched Persuasion forces him on,
	the unbearable child of Ruin, who plans ahead.
	Every remedy is useless. It is not hidden;
	the damage shows, a light with a terrible shine.
	Like bad bronze rubbed and knocked,
	he shows black when he is put to the test,
	since he is a child chasing a bird on the wing,
	and he has put a stain on his city no one can bear.
	No god hears his prayers,
	and the man who keeps company with these things,
	the unjust man, they pull down.
	Such was Paris, who came
	to the house of the sons of Atreus
	and shamed the table where he was a guest
	by stealing a wife.
Chorus.
	She left to her own people the clash of shields,
	the churning of spears, the arming of ships,
	and carrying to Ilium destruction for a dowry,
	she went lightly through the gates,
	daring what should not be dared. And the prophets of the house
	groaned loud and said:
	"Oh, oh, the house, the house and its princes!
	Oh, the bed and the tracks where she lay and loved her husband!"
	You can see him: silent, dishonoured, not reproaching,
	sitting apart in bitter grief.
	In his longing for the woman over the sea
	a ghost will seem to rule the house.
	The grace of beautiful statues
	is hateful to the husband;
	in the emptiness of their eyes
	all Aphrodite is gone.
Chorus.
	Grieving shapes come in dreams
	and bring a joy that is useless,
	useless, for when a man thinks he sees good things,
	the vision slips through his hands and is gone,
	never to come back, on wings, down the paths of sleep.
	These are the griefs at home, at the hearth,
	and there are griefs that go far beyond them:
	in every house of all who set out together
	from the land of Greece
	a grief no heart can stand shows plain.
	Many things touch the liver.
	A man knows whom he sent away;
	but instead of men,
	urns and ashes
	come home to each man's house.
Chorus.
	Ares, who trades bodies for gold
	and holds the scales in the battle of spears,
	sends back from Ilium to the families
	a heavy dust out of the fire, wept over with hard tears,
	filling the neat urns with ashes
	in exchange for men.
	They groan and praise him: this one,
	how well he knew battle; that one,
	fell well in the killing,
	for another man's wife.
	Somebody snarls that under his breath,
	and grief with resentment in it creeps against
	the sons of Atreus, who brought the case.
	And others, there, around the wall,
	fine-looking men, hold graves in the earth of Ilium;
	the enemy's soil has covered the men who took it.
Chorus.
	Heavy is the talk of citizens with anger in it.
	It pays the debt of a curse the people have voted.
	My worry waits to hear
	something hidden under the night.
	The gods are not blind to men who kill many.
	The black Furies, in time,
	take the man who is lucky without justice
	and wear him down, turn his luck, and dim him,
	and once he is among the unseen there is no help.
	To be praised beyond measure is heavy;
	the thunderbolt is thrown from the eyes of Zeus.
	I choose a prosperity nobody envies.
	Let me not be a sacker of cities,
	nor let me be taken myself and see
	my life in another man's hands.
Chorus.
	From the beacon of good news
	the quick report runs through the city.
	But is it true? Who knows?
	Or is it some lie from a god?
	Who is so childish, so struck out of his wits,
	that he lets his heart catch fire
	at fresh news from a flame,
	and then, when the story changes, breaks down?
	It fits a woman's command
	to give thanks for a thing before it appears.
	Too ready to believe, a woman's word
	spreads fast over its borders;
	but it dies fast too,
	the glory a woman proclaims.

Chorus. We will soon know about these light-bringing torches and beacon watches and fires relayed one to the next, whether they are true, or whether this pleasant light came like a dream and tricked our minds. I see a herald coming up from the shore, shaded with olive branches. And the dust, mud's thirsty sister, right beside him, tells me this: he will not be dumb, and he will not signal to you by lighting a fire of mountain wood and sending up smoke. He will either come out and say "rejoice," and more than the beacon said, or the opposite of that, and I refuse to say it. Let good be added to the good that has already shown itself. Whoever prays otherwise for this city, let him reap the harvest of his own mind's mistake himself.

(The Herald comes in from the shore, with olive branches in his hair.)

Herald. Oh, soil of my fathers, land of Argos! In the light of the tenth year I have come back to you. Many hopes broke; I have caught hold of one. I never expected to die here in the land of Argos and have my share of the grave I love best. Now greetings, land, and greetings, light of the sun, and Zeus, highest lord of the country, and the Pythian lord, no longer shooting his arrows at us. You were hostile enough to us beside the Scamander. Now be our saviour again, and our healer, lord Apollo. All the gods of the gathering-place, I address them, and my own patron, Hermes, dear herald, whom heralds revere; and the heroes who sent us out: take back kindly what the spear has left of the army.

Oh, halls of kings, dear roofs, solemn seats, and you gods who face the sun: if ever before, then now, after so long, receive the king with honour, with shining eyes. For he has come bringing light in the darkness to you and to all these people here, King Agamemnon. Give him a good welcome; it is right, for he has dug Troy up with the mattock of Zeus who brings justice, and the ground has been worked over with it. The altars are gone and the seats of the gods, and the seed of the whole land is dying out. Such a yoke he threw round the neck of Troy, the king, the elder son of Atreus, a man the gods have blessed, and he has come home, the most worthy of honour of all men living. For Paris and the city that paid with him cannot boast that what they did was greater than what they suffered. Convicted of rape and of theft, he lost his plunder and mowed his father's house down to the ground, land and all, in total destruction. The sons of Priam have paid double for their crimes.

Chorus. Herald of the Achaeans, from the army, greetings and joy.

Herald. Joy, yes. I will no longer argue with the gods if they want me dead.

Chorus. Was it longing for this land of your fathers that wore you down?

Herald. So much that my eyes are filling with tears of joy.

Chorus. Then it was a sweet sickness you caught.

Herald. How so? Teach me, and I will master what you mean.

Chorus. You were struck with longing for people who longed for you back.

Herald. You mean this land missed the army that missed it?

Chorus. So much that I often groaned from a darkened heart.

Herald. Where did this gloom come from, this hatred weighing on your spirit?

Chorus. For a long time now I have kept silence as my medicine against harm.

Herald. How? With the princes away, were there people you feared?

Chorus. So much that now, in your own words, even death would be a great kindness.

Herald. Yes, because it has ended well. Over such a long time, some of it you could say went smoothly, and some of it was open to blame. Who but the gods goes without pain the whole length of a lifetime? If I told you the hardships, the bad quarters, the narrow gangways, the wretched bedding, what did we not groan over? What did we not get as our share of the day? And then on land there was worse still to hate. Our beds were under the enemy's walls, and out of the sky and up from the meadows the dews drizzled down on us, a steady ruin to our clothes, making our hair a nest for lice. And if someone told of the winter that killed the birds, the unbearable snow Ida sent down, or the heat, when the sea at noon fell asleep on a bed with no wave and no wind, why grieve over that? The trouble is past. It is past, and for the dead so past that they will never care even to get up again.

Why count the spent men on the tally? Why should the living feel pain at bad luck long gone? I say a long goodbye to our misfortunes. For us, what is left of the Argive army, the gain wins, and the loss does not tip the scale against it. So it is right that we boast to this light of the sun, as we fly over sea and land: "The Argive expedition took Troy at last and nailed up these spoils on the houses of the gods throughout Greece, a glory for all time." When men hear such things they must praise the city and its generals; and the grace of Zeus that brought this about will get its honour. You have the whole story.

Chorus. Your words have won and I do not deny it. The old are always young enough to learn properly. But this news most properly concerns the house and Clytemnestra, and it makes me rich along with them.

(Clytemnestra comes out of the palace again.)

Clytemnestra. I raised my cry of joy long ago, when the first night messenger of fire came, telling of the capture and the overthrow of Ilium. And somebody sneered at me and said: "On the strength of beacons you believe Troy is sacked now? How like a woman, to let her heart lift." Talk like that made me look as if I had lost my way. Still, I went on sacrificing, and in the woman's way, here and there all over the city, they raised the cry of joy in the seats of the gods, lulling the sweet-smelling flame that eats the offerings.

And now, why do you need to tell me more? I shall hear the whole story from the king himself. And I should hurry to receive my honoured husband as well as I can when he comes home. For what light is sweeter for a woman to see than this: to open the gates for her husband when the god has brought him safe home from the war? Take this message to my husband: come as fast as he can, the darling of the city; and when he comes, may he find in his house a wife as faithful as the one he left, a watchdog of the house, good to him, an enemy to those who wish him ill, and the same in everything else, having broken no seal in all this length of time. Of pleasure from another man, or of any talk of scandal, I know no more than I know the dipping of bronze.

(Clytemnestra goes back into the palace.)

Herald. A boast like that, when it is loaded with truth, is no shame for a noble woman to shout out.

Chorus. That is how she spoke to you, and you have taken it in: a fine speech, for those who read it clearly. But tell me, herald. I am asking about Menelaus. Has he come back safe with you, the beloved power of this land?

Herald. There is no way I could tell a lie so beautiful that the people I love would enjoy it for long.

Chorus. Then how could you manage to say something both good and true? When those two split apart, it is hard to hide.

Herald. The man has vanished from the Achaean fleet, himself and his ship. I am not lying.

Chorus. Did he put out from Ilium in plain sight, or did a storm, a burden shared by all, snatch him away from the fleet?

Herald. You have hit the mark like a champion archer. A long pain, and you have said it in a word.

Chorus. Alive or dead? What did the other sailors say about him?

Herald. Nobody knows enough to report it clearly, except the Sun, who feeds everything that grows on the earth.

Chorus. Then how do you say the storm came on the fleet, by the anger of the gods, and how did it end?

Herald. It is not right to stain a day of good words with a tongue that brings bad news. The honour of the gods is a separate thing. When a messenger with a grim face brings a city the disasters it prayed against, the army fallen, one wound for the whole city, the public wound, and many men cursed out of many houses by the double whip that Ares loves, a ruin with two spears, a bloody pair in one harness: when he is loaded with that kind of suffering, that is the time to sing this hymn of the Furies. But when he comes as the bearer of good news, of things saved, to a city rejoicing in its good fortune, how am I to mix the good with the bad, telling of a storm that fell on the Achaeans, and not without the gods' anger?

For fire and sea, the bitterest of enemies before, swore an oath together and showed their good faith by destroying the wretched Argive army. In the night the evil rose, and the waves ran badly. The Thracian winds smashed the ships against each other; rammed by force, horn against horn, in the hurricane and the driving rain, they went off and vanished, spun round by an evil shepherd. When the bright light of the sun came up, we saw the Aegean sea blooming with the corpses of Achaean men and the wreckage of ships. As for us and our ship, its hull unharmed, somebody stole us out of it, or begged us off, some god, not a man, with his hand on the tiller. Luck the saviour sat on our ship and was willing, so that we neither took the surge of the waves at anchor nor ran aground on rocky land. Then, having escaped a Hades of the sea, in the white daylight, not trusting our luck, we grazed our thoughts on the new suffering: the army worn out and badly battered.

And now, if any of them is still breathing, they speak of us as lost, and why would they not? We think the same of them. May it turn out for the best. As for Menelaus, first and most of all, expect him to come. If some ray of the sun finds him alive and seeing, by the devices of Zeus, who does not yet want to wipe out the family, there is some hope he will come home again. So much you have heard; and know that what you have heard is the truth.

Chorus.
	Who was it that gave her that name,
	a name so completely, so exactly true?
	Was it someone we cannot see,
	who knows ahead of time what is fated,
	and steered a tongue to hit the mark by luck?
	Helen. The bride the spear fought for,
	the woman two armies quarrelled over.
	And the name fits her. She was hell to ships,
	hell to men, hell to the city.
	Out from behind her delicate, costly curtains
	she sailed on the breath of a giant west wind,
	and after her came the hunters,
	a great crowd of men carrying shields,
	following the vanished track of her oars
	to the place where she had run her boat ashore
	on the leafy banks of the Simois,
	to a quarrel that would end in blood.
Chorus.
	And to Troy that marriage brought
	a mourning that deserved its name,
	driven home by Wrath, who finishes what she starts.
	In later time she collected the debt
	for the dishonoured table,
	for Zeus who shares the hearth,
	from the men who raised their voices
	loud in honour of the bride,
	the wedding song it fell to the bridegroom's kin
	to sing that day.
	Priam's old city has learned a new song since.
	Now, I think, she groans out loud,
	a long lament, and calls Paris
	the man whose marriage was a curse.
	She has borne a life of total ruin,
	a life of lament,
	for the pitiful blood of her people.
Chorus.
	So a man once raised a lion cub in his house,
	taken from the milk it wanted,
	still hungry for the breast.
	In the first days of its life it was gentle,
	loving with the children,
	a delight to the old.
	It was held in their arms, often,
	like a newborn baby,
	bright-eyed, turning to the hand that fed it,
	fawning, because its belly made it fawn.
Chorus.
	But time went on, and it showed the nature
	it had from its parents.
	It paid back the ones who raised it:
	unasked, it made itself a feast,
	a ruin of slaughtered sheep,
	and the house was fouled with blood,
	a grief the household could not fight,
	a great slaughter, many killed.
	A priest of Ruin, sent by a god,
	and the house itself had raised it.
Chorus.
	And so, I would say, there came to the city of Troy
	what looked like a spirit of windless calm,
	a soft ornament of wealth,
	a gentle arrow from the eyes,
	the flower of love that bites the heart.
	Then she swerved aside
	and brought the marriage to a bitter end,
	a bad neighbour, bad company,
	rushing in on the sons of Priam
	with Zeus, god of guest and host, as her escort:
	a Fury who made the brides weep.
Chorus.
	There is an old saying among men,
	spoken long ago and handed down:
	that a man's good fortune, once it is full grown,
	has children and does not die childless,
	and that out of good luck
	a family sprouts a misery that is never satisfied.
	But I stand apart from the others. I think alone.
	It is the godless deed that breeds more after it,
	and the children look like their parents.
	A house that keeps to straight justice
	has fine children always. That is its fate.
Chorus.
	Old Outrage loves to breed
	a young Outrage in bad men,
	sooner or later, when the appointed day of birth comes,
	and with it a spirit no one can fight or make war on,
	unholy Recklessness,
	and black Ruins for the house,
	the image of their parents.
Chorus.
	But Justice shines in smoky houses,
	and honours the life that keeps to the line.
	The gold-plated halls where hands are dirty
	she leaves, with her eyes turned away,
	and goes to what is clean.
	She does not worship the power of wealth
	stamped with a false mark by men's praise,
	and she steers everything to its end.

(Agamemnon comes in on a chariot, with a large escort of soldiers. Cassandra rides in the chariot behind him, silent.)

Chorus.
	Come then, my King, sacker of Troy,
	son of Atreus,
	how am I to greet you? How am I to honour you
	without overshooting, without falling short
	of the right measure of thanks?
	Many men put the look of a thing before the truth
	and step over the line of justice.
	Everyone is ready to groan beside the man who is down,
	but no bite of grief ever reaches his liver.
	And they force their unsmiling faces into smiles
	and pretend to share in other people's joy.
	But a good shepherd knows his flock,
	and a man's eyes cannot fool him
	when they fawn on him with watery friendship
	and pretend it comes from a loyal mind.
	I will not hide it from you: back then,
	when you sent the army out for Helen's sake,
	you were painted in my eyes in very ugly colours,
	a man not steering the helm of his mind well,
	buying courage for dying men
	with sacrifices.
	But now, not from the surface of my heart, and not without love,
	I say it: the work was worth it to the men who finished it well.
	And in time, by asking, you will learn
	which of your citizens kept the city justly
	while you were gone, and which did not.

Agamemnon. Argos first, and the gods of this land. It is right that I greet them first. They helped me home, and they helped me take the justice I took from Priam's city. For the gods did not listen to pleas from anyone's tongue. They cast their votes, with no divided opinion, into the urn of blood: death for the men of Troy, and destruction for the city. To the other urn Hope came near, but no hand filled it. The city is taken, and even now you can tell it by the smoke. The storms of Ruin are still alive there. The ash is dying with the city, and as it dies it breathes out rich fumes of wealth.

For this we owe the gods a gratitude that never forgets, since we threw the net of our anger around the city, and for the sake of one woman the Argive beast ground the city into dust: the horse's brood, the people carrying shields, that made its leap as the Pleiades were setting. It vaulted the tower, a lion that eats raw meat, and lapped its fill of royal blood.

That is my opening, to the gods, and I have drawn it out at length. As for what you feel, I heard it, and I remember it. I say the same, and you have an advocate in me. Few men are born with this: to honour a friend's good fortune without envy. The poison of ill will sits on the heart and doubles the load of the man who has caught the disease. He is weighed down by his own troubles, and he groans to see happiness next door. I can speak from knowledge, for I know the mirror of companionship well: the men who seemed so very devoted to me were the ghost of a shadow. Only Odysseus, the very man who did not want to sail, once he was yoked, pulled beside me willingly in harness. I say this of him whether he is dead or alive.

For the rest, matters of the city and the gods: we will call a full assembly, put them to open debate, and take counsel. Where things are going well, we must see how they may stay well for a long time. And where some healing drug is needed, we will burn or cut, with kind intentions, and try to turn away the harm of the disease.

Now I will go into my halls, to my house and its hearth, and first I will give the gods my right hand in greeting. They sent me out and they have brought me back. And since Victory has followed me, may she stay with me, and hold firm.

(Clytemnestra comes out of the palace. Behind her are slave women carrying purple cloths.)

Clytemnestra. Men of the city, elders of Argos here before me, I will not be ashamed to tell you how I love my husband. In time, shyness dies away in people. I have not learned this from others: I will tell you my own hard life, all the years this man was under the walls of Troy.

First, for a woman to sit at home alone, apart from her man, is a monstrous evil. She hears rumour after spiteful rumour. One messenger comes, and then another brings a worse blow than the last, shouting it to the house. If this man had taken as many wounds as the reports that ran home like water through a channel, he would have more holes in him than a net. If he had died as often as the stories said, he would be a second Geryon, with three bodies, and could boast of taking on a triple cloak of earth, a great deal of it above him, for of the earth below him I say nothing, dying once for each of his shapes. Because of spiteful rumours like these, other people had to loosen from my neck, more than once, the noose I had hung from above and was caught in, by force.

That is why our boy is not standing here beside me as he should, Orestes, who holds the pledges of my faith and yours. Do not be surprised at it. He is being raised by a good ally, our friend by the spear, Strophius of Phocis, who warned me of trouble from two sides: your danger under Troy, and the chance that the people, in a roar of lawlessness, might throw down the council, since it is in men's nature to kick a man harder once he is down. An excuse like that carries no trickery.

Clytemnestra. As for me, the rushing springs of my tears have run dry. There is not a drop left. My eyes are sore from late nights, weeping over the beacon-lights set for you that were never attended to. In my dreams I would be woken by the thin whine of a gnat, and I had seen more suffering happen to you than the time I slept could hold.

Now I have borne all that, and with a heart free of grief I can call this man the watchdog of the fold, the forestay that saves the ship, the pillar footed firm under a high roof, a father's only son, land appearing to sailors past all hope, the loveliest daybreak after a storm, a spring of running water to a thirsty traveller. It is sweet to escape every kind of necessity. Those are the greetings I think he deserves. And let envy stay far away. We have put up with many evils before now.

And now, my dear one, step down from this chariot. But do not set your foot on the bare ground, my lord, the foot that trampled Troy. Women, what are you waiting for? You were given the task of spreading cloth over the ground where he walks. Let his path be purple at once, so that Justice may lead him into a home he never hoped to see. The rest, a mind that sleep does not overcome will set in order justly, with the gods' help, as it is destined.

Agamemnon. Daughter of Leda, guardian of my house, your speech matched my absence: you drew it out at length. But fitting praise is a prize that should come from other people. And do not pamper me like a woman, and do not gape at me with grovelling shouts from the ground as if I were some barbarian, and do not make my path a thing for envy by spreading cloth on it. That is how we honour the gods. For a mortal to walk on embroidered beauty is never, to my mind, a thing without fear. I tell you to honour me as a man, not a god. Footcloths and embroideries: Rumour shouts the difference between them. Not to think badly is the gods' greatest gift. Call a man blessed only when he has ended his life in the prosperity he loves. If I can act like this in everything, I have good confidence.

Clytemnestra. Come now, tell me this, and do not go against what you really think.

Agamemnon. What I think, be sure of it, I will not corrupt.

Clytemnestra. If you had been afraid, would you have vowed to the gods to do this?

Agamemnon. Yes, if any man ever would. I would have declared it as my vow, knowing well what I said.

Clytemnestra. And what do you think Priam would have done, if he had won what you have won?

Agamemnon. He would have walked on the embroidered cloth. I am quite sure of it.

Clytemnestra. Then do not be ashamed of what people will say.

Agamemnon. And yet the voice of the people has great power.

Clytemnestra. Yes, but the man nobody envies is a man nobody admires.

Agamemnon. It is not a woman's part to be hungry for a fight.

Clytemnestra. But for the fortunate, it is fitting even to be beaten.

Agamemnon. And you, do you value winning this quarrel so much?

Clytemnestra. Give in. Yield the victory to me, but of your own free will.

Agamemnon. Well, if that is what you want, let someone quickly unlace my boots, the slaves that serve my feet as I walk. And as I walk on these purple cloths of the gods, may no envious eye strike me from far off. I feel a great shame at ruining the house with my feet, spoiling wealth, spoiling weaving bought with silver. So much for that. This foreign girl, take her into the house kindly. God looks with favour from far away on a master who is gentle in his power. Nobody puts on the yoke of slavery by choice. She is the pick of the flower of many treasures, the army's gift to me, and she came with me. Now, since I have been brought round to obey you in this, I will go into the halls of my house, walking on purple.

Clytemnestra. There is the sea, and who will drain it dry? It breeds an ever-fresh ooze of purple, worth its weight in silver, to dye our clothes. And the house has enough of this, my lord, thanks to the gods. This house does not know how to be poor. I would have vowed the trampling of many cloths, if the oracle had ordered it for this house, when I was working out a ransom for this life. While the root lives, the leaves come back to the house and spread their shade against the dog star. So now that you have come to the hearth of your home, you are a sign that warmth has come in winter. And when Zeus makes wine from the bitter unripe grape, then already there is coolness in the house, when the master, the perfect man, walks his own halls again.

(Agamemnon walks over the purple cloth into the house.)

Zeus, Zeus who brings things to their end, bring my prayers to their end. And take care of what you mean to bring to an end.

(She goes into the house after him.)

Chorus.
	Why does this terror keep hovering,
	standing guard before my heart,
	my heart that reads the signs?
	Why does my song play the prophet,
	unasked, unpaid?
	Why can I not spit it out
	like a dream too hard to read,
	and let a confidence I can trust
	sit on the throne of my mind?
	Time has heaped the sand of the beach
	over the stern-cables thrown ashore
	when the army took ship and set out for Troy.
Chorus.
	I have seen their homecoming with my own eyes.
	I am my own witness.
	But still my heart, self-taught, inside me,
	sings the dirge of the Fury, with no lyre,
	and cannot hold the whole of hope's welcome courage.
	My insides are not talking nonsense.
	My heart is spinning in whirlpools
	that come to their end,
	against a mind that has good reason to fear.
	I pray that what I expect
	falls out a lie
	and never comes to its end.
Chorus.
	Truly, great health does not know when to stop.
	Disease is its neighbour,
	leaning on the shared wall.
	And a man's fortune, sailing straight,
	strikes on a hidden reef.
	But if fear throws overboard a part of the cargo,
	the goods he owns,
	with a well-measured swing of the sling,
	the whole house does not go down,
	overloaded with grief,
	and the hull is not swamped.
	And truly Zeus gives with both hands,
	and the yearly furrows
	kill the disease of hunger.
Chorus.
	But a man's black blood, once it has fallen
	to the ground in front of him, and he is dead,
	who can sing it back by any charm?
	The one man who knew the right way
	to bring back the dead:
	did Zeus not stop him, as a warning?
	If one fate set by the gods
	did not hold back another fate
	from getting more than its share,
	my heart would have run ahead of my tongue
	and poured all this out.
	As it is, it mutters in the dark,
	sore, and with no hope
	of ever unwinding anything in time,
	while my mind is on fire.

(Clytemnestra comes out of the palace again.)

Clytemnestra. Go inside, you too. I mean you, Cassandra. Zeus, without anger, has made you a sharer in the holy water of this house, standing with many other slaves beside the altar of the god who guards its wealth. Get down from this chariot, and do not be too proud. Even Alcmene's son, they say, was once sold, and put up with eating the bread of a slave. And if necessity tips the scale this way, there is much to be thankful for in masters whose wealth is old. Those who reap a rich harvest they never hoped for are cruel to their slaves in everything, and beyond the measure. From us you get what custom allows.

Chorus. She has finished speaking to you, and plainly. You are caught inside the nets of fate. Perhaps you will obey, if you are going to obey. Perhaps you will not.

Clytemnestra. Well, unless she twitters in some unknown barbarian tongue, like a swallow, my words are reaching inside her mind, and I am winning her round.

Chorus. Go with her. What she says is the best of what is left to you. Do as she says. Leave your seat in the chariot.

Clytemnestra. I have no time to waste out here at the door with this woman. The sheep are already standing at the central hearth for the slaughter, for a joy we never hoped we would have. As for you, if you are going to do any of this, do not waste time. But if you do not understand and are not taking in what I say, then instead of your voice, make a sign with your barbarian hand.

Chorus. The stranger seems to need an interpreter, a clear one. She is like a wild animal, just caught.

Clytemnestra. She is mad. She listens to her own sick mind. She has come here leaving a city just taken, and she does not know how to carry the bit until she has foamed her rage away in blood. I will not throw more words at her and be insulted.

(Clytemnestra goes back into the house.)

Chorus. I will not be angry with her. I pity her. Come, poor woman, leave the chariot empty. Give in to this necessity, and put on the new yoke.

Cassandra.
	Otototoi, popoi, da!
	Apollo! Apollo!

Chorus. Why are you crying otototoi over Apollo? He is not the kind of god who has anything to do with mourning.

Cassandra.
	Otototoi, popoi, da!
	Apollo! Apollo!

Chorus. Again she calls on the god with ill-omened cries, a god who has no business standing by at a lament.

Cassandra.
	Apollo, Apollo,
	god of the roads, my destroyer. Your name says it.
	You have destroyed me, and it cost you nothing, this second time.

Chorus. She is going to prophesy, it seems, about her own troubles. The god's gift stays in her mind, slave though she is.

Cassandra.
	Apollo, Apollo,
	god of the roads, my destroyer.
	Ah, where have you brought me? To what kind of house?

Chorus. To the house of the sons of Atreus. If you do not understand that, I am telling you. And you will not say it is a lie.

Cassandra.
	No. To a house that hates the gods,
	a house that has seen too much: kin killing kin,
	heads cut off,
	a slaughterhouse for men, a floor spattered with blood.

Chorus. The stranger seems to have a nose like a hound's. She is on the scent, and she will find the blood.

Cassandra.
	Here is the evidence I trust:
	those babies, crying over their own slaughter,
	their roasted flesh eaten by their father.

Chorus. We had heard of your fame as a prophet. But we are not looking for any prophets here.

Cassandra.
	Oh, popoi, what is she plotting?
	What is this new grief? Great, great
	is the evil she is plotting in this house,
	unbearable to the people who are its own, past any cure.
	And help stands far away.

Chorus. These prophecies I know nothing about. But those others I understood. The whole city is shouting them.

Cassandra.
	Oh, wretched woman, you will do it, then?
	The husband who shares your bed:
	you wash him bright in the bath, and then, how do I say the end?
	It will be soon. Hand after hand
	she stretches out, reaching.

Chorus. I still do not follow. Now, after riddles, I am helpless before oracles with a film over their eyes.

Cassandra.
	E, e! Papai, papai! What is this that shows itself?
	Is it some net of Hades?
	No. The net is the woman who shares his bed,
	who shares the guilt of murder.
	Let the pack that never has enough of this family
	scream in triumph over a sacrifice
	that should be answered with stoning.
Chorus.
	What Fury is this you are calling
	to raise its cry over this house?
	Your words do not brighten me.
	The blood runs back to my heart, yellow as saffron,
	the way it does for a man struck down,
	when it ends together with the last rays of his life.
	And Ruin comes fast.
Cassandra.
	Ah, ah, look, look! Keep the bull
	away from the cow! She has caught him in the robe,
	her trick with the black horn,
	and she strikes. He falls in the vessel full of water.
	It is a cauldron of treacherous murder I am telling you of.
Chorus.
	I would not boast that I am a fine judge of oracles,
	but I make this out to be some evil.
	From oracles what good word
	ever comes to men?
	Through evil, the wordy arts of prophets
	bring men to learn fear.
Cassandra.
	Oh, oh, the bad luck of my wretched fate!
	It is my own suffering I cry out now, poured on top of the rest.
	Why have you brought me here, poor thing that I am?
	For nothing, except to die with him. What else?
Chorus.
	Your mind is out of itself, some god is carrying you,
	and you cry over yourself
	a song that is no song, like that brown bird
	who never has enough of crying, ah, in her wretched heart,
	moaning Itys, Itys, a life
	that flowers on both sides with grief,
	the nightingale.
Cassandra.
	Oh, oh, the fate of the clear-voiced nightingale!
	The gods wrapped her in a feathered body
	and gave her a sweet life, without tears.
	What waits for me is the split of a two-edged blade.
Chorus.
	Where do they come from, these rushing, god-driven pains
	that mean nothing?
	Why do you beat out the terrible things in a song
	of ugly screams and high straining notes?
	Where did you learn the landmarks on this road of prophecy,
	a road of evil words?
Cassandra.
	Oh, the marriage, the marriage of Paris, death to the ones he loved.
	Oh, Scamander, the river my fathers drank from.
	Once, poor wretch, I was raised beside your banks,
	and grew up there.
	Now beside Cocytus and the banks of Acheron
	I think I will soon be singing my prophecies.
Chorus.
	What is this word you have said, far too clear?
	A newborn child could hear it and understand.
	I am struck through, as by a bloody fang,
	by your bitter fate, as you cry your small sad cries,
	things that break me to hear.
Cassandra.
	Oh, the pains, the pains of my city, destroyed to the last.
	Oh, my father's sacrifices before the walls,
	the herds killed, the cattle from the pasture,
	and they were no cure at all,
	they could not stop the city suffering what it has.
	And I, my mind on fire, will soon be thrown to the ground.
Chorus.
	These words follow the ones before.
	Some god who means you harm
	falls on you with all his weight
	and makes you sing of sufferings, groaning, full of death.
	And the end of it I cannot find.

Cassandra. Now my prophecy will no longer look out at you from behind a veil, like a bride on her wedding morning. It is going to come clear and rushing, like a wind that blows into the sunrise, so that a grief far bigger than mine breaks like a wave against the light. I will not teach you in riddles any more. And be my witnesses. Run beside me while I follow the scent of crimes done long ago. There is a choir that never leaves this roof. It sings in unison, and the sound is ugly, because what it sings is not good. And it has been drinking, this gang of revellers that stays in the house and cannot be got out of the door: it has drunk human blood to make itself bolder, this troop of Furies born with the family. They sit in the house and sing their song, the song of the first ruin that began it all, and each in turn they spit in disgust at a brother's bed and hate the man who trampled it. Did I miss? Or did I hit something, like an archer? Or am I a lying prophet, a babbler who knocks on doors? Swear it first, and then be my witness: I know the story of this house's old sins.

Chorus. And how could an oath, however honestly the pledge was made, cure anything? But I am amazed at you. You were raised across the sea, and you speak of a city that talks another language as if you had been standing right there.

Cassandra. The prophet Apollo set me to this work.

Chorus. Surely not a god, struck with desire?

Cassandra. Before now I was ashamed to talk about this.

Chorus. Yes. Everyone is more delicate when things are going well.

Cassandra. But he was a wrestler, and he breathed his favour on me hard.

Chorus. And did the two of you come to the making of children, in the usual way?

Cassandra. I gave Apollo my consent, and then I cheated him.

Chorus. Were you already taken by the god's art by then?

Cassandra. Already I was prophesying to my people every suffering that was coming.

Chorus. Then how did Apollo's anger leave you unharmed?

Cassandra. After that fault I could persuade no one of anything.

Chorus. To us, at least, what you prophesy seems worth believing.

Cassandra. Iou, iou! Oh, oh, the pain! Again the terrible labour of true prophecy spins me and shakes me with its opening notes, and the opening is dreadful. Do you see them, these children sitting by the house, young, like the shapes in dreams? Children killed, it seems, by the people who should have loved them, their hands full of meat, their own flesh for food, holding up their guts and innards, a load that would make you weep, plain to see. And their father tasted them. For that, I tell you, someone is planning to make them pay: a lion with no courage, rolling in the bed, keeping the house, oh, against my master when he comes home. My master, yes. I have to wear the yoke of slavery now. And the commander of the ships, the man who turned Troy upside down, does not know what that hateful bitch's tongue is going to bring him, by an evil stroke of luck. She licked his hand. She talked, and stretched her ears out in gladness, like a Ruin that works in secret. That is her daring: the female is the killer of the male. What hateful monster can I call her and hit the mark? A two-headed snake? Some Scylla living in the rocks, the wreck of sailors? A raging mother out of Hades, breathing a war without truce against her own family? And how she shouted for joy, that woman who dares anything, as if the battle had turned, while she pretended to be glad he had come home safe. Whether I convince you of any of this or not makes no difference. Why should it? What is coming will come. And soon you will stand here and see it, and pity me, and say I was a prophet who told too much truth.

Chorus. Thyestes' feast on his children's flesh: that I understood, and I shudder, and fear has hold of me, hearing it told true, with nothing made up to look like truth. But the rest of it I heard and fell off the track, and now I am running blind.

Cassandra. I say you will see Agamemnon dead.

Chorus. Poor girl, put your mouth to sleep. Say something lucky.

Cassandra. There is no healing god standing over these words.

Chorus. No, not if it is really going to happen. But let it not, somehow.

Cassandra. You pray. Their business is killing.

Chorus. What man is getting this pollution ready?

Cassandra. Then you have wandered a very long way off the track of my prophecies.

Chorus. I do not understand how the one who does it will do it.

Cassandra. And yet I know Greek. I know it far too well.

Chorus. So do the oracles from Delphi, and they are still hard to understand.

Cassandra. Papai! What fire! It is coming over me. Otototoi! Apollo, Wolf-god, oh, me, me! This two-footed lioness, who sleeps with a wolf while the noble lion is away, is going to kill me, poor wretch that I am. She is brewing a drug, and she swears she will mix my wages into her anger too. She is sharpening the sword for the man, to pay back murder for bringing me here. So why do I still wear these things that make a joke of me, the staff and the prophet's wreaths around my neck? You, at least, I will destroy before my own hour comes.

(She breaks the staff and throws it down with the wreaths and the rest of her prophet's dress, and tramples them.)

Go to ruin! There. You are on the ground, and that is how I pay you back. Make some other woman rich in ruin instead of me. Look, Apollo himself is stripping me of the prophet's dress. He watched me in this finery being laughed at, laughed at hard, by friends who were enemies, all of them of one mind, and all for nothing. They called me a wandering beggar-woman, a fortune-teller at the door, a starving wretch, and I bore it. And now the prophet has finished with his prophetess and led me away to this, to a death like this. Instead of my father's altar a butcher's block is waiting for me, hot with the red blood of the victim cut down before the sacrifice, and the victim is me. But we will not die unhonoured by the gods. Someone else will come, another avenger for us, a child who kills his mother, a son who makes men pay for his father. An exile, a wanderer, a stranger to this land, he will come back and set the coping-stone on these ruins for his family. For the gods have sworn a great oath: the body of his father, lying on its back, will bring him home. Why do I stand here wailing and pitying myself? I saw the city of Troy fare as it fared, and the men who took the city are ending like this, by the gods' judgement. I will go and do it. I will bear dying. I greet these doors as the gates of Hades. And I pray for a clean stroke, so that I close these eyes without a struggle, with the blood running out and death coming easy.

Chorus. Woman, you have suffered so much and you know so much, and you have gone on a long time. But if you really know your own death, how can you walk to the altar so bravely, like a cow the god drives?

Cassandra. There is no escape, friends. No. Not with any more time.

Chorus. But the last of the time is worth the most.

Cassandra. The day is here. I would gain very little by running.

Chorus. Then know this: you are bearing it with a brave heart.

Cassandra. Nobody says that to the happy ones.

Chorus. But to die with honour is a grace for a mortal.

Cassandra. Oh, father. Oh, for you and your noble children.

(She stops at the door and turns back.)

Chorus. What is it? What fear is turning you back?

Cassandra. Oh. Oh.

Chorus. Why that cry? Unless it is some horror in your mind.

Cassandra. The house breathes murder. It drips blood.

Chorus. And what of it? That is the smell of the sacrifices at the hearth.

Cassandra. It is the same kind of breath that comes off a grave.

Chorus. That is not Syrian incense you are describing, to make the house splendid.

Cassandra. Well, I will go in, and wail inside the house too, for my own fate and Agamemnon's. Enough of life. Oh, friends. I am not shrinking in fear like a bird at a bush for no reason. When I am dead, be my witnesses to this: when a woman dies for me, a woman, and a man falls for the man who married badly. I ask this of you as a guest's gift, now that I am going to die.

Chorus. Poor woman, I pity you for the death you have foretold.

Cassandra. Once more I want to speak, one speech, not a dirge, my own, for myself. I pray to the sun, in the last of his light I will see, that my enemies, my murderers, pay my avengers in the same coin for a slave killed, an easy thing to overpower. Oh, the affairs of men. When they go well, a shadow can turn them over. When they go badly, a wet sponge, a few strokes, and the picture is gone. And of the two, it is the second I pity far more.

(She goes into the house.)

Chorus.
	Doing well is a thing no mortal
	ever gets enough of.
	Nobody points at a great house
	and turns it away from the door
	with the words, Come in no more.
	And to this man the blessed gods
	gave Priam's city to take,
	and he comes home honoured by heaven.
	But now, if he must pay for the blood of those before him,
	and by dying for the dead
	bring other deaths to pass in payment,
	what mortal, hearing this, could boast
	he was born to a fate that does him no harm?

(A cry comes from inside the house.)

Agamemnon. Oh! I am hit. A deep blow, a deadly one, in here.

Chorus. Quiet. Who is that crying out, hit with a deadly blow?

Agamemnon. Oh! Again. I am hit a second time.

Chorus. The deed is done, I think, by the king's groaning. But let us put our heads together, in case there is some safe plan.

(The old men speak one after another, each giving his own view.)

Chorus. I tell you my opinion: shout for the townspeople, call them here to the house.

I say we break in as fast as we can and prove the deed while the sword is still dripping.

I share that opinion, and I vote we do something. This is no time to hang back.

It is plain to see. Their first move announces what they mean for the city: a tyranny.

Yes, because we are wasting time. And they are trampling the fine name of Delay into the dirt, and their hands are not asleep.

I do not know what plan I could hit on to propose. The man who acts should be the man who plans it too.

I am the same. I cannot find a way to raise a dead man again with words.

What, are we to drag our lives out like this and bow to these men who defile the house, and let them lead us?

No. It cannot be borne. Better to die. Death is a gentler fate than tyranny.

Are we really going to take groans as evidence and prophesy that the man is dead?

We should know for certain before we get angry about it. Guessing is one thing and knowing is another.

That is the view I find myself pushed into from every side: to find out clearly how it stands with the son of Atreus.

(The doors of the house open. Agamemnon lies dead in a silver bath, tangled in the robe, and Cassandra's body lies beside his. Clytemnestra stands over them with the sword in her hand.)

Clytemnestra. I said a great deal before to suit the moment, and I will not be ashamed to say the opposite now. How else could a woman, working out hatred against people she hated, people who pretended to be her friends, fence in the nets of ruin too high to jump out of? This contest is an old one, from an old quarrel. I have been thinking about it for a long time, and it has come at last, late as it is. I stand where I struck, over the work as I finished it. And I did it like this, and I will not deny it: so that he could neither run from his death nor fight it off, I threw a net around him with no way out, the kind you throw for fish, a robe, a rich and deadly robe. I struck him twice, and with two groans his legs went from under him. And when he was down I gave him a third blow, a thank-offering to the Zeus under the earth who keeps the dead safe, a prayer fulfilled. So he lay there and coughed his life out, and as he blew out the sharp spurt of his blood he hit me with a dark spray of bloody dew. And I was glad of it. It fell on me like rain from heaven on the sprouting corn, and I was as glad as the corn is, when the buds are being born. Since that is how things are, old men of Argos, be glad of it, if you can be glad. I exult in it. And if it were the right thing to pour a libation over a dead body, over this one it would be just. It would be more than just. He filled a great bowl in this house with so many cursed evils, and now he has come home and drunk it down himself.

Chorus. We are amazed at your tongue, how bold it is in your mouth, to boast a speech like that over your husband.

Clytemnestra. You are testing me as if I were a woman with no sense. My heart does not tremble, and I say this to people who know it already. Praise me or blame me, it is all the same. This is Agamemnon, my husband, and a corpse, the work of this right hand, a craftsman who did justice. That is how things stand.

Chorus.
	Woman, what evil thing
	did you eat that grew out of the earth,
	what did you drink that welled up from the running sea,
	that you took this sacrifice on yourself, and the curses the people roar?
	You threw him off. You cut him off. And you will be cut off from the city,
	a huge hatred to its people.

Clytemnestra. Now you sentence me to exile from the city, to the hatred of the townspeople and the curses the people roar. But then you brought nothing against this man. He thought no more of it than the death of a sheep, when his flocks were thick with fleece: he sacrificed his own child, the child I bore, my dearest labour, to charm the winds of Thrace. Was it not him you should have driven out of this land, to pay for the filth he brought on it? But when you hear what I have done, you are a hard judge. Well, I tell you this. Threaten me like that, and understand that I am ready: if you beat me by force, on equal terms, you rule me. But if the god decides otherwise, you will learn, late as the lesson comes, to keep yourselves in hand.

Chorus.
	Your plans are big
	and your talk is arrogant.
	Just as your mind is mad
	with the luck that drips with slaughter,
	so the smear of blood on your eyes shows plain.
	Unhonoured yet, stripped of your friends,
	you will pay blow for blow.

Clytemnestra. Then hear this too, the right I swear by: by Justice, who brought my child's case to its end, by Ruin, and by the Fury, to whom I cut this man's throat, hope does not walk the halls of fear for me, so long as Aegisthus lights the fire on my hearth and keeps faith with me as he has before. He is no small shield for my confidence. Here lies the man who wronged his wife, the pet of every Chryseis under the walls of Troy. And here she lies, his prisoner, his seer, his bedfellow, his faithful whore who talked oracles and knew the sailors' benches as well as he did. The two have got what they deserved. He, like that. She, like a swan, sang her last dying lament and lies here, his lover. And to me she has brought a side dish for my bed, an extra to my pleasure.

Chorus.
	Oh, if only some fate would come, quickly,
	without too much pain,
	without long lying in bed,
	and bring us the sleep that never ends, that lasts for ever,
	now that our kindest guardian is brought down,
	who suffered so much for a woman's sake
	and has lost his life at a woman's hand.
Chorus.
	Oh, oh, Helen, out of your mind,
	one woman who destroyed so many,
	so very many lives under Troy.
	Now you have put on your last crown, the one they will remember,
	for blood that cannot be washed out.
	Truly there was a spirit of strife in this house then,
	strong-built, and a man's misery.
Clytemnestra.
	Do not pray for a share of death
	because these things weigh on you,
	and do not turn your anger on Helen
	as a killer of men, as if she alone
	destroyed the lives of so many Greeks
	and worked a pain that nothing can heal.
Chorus.
	Spirit that falls on this house
	and on the two grandsons of Tantalus,
	you rule through women with a strength that matches theirs,
	and it bites at my heart.
	She stands over the body like a hateful crow,
	against all law,
	and glories in the song she sings.
Clytemnestra.
	Now you have set your mouth's judgement straight,
	calling on the spirit of this family,
	the one fattened three times over.
	He is the one who feeds the lust for lapping blood
	in the belly of this house: before the old wound
	stops hurting, there is new blood.
Chorus.
	Yes, a great spirit you speak of,
	a heavy anger that keeps this house.
	Oh, oh, an evil tale to tell,
	of a ruinous fate that is never full.
	Ie, ie, through Zeus,
	the cause of all, the worker of all.
	For what is brought to pass for mortals without Zeus?
	Which of these things is not the gods' doing?
Chorus.
	Oh, oh, my King, my King,
	how shall I weep for you?
	What can I say, out of a heart that loved you?
	You lie in this spider's web,
	breathing your life out in a godless death.
	Oh, oh, this bed no free man should lie on,
	brought down by a treacherous death
	from your wife's hand, with a two-edged weapon.
Clytemnestra.
	You claim this deed is mine?
	Do not even count me
	as Agamemnon's wife.
	It was the old, bitter avenger
	of Atreus, that cruel host at the feast,
	who took the shape of this dead man's wife
	and paid him back with this,
	a grown man sacrificed on top of the children.
Chorus.
	That you are guiltless of this murder,
	who will witness that? How? How?
	And yet the avenger from his father's side
	could have been your helper.
	Ares, the black god of war, forces his way on
	through streams of kindred blood,
	and wherever he goes forward he will grant justice
	for the clotted gore of children eaten.
Chorus.
	Oh, oh, my King, my King,
	how shall I weep for you?
	What can I say, out of a heart that loved you?
	You lie in this spider's web,
	breathing your life out in a godless death.
	Oh, oh, this bed no free man should lie on,
	brought down by a treacherous death
	from your wife's hand, with a two-edged weapon.
Clytemnestra.
	I do not think his death
	was one no free man should die.
	Did he not bring a treacherous ruin
	on this house himself?
	He did to the child I grew from him,
	Iphigenia, so much wept for,
	what he deserved to suffer for. Let him not boast
	in Hades: killed by the sword,
	he has paid with death for what he began.
Chorus.
	I have no plan. I am stripped
	of the ready thought that could tell me
	where to turn, and the house is falling.
	I am afraid of the beat of the rain of blood
	that shakes the house: it is not drops any more.
	And Fate is sharpening justice for another deed of harm
	on other whetstones.
Chorus.
	Oh, earth, earth, if only you had taken me
	before I saw this man lying low
	in the silver-sided bath.
	Who will bury him? Who will sing his dirge?
	Will you dare do it, you who killed
	your own husband, to wail over him
	and pay his soul a graceless grace
	for great things done, and wrong him again?
	Who will speak the praise over the godlike man's tomb,
	with tears falling,
	and labour at it with truth in his heart?
Clytemnestra.
	That care is not yours to trouble about.
	By our hands
	he fell, he died, and we will bury him,
	and not with weeping from the house.
	No: Iphigenia, his daughter,
	as she should, will meet her father gladly
	at the fast-running ferry of sorrows,
	and throw her arms around him, and kiss him.
Chorus.
	Here comes reproach to answer reproach.
	It is hard to judge.
	The robber is robbed. The killer pays.
	And it stays, while Zeus stays on his throne:
	the doer must suffer. That is the law.
	Who could throw the seed of the curse out of the house?
	The family is glued to ruin.
Clytemnestra.
	You have walked into that prophecy with the truth on your side.
	And I, for my part, am willing
	to swear an oath to the spirit of the house of Pleisthenes:
	I will be content with this, hard as it is to bear,
	and for the rest, let him go out of this house
	and wear down some other family
	with deaths inside itself.
	A small share of the goods is enough for me, all I want,
	if I can take the madness of kin killing kin
	out of these halls.

(Aegisthus comes in with armed men behind him.)

Aegisthus. Kind light of the day that brings justice! Now at last I can say that the gods who avenge men look down from above on the sufferings of the earth, now that I see this man lying here, to my delight, in the woven robe the Furies made, paying in full for the schemes of his father's hand. For Atreus, who ruled this land, this man's father, when his right to rule was challenged, drove my father Thyestes, to say it plainly, his own brother, out of the city and out of his home. And when the wretched Thyestes came back as a suppliant to the hearth, he found a fate safe enough for himself: he did not die and stain his native ground with his own blood. But as his guest-gift, this man's godless father Atreus, more eager than friendly, pretending to keep a feast-day cheerfully with a meal of meat, served my father a dinner of his own children's flesh. The feet, and the fingers spread out to their tips, he broke off and hid underneath, and the men sat each at his own table. My father took the unrecognisable pieces at once, not knowing, and ate a meal that has been, as you see, the ruin of the family. Then he realised the unholy thing that had been done and screamed, and fell back vomiting the butchered flesh, and called down a fate too heavy to bear on the house of Pelops, kicking the table over to go with his curse: so perish the whole race of Pleisthenes. That is why you see this man fallen here. And I am the one who stitched this murder together, and justly. He drove me out too, with my miserable father, the third child, a baby still in swaddling clothes. But I grew up, and Justice brought me home again. I laid hands on this man from outside the house, and I put together every piece of the plan that undid him. So now even death would be a fine thing for me, having seen him in the net of Justice.

Chorus. Aegisthus, I have no respect for outrage in the middle of misery. You say you killed this man on purpose, and that you alone planned this pitiful murder. Then I say your head will not escape, in a court of justice, the curses the people throw, and the stones. Be sure of it.

Aegisthus. You talk like that, you, sitting at the lower oar, when the men on the upper bench hold the ship? Old as you are, you will learn how heavy a lesson is for a man your age, when the lesson set is to keep yourself in hand. Chains and the pangs of hunger, even in old age, are the very best prophet-doctors for teaching the mind. You have eyes. Do you not see this? Do not kick against the goad. You will hit it and hurt yourself.

Chorus. Woman. You stayed at home and waited for the men to come back from the fighting, disgracing a man's bed all the while, and you plotted this death for the general of the army?

Aegisthus. Those words too will be the start of your tears. You have a tongue that is the opposite of Orpheus's. He led everything after him with joy at his voice. You have stirred us up with your childish yelping, and you will be led away. When you are mastered you will look tamer.

Chorus. So you are going to be tyrant over the men of Argos, you, who planned this man's death and then did not have the courage to do the deed with your own hand?

Aegisthus. Because the trapping was clearly the woman's job. I was an old enemy, and suspected. But now I will try to rule the citizens with this man's money. And the one who does not obey, I will yoke him under a heavy yoke, and he will be no well-fed trace-horse on barley for me. No. Hunger, that hateful lodger who lives with darkness, will see him gentle.

Chorus. Then why, out of the cowardice of your soul, did you not kill the man yourself? Why did a woman kill him, a pollution on the land and on the gods of the land? Does Orestes still see the light somewhere, so that he can come home with luck on his side and kill this pair, and win outright?

Aegisthus. Since you mean to act and talk like this, you will learn soon. Come on, my men, my guard: your work is not far off.

Chorus. Come on, then. Every man get his sword ready, hand on the hilt.

Aegisthus. I have my hand on my hilt too, and I do not refuse to die.

Chorus. Die, you say. We take the omen. We choose that luck.

Clytemnestra. No, dearest of men. Let us not do any more harm. Even this is a great deal to reap, a wretched harvest. There is enough suffering already. Let us not have more blood. Go home, honoured old men, and give way to what is fated before you get hurt. What we did had to be done. And if this trouble turns out to be enough, we will accept it, struck hard as we have been by the heavy claw of the spirit. That is a woman's counsel, if anyone thinks it worth learning.

Aegisthus. But that these men should pick the flowers of empty talk and throw them at me, and fling out words like that, and put their luck to the test, and miss the sensible line, and insult the man in power!

Chorus. It would not be like men of Argos to fawn on a coward.

Aegisthus. I will come after you yet, in the days to come.

Chorus. Not if the god steers Orestes home.

Aegisthus. I know how exiles feed on hope.

Chorus. Go on. Get fat, and dirty justice, while you can.

Aegisthus. Know that you will pay me for this stupidity.

Chorus. Crow away, brave, like a cock beside his hen.

Clytemnestra. Pay no attention to their empty yelping. You and I are masters of this house, and you and I will set this house in order.

The Libation Bearers

(Argos, years after the murder of Agamemnon. His tomb stands in the open ground before the palace. Orestes comes in with his friend Pylades and lays a lock of his hair on the grave.)

Orestes. Hermes of the underworld, you who watch over the powers your father handed down to you: be my saviour, fight at my side, I am asking you. I have come back to this land. I have come home from exile. Here on the mound of this tomb I call to my father to hear me, to listen. Here is a lock of hair for Inachus, the river of my home, in thanks for the years it raised me, and here is a second one, for grief. Because I was not here to cry over your death, father, and I never stretched out my hand when they carried your body to the grave.

What is this I am looking at? What is this crowd of women coming this way, so plain to see in their black cloaks? What kind of trouble should I read into it? Has some new blow fallen on the house? Or would I be right to guess that they are carrying offerings to my father, drink poured out to soothe the dead below? It can be nothing else. Yes, that is Electra, I think, my own sister, walking with them, and her bitter grief marks her out. Zeus, let me pay back my father's death, and be my willing ally.

Pylades, let us stand out of the way, so I can find out for certain what these women have come here to pray for.

(Orestes and Pylades step back out of sight. Electra comes in with the women of the house, slaves, who carry the offerings for the grave.)

Chorus.
	Sent out from the house, I have come
	to carry the offerings, and my hands beat the time,
	sharp blow on blow.
	My cheek shows it, torn red,
	where the nail has cut fresh furrows,
	and all my life long my heart feeds on crying.
	Grief has ripped the linen to rags,
	the weaving of my dress is torn
	across the breast, the cloth
	struck down by blows of fortune
	that no one laughs at.
Chorus.
	For Terror, with his hair standing on end,
	the dream-prophet of this house,
	breathing anger out of sleep,
	screamed in the dead of night
	from the innermost rooms,
	a cry of fear that fell heavy
	on the women's quarters.
	And the readers of dreams,
	speaking for the god, under oath,
	cried out that the dead under the earth
	are furious, that they blame,
	that they rage at the ones who killed them.
Chorus.
	With that kind of thanks, a thankless thanks,
	to turn the evil away,
	Oh, Earth, my mother,
	the godless woman sends me here.
	I am afraid to let the words out of my mouth.
	What ransom is there for blood once it has fallen on the ground?
	Oh, hearth of pure misery,
	Oh, house dug up to its foundations!
	Sunless, hated by men,
	the dark covers this house
	since its master was killed.
Chorus.
	The awe of majesty that nothing could fight,
	nothing could tame, nothing could break in war,
	that went in through the ears and the hearts of the people,
	stands aside now.
	And people are afraid.
	Success: that, among men, is a god,
	and more than a god.
	But the scales of Justice keep watch.
	For some the blow comes fast, in daylight;
	for some, pain waits and ripens
	in the twilight between;
	and some an empty night holds fast.
Chorus.
	Because of blood drunk down by the earth that nursed us,
	the murder that wants payment has set hard
	and will not drain away.
	Ruin, an agony, splits the guilty man
	till he swells with a sickness that fills him whole.
Chorus.
	For the man who touches a bride's bed that is not his
	there is no cure. All the rivers,
	flowing in one course to wash the blood
	from the polluted hand, run for nothing.
Chorus.
	And I, since the gods threw a necessity around my city
	and led me out of my father's house
	into a slave's lot,
	I must approve what my masters do,
	just and unjust, forcing my heart down,
	and master my bitter hatred.
	But under my veil I weep
	for the senseless fate of my lord,
	frozen through with hidden grief.

Electra. You slave women who keep this house in order: since you are here beside me, walking with me in this procession of prayer, advise me about this. What should I say as I pour these offerings of mourning? How do I find kind words? How do I pray to my father? Do I say I bring them from a loving wife to a loved husband, from my mother? I have not got the nerve for that, and I do not know what I should say as I pour this offering onto my father's tomb. Or do I say the words people usually say, "Repay with good the ones who send these honours," a gift, that is, to match their crimes? Or do I pour them out in silence and dishonour, the way my father died, for the earth to drink, and then walk back the way I came, like someone throwing out the rubbish after a purification, tossing the bowl away without turning my eyes? Share this decision with me, friends. We share a hatred in this house. Do not hide your thoughts in your heart out of fear of anyone. What is fated waits for the free man and for the one whom another's hand rules over. If you have something better to say than this, say it.

Chorus. I honour your father's tomb as if it were an altar, and since you tell me to, I will say what my heart says.

Electra. Say it, as you honour my father's grave.

Chorus. Pour, and speak good words for the people who mean us well.

Electra. And which of the people I love do I call by that name?

Chorus. Yourself first, and then anyone who hates Aegisthus.

Electra. Then I am to make this prayer for myself and for you?

Chorus. You see it now. Work the rest out yourself.

Electra. Then who else do I add to our side?

Chorus. Remember Orestes, even though he is far from home.

Electra. Good. That is the best reminder you could have given me.

Chorus. Now, for the guilty ones who did the killing, remember —

Electra. What do I say? Teach me, I have never done this. Show me the form.

Chorus. That someone come for them, a god or a man —

Electra. A judge, do you mean, or an avenger?

Chorus. Say it plainly: someone who will kill them in return.

Electra. And is it right for me to ask the gods for that?

Chorus. How could it not be right, to pay back an enemy with harm?

Electra. Greatest of heralds, of the world above and the world below, Hermes of the underworld, help me. Carry my call to the spirits under the earth, the ones who keep watch over my father's house, and tell them to hear my prayers. And call Earth herself, who gives birth to everything, and rears it, and takes its swelling harvest back again. And I, as I pour this water to the dead, call on my father and say: pity me, and pity Orestes, whom I love. How are we to be masters in our own house again? Because as things stand we have been sold, we are drifting, thanks to the woman who bore us, and she has taken another man in exchange for you: Aegisthus, who shared in your murder. I am no better than a slave. Orestes is driven out of his inheritance, and the two of them are wallowing in the winnings of your work, and the arrogance of it is enormous. That Orestes may come home, with some luck on his side, I pray to you, father. Hear me. And for myself, grant that I turn out far more decent than my mother, and my hand far cleaner.

Those are the prayers for us. For our enemies I say this: father, let your avenger appear, and let the killers be killed in their turn, as Justice demands. I set that in the middle of my prayer for good, speaking this curse against them. But to us, send up good things from below, with the help of the gods and Earth and Justice who brings victory.

(Electra pours the offerings out over her father's grave.)

Those are my prayers, and over them I pour these offerings. It is your part, by custom, to crown them with your cries, and raise the song for the dead.

Chorus.
	Let the tear fall, splashing as it drops,
	for our lord who has fallen,
	onto this rampart against evil,
	this ward against the pollution
	the good turn from and pray away,
	now the offerings are poured.
	Hear me, hear me, my honoured lord,
	out of the dark where your mind lies.
	Otototototoi.
	Let some man come, strong with the spear,
	to set the house free,
	an Ares, with the Scythian bow bending back in his hands,
	shaking its arrows in the fight,
	swinging a sword hilt-to-hand at close quarters.

Electra. My father has his offerings now, the earth has drunk them. But here is something new. Share it with me.

Chorus. Say it. My heart is dancing with fear.

Electra. I see a lock of hair here, cut off and left on the tomb.

Chorus. A man's? Or some girl's, with the deep belt at her waist?

Electra. That is easy for anyone to guess.

Chorus. How so? Let the old learn from the young.

Electra. There is no one who could have cut it but me.

Chorus. True. The ones who should have mourned him with their hair are his enemies.

Electra. And look, to my eye it is very like, the same feather as —

Chorus. Like whose hair? That is what I want to know.

Electra. Very like our own, to look at.

Chorus. Then can it be a secret gift from Orestes?

Electra. It is his curls it looks like most.

Chorus. And how did he dare to come here?

Electra. He sent it. He cut his hair and sent it, to honour his father.

Chorus. What you say gives me no less to cry over, if he is never going to set foot on this land again.

Electra. Over my heart too a wave of bitterness has broken, and I am struck as if a spear had gone right through me. Out of my eyes fall thirsty drops, nothing holds them back, a winter flood, as I look at this lock of hair. How can I expect that any other person in this town owns it? And she certainly did not cut it, the murderess, my mother, whose feeling for her children is godless and does not deserve the name of mother. But as for me, how can I say outright that this is a gift to me from Orestes, the dearest man alive? Hope is fawning on me.

Electra. Oh. If only it had a voice, and could speak kindly, like a messenger, so that I would not be torn two ways and shaken, but it would tell me plainly to spit this lock out, if it was cut from a head I hate, or, if it came from my own blood, it could grieve with me, an ornament on this tomb, an honour to my father. But we call on the gods, who know what storms are spinning us round, like sailors. And if we are meant to reach safety, from a small seed a great trunk can grow. And look, footprints, a second piece of proof, the same shape as mine, matching my own feet. Yes, here are the outlines of two pairs of feet, his own, and some companion who travelled with him. The heels, the lines of the tendons, measured out, come to exactly the same as my own tracks. I am in labour with this. It is destroying my mind.

(Orestes comes out from where he was hidden and stands in front of her.)

Orestes. Pray for the rest as well. Tell the gods your prayers have been answered, and ask that what comes next goes well.

Electra. Why? What have I got now, by the will of the gods?

Orestes. You have come face to face with what you prayed for so long.

Electra. And what person do you know that I was calling on?

Orestes. I know you were crying out for Orestes, again and again.

Electra. Then how has my prayer been answered?

Orestes. Here I am. Do not look for anyone closer to you than me.

Electra. Is this some trick you are weaving around me, stranger?

Orestes. Then I am spinning plots against myself.

Electra. No. You want to laugh at my misery.

Orestes. Then at my own too, if I laugh at yours.

Electra. Am I to call you Orestes, then? Is it really you?

Orestes. You are looking at the man himself, and you are slow to know me. Yet when you saw this lock of mourning hair, and when you were tracking my footprints, your heart took wing and you were sure you saw me. Look. Put the hair back where it was cut from, your own brother's hair, and see how it matches my head. And look at this weaving, the work of your own hands, the strokes of the batten, and the hunting scene in the design. Hold yourself together. Do not let joy knock your mind loose. I know that the people closest to us are bitter enemies to us both.

Electra. Oh, dearest of all to your father's house, the hope we wept for, the seed that saves the line, trust your own strength and you will win your father's house back. Oh, face I love, that has four shares of love in it for me. I have to call you father; and the love I owe a mother falls to you, because her I hate, with every right; and the love for my sister, slaughtered without pity; and you were my faithful brother, the one person who honoured me. May Power and Justice, and Zeus with them, the third, greatest of all, stand at your side.

Orestes. Zeus, Zeus, look down on what is happening here. See the brood of an eagle whose father is dead, dead in the coils, in the twisting knots of a terrible snake. The orphans are pinched with starving hunger, because they are not grown enough to bring their father's kill back to the nest. That is what you see when you look at me, and at her, at Electra, children robbed of their father, both of us driven out of the same house. If you destroy the nestlings of a father who sacrificed to you and honoured you so highly, where will you find another hand like his to give you the rich feast? Destroy the eagle's brood, and you will never again send signs that men can believe; and if this royal trunk is left to wither, it will not serve your altars on the days when oxen are slaughtered. Look after it. From something small you could raise this house to greatness again, though it looks now as if it has fallen flat.

Chorus. Children, saviours of your father's hearth, quiet, so that no one hears you, children, and carries all this to the ones in power for the pleasure of talking. May I see them dead one day, in the pitch, in the ooze of the flame.

Orestes. Apollo's oracle will not betray me, it is too strong.
