It ordered me to run this risk to the end, and cried out to me at the top of its voice, naming ruin after ruin, storms of it, to chill the warm blood under my liver, if I do not go after the men who killed my father, and kill them the same way they killed him, made wild as a bull by a penalty no money can pay. He said that I myself would pay for it with my own dear life, and suffer a great deal first, and none of it pleasant. Because he showed men what the anger of the dead under the earth does to the living, and he named the plagues: sores that climb onto the flesh with savage jaws and eat away what a man was born with, and white hair sprouting on the diseased place. And he spoke of other attacks the Furies make, set in motion by a father's blood: the dark arrow of the powers below, loosed when kin who fell cry out for vengeance, and madness, and empty terrors in the night, that harass a man and drive him, a man who sees clearly and yet has to feel his way in the dark, and hound him out of his city, his body flayed by a scourge of beaten bronze. And for such a man, the god said, there is no share in the wine-bowl, no drop of the friendly offering; his father's anger, unseen, bars him from the altar; no one takes him in, no one shelters him; and in the end, dishonoured by everyone, without a friend, he dies, badly, wasted to a husk by a death that spoils him whole. Oracles like that: must I not trust them? And even if I do not trust them, the thing has to be done. Many wishes come together into one: the god's commands, and my great grief for my father, and the pinch of having nothing, which presses too, and this: that my fellow citizens, the most famous men alive, who uprooted Troy with glory in their hearts, should not be the subjects of two women. Because his heart is a woman's. If not, he will soon find out.

Chorus.
	You great Fates, by the power of Zeus,
	let this end the way
	Justice is now turning.
	For a word of hatred let a word of hatred
	be paid. That is the debt collected,
	and Justice shouts it as she collects.
	For a murderous blow let a murderous blow
	be paid. The doer must suffer.
	That is the saying, three generations old.
Orestes.
	Father, unhappy father,
	what can I say, what can I do,
	to reach you on a fair wind from far away,
	there where your bed holds you,
	a light to set against your dark?
	And yet a song of grief that honours the sons of Atreus,
	the old masters of this house,
	is called a kindness, all the same.
Chorus.
	Child, the hungry jaw of the fire
	does not tame the mind of the dead man.
	Afterwards he shows his anger.
	The dying man is wailed over,
	and the man who harmed him is shown up.
	A just cry of grief for fathers, for parents,
	once it is raised full and wide,
	goes searching everywhere.
Electra.
	Hear us then, father, in turn,
	our grief and all our tears.
	Two children, over your tomb,
	sing you the dirge.
	Your grave has taken them in
	as suppliants and exiles alike.
	What of this is good? What is free of evil?
	Is ruin not past wrestling?
Chorus.
	And yet, out of all this, a god who wished it
	could still set a song with a brighter sound.
	Instead of the dirge at the graveside,
	a hymn of victory in the king's own halls
	could bring home the friend, a new wine, freshly mixed.
Orestes.
	If only under the walls of Troy,
	father, some Lycian's spear
	had cut you down and killed you!
	You would have left a good name in your house,
	and for your children, in the street,
	a life people turned to look at,
	and had a tomb heaped high
	in a land across the sea,
	a weight your house could bear.
Chorus.
	Loved there below by the men you loved,
	the ones who died well,
	standing out under the earth,
	a lord held in awe,
	right hand to the greatest kings down there,
	the kings of the dead.
	Because you were a king, while you lived,
	one of the men who fill the lot of death
	with their hands, and hold the staff men obey.
Electra.
	No, not under the walls of Troy,
	father, not killed there
	and buried by the crossing of Scamander
	with the rest of the army the spear cut down.
	Sooner let the ones who killed him
	be brought down the same way, by their own people,
	so that someone far off, who knows nothing
	of the trouble we have here,
	hears the news of their deadly end.
Chorus.
	What you say, child, is better than gold,
	greater than any great luck, greater than the Hyperboreans'.
	You can wish it, and it costs nothing.
	But the double lash is coming down now,
	I can hear it strike: the ones who help us
	are already under the earth,
	and the hands of the ones in power, hateful men, are unholy.
	It has gone the children's way.
Orestes.
	That went through my ear
	straight as an arrow.
	Zeus, Zeus, who send up from below
	the ruin that punishes late,
	on the hard, reckless hand of men —
	and yet it is a parent it will be paid to.
Chorus.
	Let it be mine to raise the shout, the shrill
	cry of triumph over the man
	as he is struck, over the woman
	as she dies. Why hide
	what hovers over my heart all the same?
	Before the prow of my heart
	a sharp wind blows, anger,
	rancorous hate.
Electra.
	And when will Zeus, strong with both hands,
	bring his fist down, oh, oh,
	and split their heads?
	Let the land have its pledge.
	After injustice I demand justice.
	Hear me, Earth, and you honoured powers below.
Chorus.
	It is the law: drops of murdered blood
	spilled on the ground demand
	more blood. Slaughter shouts for the Fury,
	who comes from those killed before
	bringing ruin on top of ruin.
Orestes.
	Popoi, you royal powers below,
	look, you Curses of the dead, strong Curses,
	look at what is left of the sons of Atreus,
	helpless, thrown out of their house,
	dishonoured. Which way can we turn, Zeus?
Chorus.
	And again my heart shakes
	as I hear this pitiful cry.
	One moment I have no hope,
	and my insides go black
	at the words I hear.
	Then hope lifts me again to strength,
	pushes the pain away
	and shows me a fair face.
Electra.
	What could we say that would hit the mark
	better than the pain we have suffered from the woman who bore us?
	Let her fawn. It will not be soothed.
	Like a wolf's, raw-hearted,
	the temper we got from our mother will not be coaxed.
Chorus.
	I beat my breast to a Persian dirge,
	the way a wailing woman from Cissia does it,
	and you could see the hands reaching out
	and raining down blows, thick and fast,
	from above, from high above, one side then the other,
	till the strokes rang on my battered, miserable head.
Electra.
	Oh, oh, cruel,
	mother who would dare anything, at that cruel funeral
	you dared to bury a king without his people,
	a husband without mourning,
	and no one wept.
Orestes.
	Nothing but dishonour, all of it. Oh.
	And will she not pay for dishonouring my father,
	with the help of the gods,
	with the help of my own hands?
	Let me only take her life, and then let me die.
Chorus.
	And he was mutilated, you should know this.
	The woman who buried him like that
	did it on purpose, to make his death
	a weight your life could not bear.
	Now you have heard your father's shame, his suffering.
Electra.
	You tell how my father died. And I was kept out of the way,
	dishonoured, counted as nothing.
	Shut up in a back room like a vicious dog,
	I let the tears run, which came more easily than laughter,
	and poured out my grief where no one could see.
	Hear that, and write it on your heart.
Chorus.
	Yes, let it bore through your ears
	to a mind that stands quiet and firm.
	That is how things are.
	What comes next, you must be eager to learn yourself.
	You must go into the fight with a will that does not bend.
Orestes.
	I am speaking to you, father. Stand with the people who are yours.
Electra.
	And I add my voice to his, in tears.
Chorus.
	And all of us together take up the cry.
	Hear us. Come up into the light.
	Stand with us against our enemies.
Orestes.
	Ares will meet Ares, Justice will meet Justice.
Electra.
	Oh gods, judge our plea justly, as justice asks.
Chorus.
	A shiver creeps over me as I hear these prayers.
	What is fated has been waiting a long time,
	but it can come to people who pray.
Chorus.
	Oh, the trouble bred in the blood,
	and the bloody stroke of Ruin
	that jars against the music.
	Oh, the griefs, unbearable, past all groaning.
	Oh, the pain that will not be stopped.
Chorus.
	The house has a cure to dress these wounds,
	not from others, not from outside,
	but from itself, from its own people,
	through raw, bloody strife.
	This is the hymn to the gods under the earth.
Chorus.
	Hear it, then, blessed powers below,
	hear this prayer, and gladly send
	your help to the children, for victory.

Orestes. Father, you died a death no king should die. I ask you: give me the mastery of your house.

Electra. And I ask you for this, father: let me escape, once I have brought great destruction down on Aegisthus.

Orestes. Do that, and men will set the feasts for you that custom owes the dead. If not, you will sit without honour while the others are fed on the rich, savoury smoke of the burnt offerings the earth receives.

Electra. And I, when I marry, will pour offerings to you out of my whole inheritance from my father's house, and before anything else I will give this tomb of yours the place of honour.

Orestes. Earth, send up my father to watch me fight.

Electra. Persephone, grant us victory, and make it beautiful.

Orestes. Remember the bath, father, where they robbed you of your life.

Electra. And remember how they thought up a new kind of net for you.

Orestes. You were caught, father, in fetters that no smith forged.

Electra. And shamefully, in a robe they had plotted over.

Orestes. Do taunts like these wake you, father?

Electra. Are you lifting that head we love, and holding it upright?

Orestes. Either send Justice to fight beside the people who are yours, or let us get the same grip on them that they got on you, if you want to win in your turn after losing.

Electra. And hear this last cry of mine, father, as you look at these nestlings crouched on your tomb. Pity your children, the female and the male together, and do not let this seed of the house of Pelops be wiped out. Because that way you are not dead, even though you died. Children keep a man's name alive after death, like the corks that carry a net and hold the flax cord up out of the deep. Hear us. It is for you that we are grieving like this. Honour what we say and you save yourself.

Chorus. There is nothing to fault in that. You have drawn the plea out long enough to pay this tomb the honour it never had, when no one mourned it. For the rest, since your mind is set straight on the deed, act now, and put your luck to the test.

Orestes. It will be done. But it is not off the track to ask how she came to send these offerings, and what she was thinking of, honouring too late a wound that nothing can heal. A miserable kindness to send to a dead man who cannot feel it. I cannot make it out. The gifts are smaller than the crime. Pour out everything you own to pay for one man's blood and the effort is wasted. That is how the saying goes. If you know, tell me. I want to hear it.

Chorus. I know, child. I was there. It was dreams, and terrors that walk at night, that shook her, and made her send these offerings, godless woman that she is.

Orestes. And did you find out what the dream was, so that you can tell it properly?

Chorus. She dreamed she gave birth to a snake. That is her own account.

Orestes. And where does the story end? What is the point of it?

Chorus. She laid it down in swaddling clothes, as if it were a child.

Orestes. What food did it want, the newborn thing with the fangs?

Chorus. In the dream she offered it her own breast.

Orestes. And her nipple was surely not left unwounded by that loathsome thing?

Chorus. No. It sucked out a clot of blood along with the milk.

Orestes. This is not empty. This dream means a man.

Chorus. Then she screamed in her sleep and woke in terror, and all through the house lamps that had been blinded by the dark flared up to comfort our mistress. And after that she sent these offerings of mourning, hoping they would be a cure cut clean through her troubles.

Orestes. Then I pray to this earth and to my father's grave that this dream comes true in me. I read it, and it fits at every joint. If the snake came out of the same place I came from, and was wrapped in my swaddling clothes, and opened its mouth around the breast that fed me, and mixed a clot of blood into the sweet milk, and she screamed with fear at the pain of it, then she must die by violence, because she has nursed a monster, a thing of horror. I am the snake. I turn snake, and I kill her, just as this dream says.

Chorus. I take you as my reader of this omen. Let it be so. For the rest, tell your friends their parts. Tell some what to do and others what to leave alone.

Orestes. The story is simple. My sister goes inside, and I tell her to keep this agreement of ours hidden, so that the people who killed an honoured man by a trick will be caught by a trick in their turn, and die in the same net. That is what Apollo, lord Apollo, decreed, the prophet who has never yet told a lie.

I will come to the outer gate looking like a stranger, with all the gear of a traveller, and Pylades here comes with me, a guest and friend of the house, sworn to it by the spear. We will both put on the speech of Parnassus, copying the accent of the Phocian tongue. And suppose none of the doorkeepers gives us a cheerful welcome, because the house is in the grip of an evil god: then we will wait there, so that anyone walking past the house will think it over and say, "Why is Aegisthus shutting his door on a man who asks for shelter, if he is at home and knows about it?"

But if I do get across the threshold of the outer gate, and find that man sitting on my father's throne, or if he comes to meet me face to face and lifts his eyes and drops them again, then be sure of this: before he can say, "Where is the stranger from?", I will have him dead, with my quick bronze through him. And the Fury of murder, who has never gone short of slaughter, will drink her third drink, blood unmixed with water.

Now you, Electra, keep careful watch on everything inside the house, so that all this fits together tight. And you women, I advise you to carry a tongue that says only what is right: silence where silence is needed, and the words the moment calls for. As for the rest, I call on the god who spoke to me to turn his eyes this way and set the sword-fight straight for me.

(Orestes, Pylades and Electra go out.)

Chorus.
	The earth breeds many things
	that are terrible, griefs that bring terror,
	and the arms of the sea are crowded
	with monsters that hate us.
	Between the sky and the ground
	lights hang high in the air and come flashing down,
	and every creature with wings, everything that walks,
	could tell you about the anger of the whirlwind,
	the storm that comes roaring.
Chorus.
	But the reckless pride of a man,
	who could put words to that?
	Or to the loves that stop at nothing
	in the hard hearts of women,
	loves that live side by side with the ruin of mortals?
	A loveless love, the kind that masters the female,
	wins its twisted victory
	over the yoked pairs who share one bed,
	beasts and human beings alike.
Chorus.
	Let anyone whose mind is not all feathers
	know this, and learn from it:
	the plan that Althaea lit with fire,
	Thestius's wretched daughter,
	the killer of her own child.
	She burned the red log, the one
	that was the same age as her son,
	that had shared his life from the day
	he came out of his mother crying,
	and would have measured him out, step for step,
	to the day the Fates had fixed.
Chorus.
	And there is another to hate in the old stories,
	a girl with blood on her hands, Scylla,
	who destroyed the man she should have loved
	because his enemies asked it,
	won over by the gift of Minos,
	a Cretan necklace worked in gold.
	While Nisus, her father, breathed in his sleep
	suspecting nothing, she cut away
	the one hair that kept him from dying,
	the bitch-hearted girl.
	And Hermes, who leads the dead, came for him.
Chorus.
	And since I have called to mind
	pains that nothing could soften,
	this is the moment for a marriage no one could love,
	a thing this house prays to be rid of,
	and the schemes a woman's mind laid
	against her husband in his armour,
	a husband even his enemies had reason to honour.
	I honour the hearth that no fire of passion heats,
	and in a woman a spear-point that dares nothing.
Chorus.
	Of all crimes the one from Lemnos comes first
	in the telling, the women who killed every man on the island.
	People still groan over it as a thing to spit at,
	and whenever a new horror comes
	they measure it against Lemnos.
	Because of a deed the gods loathed
	a whole race was dishonoured and is gone.
	No one respects what the gods hate.
	Which of these stories have I gathered without good reason?
Chorus.
	The sword is near the lungs.
	Sharp, bitter, it drives clean through,
	because Justice sends it.
	For what is not right
	is lying on the ground, kicked and trampled,
	when a man has stepped over the whole majesty of Zeus
	against everything that is lawful.
Chorus.
	The anvil of Justice is planted firm.
	Fate, who forges swords, is hammering the bronze ahead of time,
	and the Fury, famous in story, brooding in the deep,
	is bringing a child into the house
	to pay off, at last, the filth of old blood.

(Orestes and Pylades come to the palace door, dressed as travellers. Orestes knocks.)

Orestes. Boy! Boy! Do you hear me knocking at the courtyard door? Who is inside? Boy! Boy, I say it again, is anyone at home? This is the third time I have called for someone to come out of the house, if Aegisthus keeps a house that welcomes strangers.

Servant. All right, all right, I hear you. Where is the stranger from? What country?

Orestes. Announce me to the masters of the house. It is to them I have come, and I bring news. And hurry, because the dark chariot of night is coming on fast, and it is time for travellers to drop anchor in a house that takes in guests. Let someone with authority come out, the woman in charge of the place, though a man would be more fitting. Between men there is no embarrassment to blur the words. A man speaks to a man with confidence and shows plainly what he means.

(The Servant goes in. Clytemnestra comes to the door with a slave woman behind her.)

Clytemnestra. Strangers, tell me what you need. We have here everything a house like this should have: hot baths, beds to charm away the tiredness of the road, and the presence of honest faces. But if there is some other business that needs more careful counsel, that is men's work, and we will share it with them.

Orestes. I am a stranger, a man of Daulis in Phocis. I was on the road to Argos, carrying my own pack on my own back, and just as I unyoked my feet and stopped here, a man fell in with me, a stranger to me as I was to him. He asked where I was going and told me his own road. He was Strophius the Phocian, I learned his name as we talked, and he said to me: "Stranger, since you are going to Argos anyway, remember this carefully and tell his parents that Orestes is dead. Do not, whatever happens, let it slip your mind. And whether his people decide to bring him home or to bury him where he died, a foreigner, a guest for ever, carry their instructions back to me." For now the sides of a bronze urn hold the ashes of a man who has been well mourned. That is what I heard, and that is what I have told you. Whether I happen to be speaking to the people whose business this is, his own family, I do not know. But his parent ought to know the truth.

Clytemnestra. Oh, no. Your words say we are sacked to the top of the walls. Curse on this house, so hard to wrestle down, how far you see! Even what was laid well out of your reach you bring down with a well-aimed arrow from a long way off, and you strip me bare of the people I love, wretched woman that I am. And now Orestes. He was being sensible, keeping his foot out of the deadly mud. And now the one hope of a cure for this house, for its fine wild revelling, write him down as a deserter.

Orestes. For my part, with hosts as fortunate as you, I would have liked to make myself known and be welcomed on account of good news. Where is there more goodwill than between guest and host? But to my mind it would have been an offence against the gods not to carry out a charge like this for people I was bound to, when I had given my word and been taken in as a guest.

Clytemnestra. You will get nothing less than you deserve, and be no less a friend to this house for it. Someone else would have come and brought this news just the same. But it is the hour when strangers who have been on the road all day on a long journey should get what is proper.

(She turns to the slave woman.)

Take him to the men's guest rooms, him and these attendants of his and his fellow traveller, and let them be looked after there as this house does things. I order you to see to it, and you will answer for it. Meanwhile we will share this with the master of the house, and, since we are not short of friends, we will take counsel together about what has happened.

(All go into the house. The Chorus is left alone.)

Chorus.
	Well then, women of the house, slaves and friends,
	when will we show the strength
	that is in our mouths, for Orestes?
	Sacred earth, and sacred lip of the mound
	that lies now on the body of the king,
	the commander of the fleet,
	hear us now, help us now.
	Now is the moment for Persuasion, the cunning one,
	to go down into the fight beside him,
	and for Hermes of the underworld, Hermes of the night,
	to stand over this contest
	of the killing sword.

(The Nurse comes out of the house, crying.)

Chorus. It looks as if the stranger is working some harm. There is Orestes' nurse coming out in tears. Cilissa, where are you going, out through the palace gates? And grief walking with you, a companion nobody hired?

Nurse. The mistress orders me to call Aegisthus for the strangers, as fast as I can, so that he can come and hear this fresh news more clearly, man from man. In front of the servants she put on a gloomy face, but behind her eyes she was hiding her laughter at how well things have turned out for her. For this house it is disaster from top to bottom, and the strangers told it plainly. And he, when he hears the story, he will be glad in his heart all right. Oh, I am wretched. The old griefs, all mixed together, the ones so hard to bear that happened in this house of Atreus, they hurt my heart inside my chest badly enough. But I never had to hold up under a blow like this. The other troubles I bailed out patiently, like water from a boat. But my Orestes, my darling, the thing I wore my soul out on, the one I took from his mother's arms and brought up. And the shrill orders that had me walking the floor all night, all the drudgery that got me nothing, and I put up with it. A thing with no sense in it you have to feed like an animal, how else? By guessing what it wants. A baby still in its nappies cannot tell you anything, whether it is hungry, or thirsty, or needs to pee. A baby's little belly answers to nobody. I had to be a prophet for all that, and many a time, I think, I got it wrong, and then I was the one washing the baby's linen. Laundress and nurse, it was the same job. I did both those trades with my two hands, and that is how I took Orestes from his father to raise him. And now, wretched woman, they tell me he is dead. And I am on my way to the man who has done this house so much harm, and he will be glad enough to hear it.

Chorus. Then how did she tell him to come? With what, in what company?

Nurse. How do you mean, how? Say it again so I understand you better.

Chorus. With his bodyguard, or by himself?

Nurse. She says to bring his spearmen with him.

Chorus. Then don't give that message to the master we all loathe. Tell him to come by himself, so he can hear it without being afraid, and tell him as fast as you can, with a glad heart. In a messenger's mouth a crooked message gets straightened out.

Nurse. What? Are you pleased about the news that has just come?

Chorus. Why not, if Zeus is ever going to turn the wind of our troubles?

Nurse. And how? Orestes, the hope of the house, is gone.

Chorus. Not yet. It would be a bad prophet who read it that way.

Nurse. What are you saying? Do you know something beyond what has been told?

Chorus. Go and give the message. Do what you were sent to do. The gods take care of what the gods care about.

Nurse. Well, I will go, and do as you say. And may it all turn out for the best, with the gods' help.

(The Nurse goes out.)

Chorus.
	Now, Zeus, father of the Olympian gods,
	at my asking
	grant that the fortunes of this house be settled, rightly,
	for those who long to see good order.
	Every word I have cried out is for justice.
	Zeus, may you keep it safe.
Chorus.
	Ah, ah. Set the man inside the house
	in front of his enemies, Zeus,
	because if you raise him up high
	he will gladly pay you back
	twice over, and three times.
Chorus.
	Know that the orphaned colt of a man you loved
	is yoked to a chariot of troubles.
	Set a measure to his running,
	so that we can see him keep his stride
	safe over the ground,
	reaching, at full gallop, to the finish.
Chorus.
	And you gods inside the house,
	who keep the inner room that is glad with its wealth,
	hear us, you who feel with us.
	Come, wash out the blood of what was done long ago
	with fresh justice.
	Let old Murder breed no more children in this house.
Chorus.
	And you who live in the great cavern,
	the one so finely built, Apollo,
	grant that this man's house lifts up its eyes again,
	and looks with loving eyes on the bright light of freedom
	out from under its dark veil.
Chorus.
	And may Maia's son, Hermes, take a hand in it, rightly,
	since no one carries an action on a fairer wind
	when he wants to.
	Many hidden things he brings to light at need,
	and with a word no eye can follow
	he brings night and darkness over people's eyes,
	and by day he is no easier to see.
Chorus.
	And then at last, for the house set free,
	we will sing the famous song,
	the song women sing when the wind sits fair,
	not the shrill, high song of mourning:
	the ship sails well.
	This is my gain, mine, and it grows,
	and ruin stands far off from the people I love.
Chorus.
	And you, take heart when the moment for action comes.
	When she cries out to you "Son,"
	shout back "Father,"
	and carry through the ruin that no one can blame.
Chorus.
	Hold up the heart of Perseus in your chest,
	and for the ones you love under the earth
	and the ones above it,
	do this favour to their bitter anger:
	set bloody ruin inside the house,
	and wipe out the man who is guilty of the murder.

(Aegisthus comes in, alone.)

Aegisthus. I have come, not uninvited but sent for. I hear there is fresh news, that some strangers have arrived and are telling a story that is anything but welcome: Orestes is dead. To take this on top of everything else would be a load dripping with terror for a house that is still raw and bleeding from the murder before it. How am I to judge whether this is true, alive and real, or just women's talk, frightened out of them, that jumps up into the air and dies for nothing? What can you tell me about it that would make it plain to my mind?

Chorus. We heard it, yes. But go in and ask the strangers yourself. There is no strength in messengers compared with a man questioning the man himself.

Aegisthus. I want to see this messenger and test him again, whether he was there himself, close by, when the man died, or whether he is only repeating a dim rumour he picked up. He will not steal past a mind with its eyes open.

(Aegisthus goes into the house.)

Chorus.
	Zeus, Zeus, what am I to say?
	Where do I begin this prayer,
	this calling on the gods?
	How can I find words to match my goodwill
	and finish what I am saying?
	Now the blood-stained edges
	of the blades that cut men down
	are about to do one of two things:
	destroy the house of Agamemnon
	altogether, for good,
	or light fire and light for freedom,
	and win him the rule of the city
	and the great wealth of his fathers.
	That is the wrestling match godlike Orestes
	is about to join, one against two,
	the fresh man waiting at the edge of the ring.
	Let it be for victory.

(A cry comes from inside the house.)

Aegisthus. Ah! Ah! Otototoi!

Chorus. Listen. Listen, again. How is it? How has it been decided for the house? Let us stand back while the thing is being finished, so that we seem to have no part in these evils. The fight has been settled now, one way or the other.

(The Chorus draws aside. A Servant of Aegisthus runs out of the house.)

Servant. Oh, oh, oh, the master is struck down! Oh, again, a third time I cry it: Aegisthus is no more. Open up, as fast as you can, and unbar the women's doors. It will take a young man's strength, and not to help him, he is finished. What use would that be? Help! Help! Am I shouting to the deaf, wasting my voice for nothing on people who are asleep? Where is Clytemnestra? What is she doing? Her own neck is on the razor's edge now, I think, ready to fall, struck down by justice.

(Clytemnestra comes in, alone.)

Clytemnestra. What is this? What is this shouting you are raising in the house?

Servant. The dead are killing the living, I tell you.

Clytemnestra. Oh. I understand the riddle. We are to die by treachery, exactly as we killed. Someone give me an axe, a man-killing axe, quickly! Let us find out whether we win or lose. That is where I have come to, in this whole evil business.

(The Servant goes. The door opens. Aegisthus lies dead. Orestes stands over him with a sword, Pylades behind him.)

Orestes. You are the one I am looking for. He has had enough.

Clytemnestra. Oh. You are dead, Aegisthus, my love, my strength.

Orestes. You love the man? Then you will lie in the same grave with him, and never desert him now that he is dead.

Clytemnestra. Stop, my son. Have some respect, child, for this breast. Here is where you dozed off so many times, sucking with your soft gums the milk that made you grow.

Orestes. Pylades, what do I do? Shall I be ashamed to kill my mother, and spare her?

Pylades. Then what becomes of the rest of Apollo's oracles, the ones given at Delphi? What becomes of the oaths you swore? Count every man your enemy sooner than the gods.

Orestes. I judge you the winner. You advise me well.

(He turns to Clytemnestra.)

Come with me. I mean to cut your throat right next to him. While he was alive you thought him a better man than my father. Sleep with him in death, since you love this man and hate the one you should have loved.

Clytemnestra. I brought you up. I want to grow old with you.

Orestes. Kill my father and then live in the same house as me?

Clytemnestra. Fate had a share in that, my child.

Orestes. Then Fate has arranged this death too.

Clytemnestra. Have you no fear of a parent's curse, child?

Orestes. You gave birth to me and threw me out into misery.

Clytemnestra. I did not throw you out. I sent you to the house of an ally.

Orestes. I was sold, shamefully, and I was a free man's son.

Clytemnestra. Then where is the price I got for you?

Orestes. I am ashamed to throw that in your face outright.

Clytemnestra. No, go on. And name your father's follies in the same breath.

Orestes. Do not put the man who was out there suffering on trial, you who sat at home.

Clytemnestra. It is hard on a woman, child, to be kept from her husband.

Orestes. Yes, but it is the husband's hard work that feeds the women sitting at home.

Clytemnestra. You seem determined, child, to kill your mother.

Orestes. You are killing yourself. Not I.

Clytemnestra. Look out. Beware the hounds of a mother's anger.

Orestes. And my father's hounds, how do I escape them, if I leave this undone?

Clytemnestra. I see it. I am alive, and wailing at a tomb, for nothing.

Orestes. Yes. My father's fate marks out this death for you.

Clytemnestra. Oh, no. This is the snake I gave birth to and fed.

Orestes. The fear in your dream was a true prophet. You killed the man you should not have; now suffer what you should not.

(He drives her into the house. Pylades follows.)

Chorus. I grieve, even for these two, and the double blow that has fallen on them. But since Orestes, who has suffered so much, has reached the crest of all this blood, we choose this anyway: that the eye of the house should not fall and be lost altogether.

Chorus.
	Justice came to the sons of Priam in time,
	a heavy punishment, and just.
	And to the house of Agamemnon
	came a double lion, a double War.
	The exile the oracle at Delphi sent
	has driven it through to the end,
	sped on his way by good counsel from the gods.
Chorus.
	Cry out, cry out for joy, for the house of our masters,
	its escape from evil, from the wasting of its wealth
	under two who fouled it,
	from a fortune that had lost its road.
Chorus.
	And he has come, the one whose business
	is the secret fight, the punishment that plots,
	and in the fight the true daughter of Zeus
	touched his hand.
	Justice, we mortals call her,
	and we hit the right name.
	She breathes deadly anger on her enemies.
Chorus.
	Cry out, cry out for joy, for the house of our masters,
	its escape from evil, from the wasting of its wealth
	under two who fouled it,
	from a fortune that had lost its road.
Chorus.
	What Apollo of Parnassus cried out,
	Apollo who holds the great hollow in the earth,
	comes now against the harm that had settled in so long,
	with a cunning that is no cunning.
	The god's word wins, somehow,
	so that I need not serve the wicked.
	The power that holds the sky deserves our worship.
Chorus.
	Look, the light is here.
	The great curb is off the mouth of the house.
	Rise up, house. Too long, far too long,
	you have lain flat on the ground.
Chorus.
	Soon time, that finishes everything,
	will cross the threshold of the house,
	when every stain is driven from the hearth
	by the cleansing rites that drive out ruin.
	And the dice of fortune will fall again
	and lie with their fair faces up,
	kind to see, kind to hear,
	for the people who live in this house.
Chorus.
	Look, the light is here.
	The great curb is off the mouth of the house.
	Rise up, house. Too long, far too long,
	you have lain flat on the ground.

(Orestes stands over the two bodies. Attendants spread out the robe in which Agamemnon was killed.)

Orestes. Look at them, the two tyrants of this land, who murdered my father and sacked my house. They were majestic once, sitting on their thrones, and they are lovers still, if you judge by what has happened to them, and their oath holds good to its pledges. Together they swore death to my poor father, and swore to die together, and they have kept their word well. And look again, you who are hearing this tale of evils, at the device, the thing that bound my poor father, the shackles for his hands, the fetters for his feet, the pair of them. Spread it out. Stand round it in a circle and show it, this thing that covered a man, so that the Father can see it, not mine, but the one who watches over everything here, the Sun, so that he can see my mother's unholy work and stand as my witness one day in court that I hunted down this death, my mother's death, justly. Aegisthus's death I do not even count. He has paid the penalty for a seducer, as the law says. But she, who plotted this hateful thing against her husband, the man whose children she carried as a weight under her belt, a weight she loved once, and now, as it turns out, a hateful evil, what do you think of her? If she had been born a moray eel or a viper, she would have rotted anyone she touched without even biting, by sheer daring, by the crookedness of her mind. And this thing, what do I call it, however careful I am with my words? A trap for a wild animal? A shroud for a corpse, wrapped round its feet in the bath? No. A net. Call it a hunting net, or a robe to tangle a man's feet. This is what a highway robber would own, a man who cheats strangers and makes his living by stealing silver, and with this snare he could kill men by the dozen and warm his heart at it. May I never have a woman like that living in my house. Sooner let the gods kill me childless.

Chorus.
	Aiai, aiai, for the miserable work.
	You were finished off by a hateful death.
	Ah, ah.
	And for the one who is left, suffering is coming into flower.

Orestes. Did she do it, or did she not? This robe is my witness, dyed where Aegisthus's sword went in. The ooze of blood has worked with time to spoil the many dyes of the embroidery. Now I praise him. Now I am here to mourn him, speaking to this woven thing that killed my father. I grieve for the deed and the suffering and my whole family. The stain of this victory is nothing anyone would envy.

Chorus.
	No one alive gets through a whole life unhurt,
	unharmed to the end.
	Ah, ah.
	One trouble is here now, and another is on its way.

Orestes. But so that you know, for I do not know how this ends: I am like a charioteer driving his horses wide of the course. My mind is hard to rein in and it is carrying me off, beaten, and at my heart fear is ready to sing and dance to the tune of anger. While I still have my wits, I proclaim to the people I love, and I say it: I killed my mother, and not without justice, the unclean murderer of my father, a thing the gods hated. And as for what drugged me with courage for this deed, I name above all Apollo, the prophet of Delphi, who told me that if I did this I would be clear of all blame, and if I let it go, I will not say the penalty. No bowshot could reach that height of pain. And now look at me, how I go, with this branch and this wreath, a suppliant, to the seat at the centre of the earth, Apollo's ground, and the fire they call undying, in flight from this blood, my own family's blood. Apollo told me to turn to no other hearth. And I charge all the people of Argos to bear witness for me, in time to come, to how these evils were brought about. I go out a wanderer, a stranger to this land, and living or dead I leave this name behind me.

Chorus. But you have done well. So do not yoke your mouth to an ill-omened speech, or put evil on your tongue. You have set the whole city of Argos free, and cut off the heads of two snakes with one lucky stroke.

Orestes. Ah, ah! Women, slaves of this house, look at them, there, like Gorgons, dressed in grey, wound about with thick, swarming snakes. I cannot stay here any longer.

Chorus. What are these visions that whirl you round, of all men the one your father loved best? Hold on. Do not let fear beat you altogether.

Orestes. These are no visions to me. These are real. They are the hounds of my mother's anger.

Chorus. It is the blood, still fresh on your hands. That is what is throwing your mind into confusion.

Orestes. Lord Apollo, look, there are more and more of them, and from their eyes they are dripping blood, foul blood.

Chorus. There is one cleansing for you. Apollo's touch will set you free from these torments.

Orestes. You do not see them. I see them. I am being driven. I cannot stay here any longer.

(Orestes runs out.)

Chorus. Then good luck go with you, and may the god look on you kindly, and guard you, and bring you good fortune when you need it.

Chorus.
	Here, on the house of the kings,
	a third storm has blown itself out,
	a storm out of the family's blood.
	First came the eating of children,
	the wretched sufferings of Thyestes.
	Second, the suffering of a man, a king:
	the war leader of the Achaeans died,
	cut down in his bath.
	And now a third has come from somewhere,
	a saviour, or shall I say a death?
	Where will it end? Where will it come to rest,
	the fury of ruin, lulled to sleep at last?

The Eumenides

(Delphi, before the temple of Apollo. The Priestess comes out to open the day's prophecy.)

Priestess. First of all the gods, in this prayer, I give the place of honour to Earth, the first prophet. After her, Themis, who was the second to sit on this seat of prophecy, her mother's seat, so the story goes. Third, by allotment, with Themis willing and no one forcing her, another Titan sat here, a child of Earth, Phoebe. And she gave it as a birthday gift to Phoebus, Apollo, who carries her name as his own. He left the lake and the ridge of Delos, put in at the shores of Athena where the ships come and go, and came to this land and to his seat on Parnassus. The sons of Hephaestus, the men of Athens, built him a road and escorted him with great reverence, taming a land that had been wild. And when he arrived, the people paid him high honour, and so did Delphus, the lord who steers this country. Zeus filled his mind with the inspired skill and set him on this throne, the fourth prophet. And Apollo speaks for Zeus his father.

These are the gods I put first in my prayers. And Athena of the Forecourt, who stands before the temple, has the place of honour in my words. I worship the Nymphs, where the Corycian rock is hollow, loved by the birds, where the spirits come and go. Dionysus holds that ground, and I do not forget him, not since the day the god led his Bacchants to war and stitched up a death for Pentheus like a hunted hare. I call on the springs of the Pleistus, on the power of Poseidon, and on Zeus the highest, who brings all things to their end. And then I take my seat on the throne as prophet. May they grant me now an entrance far better than any before. And if any Greeks are here, let them come in by lot, in their turn, as the custom is. I prophesy as the god leads me.

(She goes into the temple. A moment later she comes back out on her hands and knees.)

Priestess. Horrible to tell, horrible to see with my own eyes. What is inside has sent me back out of the house of Apollo so weak that I cannot stand, cannot walk upright. I am running on my hands, not on my legs. A frightened old woman is nothing. She is a child again.

I was going in to the inner shrine, all hung with wreaths, and I saw a man on the navel stone, a man the gods must loathe, sitting in the seat of a suppliant, his hands dripping blood, holding a sword just drawn and an olive branch cut from the top of the tree, wreathed properly with a great tuft of wool, white fleece. That much I can tell you clearly. And in front of this man an astonishing company of women was asleep, sitting on the thrones. Not women. Gorgons, I would say. No, not even Gorgons: I cannot compare them to any picture of Gorgons. I saw a painting once of the creatures carrying off the feast of Phineus. These have no wings, that I could see. They are black, and altogether disgusting. They snore, and the breath that comes out of them is not to be gone near. Out of their eyes drips a foul liquid. And their dress is not fit to bring before the statues of the gods or into the houses of men. I have never seen the tribe this company comes from, nor the land that can boast it reared this brood without paying for it, and did not weep for the labour afterwards.

What happens from here is the business of the master of this house, mighty Apollo himself. He is a healer and a prophet, a reader of signs, and for other men he cleans the pollution out of their houses.

(She goes. The inside of the temple is seen. Orestes sits at the navel stone with the Furies asleep on the seats around him, and Hermes stands behind. Apollo comes out from the inner shrine and stands beside Orestes.)

Apollo. No, I will not betray you. I am your guardian to the end, standing close beside you or far off, and I will not go soft on your enemies. Look at them now, these mad women, caught. They have dropped asleep, these loathsome girls, these ancient old-woman children, whom no god, no man, no beast ever goes near. They were born for evil and nothing else, because evil darkness is what they live in, and Tartarus under the earth, hated by men and by the gods of Olympus. All the same: run, and do not go soft. They will drive you the length of the mainland and over all the ground a wanderer treads, over the sea and through the island cities. Do not tire too soon, brooding over this labour like a herdsman over his cattle. Come to the city of Athena, sit down there, and take her ancient image in your arms. There we will have judges of your case, and words that charm, and we will find a way to release you from these troubles for good. For it was I who persuaded you to take your mother's life.

Orestes. Lord Apollo, you know how not to do wrong. And since you know that, learn not to neglect me either. Your strength to do good is a guarantee.

Apollo. Remember: do not let fear beat your mind. And you, Hermes, my own blood, my father's son, watch over him. Be true to your name and be his guide. Shepherd this suppliant of mine, for Zeus honours this right of the outcast, and send him out among men with the good luck of a good escort.

(Apollo goes back in. Orestes leaves, with Hermes guiding him. The Ghost of Clytemnestra rises among the sleepers.)

Ghost of Clytemnestra. Sleep, would you? Hey! What use are sleepers to me? It is your doing that I am dishonoured like this among the other dead. Because of the ones I killed, the reproach never lets up among the dead, and I wander in disgrace. I tell you, I carry the heaviest blame down there because of them. And after suffering so terribly at the hands of my nearest and dearest, not one of the powers is angry for my sake, though I was butchered by a son's hands, a mother-killer's hands. Look at these gashes in my heart. Look where they came from. The mind asleep has bright eyes; in the daytime the fate of mortals cannot be seen ahead. You have lapped up plenty of mine, I tell you: wineless libations, sober offerings to soothe you, and the solemn feasts of the night that I used to burn on the hearth of fire, at an hour no other god shares. And I see all of it trampled underfoot. He has slipped away and gone, like a fawn, and lightly too. Out of the middle of your nets he sprang, and he made a great mocking face at you as he went. Hear me. It is my life I am pleading for. Wake up and think, goddesses under the earth. It is a dream that calls you now, and the dream is Clytemnestra, and I am she.

Chorus. (The sleeping women whine, the way a dog whines in its sleep.) Mmm. Mmm.

Ghost of Clytemnestra. Whine away. But the man is gone, far away in flight. He has friends, and they are not like mine.

Chorus. (The whining goes on.) Mmm. Mmm.

Ghost of Clytemnestra. You sleep too deep. You have no pity for my suffering. Orestes, who murdered me, his mother, has got away.

Chorus. (The whine turns into a moan.) Ohh. Ohh.

Ghost of Clytemnestra. You moan, you doze. Will you not get up? Now! What work were you ever made for except doing harm?

Chorus. (The moaning goes on.) Ohh. Ohh.

Ghost of Clytemnestra. Sleep and hard labour, the two true conspirators, have drained the terrible snake of her strength.

Chorus. (Two sharp whines together, and then words, still asleep.) Get him. Get him, get him, get him. Look out.

Ghost of Clytemnestra. You are hunting a beast in a dream, and you yelp like a dog that never lets the work out of its mind. What are you doing? Up! Do not let weariness beat you. Do not let sleep soften you into forgetting my pain. Let my reproaches sting you in the liver. They are just, and to those who keep themselves in hand a just reproach is a goad. Send your bloody breath after him like a wind, dry him up with the heat of it, the fire out of your bellies. After him. Wither him with a second chase.

(The Ghost is gone. The Furies wake, one rousing the next.)

Chorus. Wake up. Wake her, as I am waking you. Still asleep? Up. Kick sleep off you, and let us see if any of this beginning was for nothing.

Chorus.
	Oh, oh, pupax! We have suffered, friends.
	I have suffered so much, and all for nothing.
	We have suffered a pain hard to bear, oh, popoi,
	an evil past carrying.
	The beast has slipped out of the nets and gone.
	Sleep beat me, and I have lost my prey.
Chorus.
	Oh, son of Zeus, you are a thief.
	Young, you have ridden down the old powers,
	honouring your suppliant, a godless man,
	bitter to the parents that bore him.
	You stole away a mother-killer, and you a god.
	Who will say there is any justice in that?
Chorus.
	The reproach that came to me out of my dreams
	struck me like a charioteer
	with the goad gripped in the middle,
	under the heart, under the liver.
	I can feel it, the heavy, the crushing cold
	of the public scourger's cruel whip.
Chorus.
	This is what they do, the younger gods,
	ruling past all justice
	from a throne that drips blood
	about its foot, about its head.
	I can see the navel stone of the earth
	standing thick with a terrible pollution of blood.
Chorus.
	He is a prophet, and he has fouled his own hearth,
	stained his inner shrine with pollution,
	of his own urging, at his own bidding,
	honouring mortal things against the law of the gods
	and rotting the allotments that are older than he is.
Chorus.
	He is a grief to me too, and he will not get that man loose.
	Even if he runs under the earth, he is never set free.
	Polluted as he is, he will find
	another avenger out of his own blood
	coming down on his head.

(Apollo comes out of the inner shrine, his bow in his hand.)

Apollo. Out! I order you. Out of this house, quickly. Get out of my prophetic shrine, or you will take a winged, glittering snake, shot from a golden bowstring, and bring up black foam in your agony, foam from the men you have fed on, vomiting the clots of blood you sucked out of them. This house is no place for you to come near. Your place is where the sentences are beheading and gouged-out eyes and cut throats, where boys have their manhood ruined by the destruction of the seed, where there is maiming and stoning, and where men fixed on a stake under the spine moan long and pitifully. Do you hear what kind of feast you love, and why the gods spit you out? The whole shape of your bodies tells it. Creatures like you should live in the cave of a lion that drinks blood, not rub your filth off on everyone near you in this place of oracles. Get out, you goats with no herdsman. There is no god who loves a flock like you.

Chorus. Lord Apollo, hear us in our turn. You are not partly to blame for this. You alone did all of it, and the whole blame is yours.

Apollo. How so? Draw your speech out that far.

Chorus. Your oracle told the stranger to kill his mother.

Apollo. My oracle told him to take payment for his father. What of it?

Chorus. And then you undertook to receive the fresh blood.

Apollo. Yes, and I told him to turn to this house for cleansing.

Chorus. And then you abuse us, the escort that brought him here?

Apollo. Because it is not fitting for you to come into this house.

Chorus. But this is the duty assigned to us.

Apollo. What duty is that? Go on, boast of your fine privilege.

Chorus. We drive mother-killers out of their homes.

Apollo. And what about a wife who kills her husband?

Chorus. That would not be the murder of her own blood by her own hand.

Apollo. Then you make nothing of the pledges of Hera, who makes a marriage complete, and of Zeus. You count them dishonoured and worthless. And Aphrodite is thrown aside, dishonoured, by that argument of yours, and from her come the dearest things mortals have. The bed a man and a woman are fated to share is greater than an oath, and Justice stands guard over it. If, then, when one of them kills the other, you are so slack that you take no payment and turn no angry eye on them, I say it is unjust of you to hound Orestes out of his home. One case, I see, you take very much to heart; the other, plainly, you handle far more gently. But the goddess Athena will oversee the trial of this.

Chorus. I will never let that man go. Never.

Apollo. Then chase him, and pile up more trouble for yourself.

Chorus. Do not cut my privileges short with your talk.

Apollo. I would not take your privileges if they were offered to me.

Chorus. No, because you are called great in any case, at the throne of Zeus. But I have a mother's blood leading me on. I will hunt that man down for my case, and I am on his trail now.

Apollo. And I will stand by my suppliant and rescue him. A man who turns to a god for cleansing has a terrible anger, among mortals and gods alike, if the god betrays him of his own free will.

(The Furies go out after Orestes. Apollo goes back into his shrine. The scene is now Athens, before the temple of Athena. Orestes comes in and takes hold of the ancient image of the goddess.)

Orestes. Lady Athena, I have come at Apollo's command. Receive kindly a man under a curse. I am not one who still needs cleansing, and my hand is not unclean. The edge is off my guilt already; it has been rubbed away at other houses and on the travelled roads of men. I have crossed land and sea alike, keeping the commands of Apollo's oracle, and now I come to your house and your image, goddess. Here I will keep my place and wait for the end of my trial.

(The Furies come in, scattered, one after another, hunting his trail like hounds.)

Chorus. There. Here is the clear sign of the man. Follow the hints of the informer who has no voice. The way a hound tracks a wounded fawn, we track him by the drops of blood. My lungs are heaving with all this man-killing labour. I have herded every corner of the earth, and over the sea I came flying without wings, chasing him, as fast as any ship. And now he is here somewhere, cowering. The smell of human blood smiles at me.

Chorus.
	Look. Look again.
	Look everywhere, so that the mother-killer
	does not slip away in secret
	with his debt unpaid.
	There he is, and he has found protection again:
	his arms twisted round the image of the immortal goddess,
	he wants to go to trial for what he owes.
	But that cannot be. A mother's blood on the ground
	is hard to gather back. Papai,
	the wet of it, poured into the earth, is gone.
	You must pay it back from your living body:
	let me suck the red pulp out of your limbs.
	From you I will take my feeding, a drink hard to swallow.
	I will dry you up alive and drag you down below,
	so that you pay, agony for agony, for the mother you murdered.
	And you will see every other mortal who has sinned,
	against a god or a guest
	or the parents he owes his love to,
	getting exactly what his justice deserves.
	For Hades is a great auditor of mortals
	under the earth,
	and he watches everything and writes it on the tablets of his mind.

Orestes. I have been taught by suffering, and I know many rites of cleansing. I know where it is right to speak and where to keep silent, and in this matter a wise teacher ordered me to speak. The blood is going to sleep and fading from my hand. The pollution of a mother's murder has been washed out; while it was still fresh it was driven off at the hearth of the god Apollo by the cleansing sacrifice of pigs. It would be a long story from the beginning, how many people I have visited without any harm coming to them from my company. Time cleans everything as it grows old along with it. So now, with a clean mouth, I call in all reverence on Athena, the lady of this land, to come and help me. Without a spear she will win me, and my land, and the people of Argos, as a faithful and true ally for all time. Wherever she is, in some part of the Libyan country, by the waters of Triton, the stream where she was born, standing at her ease or striding into action to help the people she loves, or surveying the plain of Phlegra like a bold commander, let her come, for a god hears even from far away, and set me free from this.

Chorus. No. Not Apollo, not the strength of Athena can save you. You will go to ruin, neglected, never learning where in your heart joy lives, a bloodless thing for the powers below to feed on, a shadow. You do not answer me. You spit my words away, you who were fattened up for me and consecrated to me? Alive you will feed me, and not cut down at any altar. Now you will hear the hymn that binds you.

Chorus.
	Come, then, let us join hands for the dance,
	since we have decided
	to show our hateful music
	and tell how our company
	deals out its allotted work among men.
	We believe we are straight in our justice.
	The man who holds out clean hands,
	no anger of ours creeps up on him;
	he goes through life unharmed.
	But the man who sins, like this one,
	and hides his bloody hands,
	we come against him to the end,
	true witnesses for the dead,
	collectors of the blood he owes.
Chorus.
	Mother who bore me, Mother Night,
	hear me. You bore me to be payment
	for the blind and for the seeing.
	The son of Leto is making me nothing:
	he is taking this cowering thing away from me,
	this hare, who is the proper offering
	to wash out a mother's blood.
Chorus.
	Over our victim we sing this song:
	a madness, a wandering of the wits,
	a wrecking of the mind,
	the hymn of the Furies,
	a spell to bind the mind, without the lyre,
	a withering for mortals.
Chorus.
	This is the lot that Fate, who pierces straight through,
	spun for us to hold for good:
	when mortals bring rash murder
	down on their own kin,
	we go with them until they go under the earth,
	and even dead
	they are not very free.
Chorus.
	Over our victim we sing this song:
	a madness, a wandering of the wits,
	a wrecking of the mind,
	the hymn of the Furies,
	a spell to bind the mind, without the lyre,
	a withering for mortals.
Chorus.
	These lots were fixed on us at our birth.
	The immortals must keep their hands off us,
	and none of them eats at our table;
	and I have no share, no portion, in the pure white robes.
Chorus.
	For I chose the overthrow of houses,
	whenever War, made tame in the home,
	kills one of its own.
	We run down that man,
	and strong as he is
	we wear him down to nothing for the fresh blood.
Chorus.
	We are eager to take these cares off another's hands,
	to let the gods off the work I do,
	so that none of it ever comes to a hearing before them;
	for Zeus has ruled that this tribe, dripping blood and hateful,
	is beneath his notice and his company.
Chorus.
	For I chose the overthrow of houses,
	whenever War, made tame in the home,
	kills one of its own.
	We run down that man,
	and strong as he is
	we wear him down to nothing for the fresh blood.
Chorus.
	Men's proud thoughts, so high under the open sky,
	melt and shrink to nothing under the earth, dishonoured,
	at our attack in our black robes
	and the spiteful dancing of our feet.
Chorus.
	Yes, I leap from high above
	and bring down the full weight of my foot,
	heavy in the fall,
	legs that trip even the fast runner,
	a ruin no one can bear.
Chorus.
	And as he falls he does not know it, so witless is the harm;
	such a darkness of pollution hovers over the man,
	and a rumour full of groaning tells
	of a dark mist over his house.
Chorus.
	Yes, I leap from high above
	and bring down the full weight of my foot,
	heavy in the fall,
	legs that trip even the fast runner,
	a ruin no one can bear.
Chorus.
	For it stays. We are good at plotting,
	and we finish what we start;
	we remember evil deeds, we are revered,
	and mortals cannot talk us round.
	We drive at our allotted work,
	unhonoured, unrespected, set apart from the gods
	in a light with no sun in it,
	a road of rocks for the seeing and the dim-eyed alike.
Chorus.
	What mortal, then, does not stand in awe
	and fear of this,
	when he hears from me the law
	that Fate decreed and the gods gave in full?
	My ancient privilege stays with me still.
	I meet with no dishonour,
	though my station is under the earth
	in a darkness the sun never reaches.

(Athena comes in, wearing the aegis.)

Athena. From far away I heard a voice calling me, from the Scamander, where I was taking possession of the land that the captains and chiefs of the Achaeans assigned to me, a great share of the spoils their spears won, root and branch, to be mine for ever, a choice gift to the sons of Theseus. From there I came, driving my tireless feet, no wings, the folds of my aegis whistling in the wind, this chariot yoked to colts in their prime. I see a strange new company in my land, and I am not afraid, but it is a wonder to my eyes. Who in the world are you? I ask you all together: you, the stranger sitting at my image, and you, who are like no race ever sown on this earth, never seen among goddesses by the gods, and not shaped like anything mortal either. But to speak ill of a neighbour who has done no wrong is far from just, and Right stands well away from it.

Chorus. You will hear it all in brief, daughter of Zeus. We are the children of Night, and we last for ever. In our homes under the earth we are called the Curses.

Athena. I know your family now, and the names you go by.

Chorus. And you will soon learn my office.

Athena. I would, if someone told it plainly.

Chorus. We drive murderers out of their homes.

Athena. And where does the killer's flight end?

Chorus. Where joy is nowhere the custom.

Athena. And you are screaming this man on into that kind of flight?

Chorus. Yes. Because he thought it right to be his mother's murderer.

Athena. Was he forced to it by something else, or afraid of someone's anger?

Chorus. Where is there a goad sharp enough to drive a man to kill his mother?

Athena. There are two parties here, and I have heard half the case.

Chorus. But he will not accept an oath, and he will not give one.

Athena. You want to be called just more than you want to act justly.

Chorus. How so? Teach me. You are not poor in cleverness.

Athena. I say that oaths must not let injustice win.

Chorus. Then examine him, and judge a straight judgment.

Athena. So you would turn the decision of the charge over to me?

Chorus. How could we not? We honour you: you are worthy, and you come of worthy parents.

Athena. And what do you want to say to this, stranger, in your turn? First tell me your country, your family and what has happened to you, and then defend yourself against this charge, if it really is trust in the justice of your case that keeps you sitting here holding my image, beside my hearth, a solemn suppliant in the manner of Ixion. Give me an answer I can understand, to all of this.

Orestes. Lady Athena, first I will lift a great worry out of your last words. I am not a suppliant who needs cleansing, and I did not sit down at your image with pollution on my hand. I will give you strong proof of that. The law is that a man stained with murder is barred from speech until a man who can cleanse blood has sprinkled him with the blood of a new-born animal cut in sacrifice. Long ago, at other houses, I was cleansed that way, with animals and with running streams. So I say that worry is out of the way. As for my family, you will soon hear how it stands. I am an Argive, and my father, whom you are right to ask about, was Agamemnon, the marshal of the men who sailed in the ships, the man with whom you made Troy, the city of Ilium, no city at all. He did not die well when he came home. My black-hearted mother killed him, after she had wrapped him in a cunning net, a net that stands witness to this day to the murder in the bath. And I came home, after all the years of exile before it, and I killed the woman who bore me. I will not deny it. It was killing paid for killing, for the father I loved. And Apollo shares the blame for it with me. He warned me of pains that would goad my heart if I did not do this to the ones who were guilty. Whether I acted justly or not, you judge. Whatever comes to me at your hands, I will accept it.

Athena. The matter is too big for any mortal who thinks he can judge it. And it is not right even for me to decide a case of murder with the quick anger of the Furies behind it. Especially since you came to my house a suppliant fully prepared, clean and harmless, and so I respect you, since you bring my city no harm. But these women have an office that is not easily sent away, and if they do not win their case, the venom of their resentment will fall on the ground afterwards, an unbearable, unending sickness in the land. So it stands. Either way, whether I let them stay or drive them out, brings disaster, and I have no way through it. But since this matter has fallen here, on me, I will choose judges of murder, bound by oath, and I will set up this court for all time. You two, call your witnesses and your proofs, sworn evidence to support your case. And I will pick the best of my citizens and come back, so that they can decide this matter truly, after they have sworn an oath to say nothing against justice.

(Athena goes out.)

Chorus.
	Now the new laws overturn everything,
	if the case of this mother-killer,
	his justice and his crime together, wins.
	From now on this one deed
	will fit every mortal's hand to recklessness.
	Many wounds, real wounds,
	struck by their own children,
	are waiting for parents
	in the time to come.
Chorus.
	No anger of ours, the maenads who watch mortals,
	will creep up on such deeds any more;
	I will let loose every kind of death.
	One man will hear from another,
	each telling his neighbour's troubles,
	and ask when the labour will ease or end,
	and some poor wretch
	will offer comfort that means nothing,
	remedies that hold no weight.
Chorus.
	Let no one struck by disaster
	call out any more
	and cry this word:
	Justice! Thrones of the Furies!
	Some father, some mother
	fresh in her grief,
	may cry those words and weep,
	now that the house of Justice
	is falling down.
Chorus.
	There is a place where fear is good,
	and it should sit
	as a watchman over the mind.
	It pays
	to learn to keep yourself in hand under the strain.
	But who, if he raised no fear
	in his heart at all,
	city or mortal alike,
	would still bow to Justice?
Chorus.
	Praise neither the life with no master
	nor the life ground down under a master.
	God gives the power to the middle in everything,
	and watches over each thing
	in its own way.
	I say a word in proportion:
	Outrage is truly the child
	of a godless heart;
	but from health of mind
	comes the happiness everyone loves
	and everyone prays for.
Chorus.
	And this I say to you, for all time:
	honour the altar of Justice.
	Do not see profit
	and kick it over with a godless foot;
	punishment will follow.
	The appointed end waits.
	So let a man put first
	the reverence he owes his parents,
	and respect
	the stranger he welcomes
	under his roof.
Chorus.
	The man who is just by his own will, and not by force,
	will not go without happiness;
	he will never be wiped out altogether.
	But the man who dares the other way, I say,
	who trespasses and hauls a great cargo
	heaped up without justice,
	will strike his sail in time, by force,
	when trouble takes him and the yardarm splinters.
Chorus.
	He calls out, and nobody hears,
	in the middle of the whirlpool he cannot fight.
	The god laughs at the hot-headed man,
	seeing him, who boasted this would never happen,
	limp with troubles he cannot help
	and failing to top the crest of the wave.
	He has driven the happiness of his whole life
	onto the reef of Justice,
	and he dies unwept, unseen.

(Athena comes in with a herald and the judges she has chosen, and a crowd of citizens behind them. The judges take their seats. Orestes stands in the place set aside for the accused, with the Furies facing him.)

Athena. Herald, make the proclamation and hold the crowd back. Let the Etruscan trumpet, the one that pierces the ear, fill with human breath and send its high note out over the people. While this council chamber fills, it is right for everyone to keep silent and to learn my ordinances: the whole city, for all the time that is to come, and these two parties here, so that their case may be judged well.

(Apollo comes in and takes his stand beside Orestes.)

Chorus. Lord Apollo, rule over what is yours. Tell us what share you have in this business.

Apollo. I have come to give evidence, because this man is by law a suppliant of mine and a guest at my hearth, and I am the one who cleansed him of the killing. And I have come to plead his case myself. The responsibility for his mother's death is mine. Athena, bring in the case, and settle it as you know how.

Athena. The word is yours. I open the case. The prosecutor speaks first and tells it from the beginning; that is the right way to teach us what happened.

Chorus. We are many, but we will be brief. Answer word for word, taking your turn. Say first: did you kill your mother?

Orestes. I killed her. There is no denying that.

Chorus. One fall already, of the three that win a wrestling match.

Orestes. You are boasting over a man who is not down yet.

Chorus. Still, you have to tell us how you killed her.

Orestes. I will tell you. With a drawn sword in my hand, I cut her throat.

Chorus. Who persuaded you? On whose advice?

Orestes. The oracles of this god here. He is my witness.

Chorus. The prophet told you to kill your mother?

Orestes. Yes, and to this moment I have no complaint against my luck.

Chorus. If the vote takes hold of you, you will soon be talking differently.

Orestes. I trust him. And my father will send help from his grave.

Chorus. Trust in the dead, then, you who killed your mother.

Orestes. I do. She was tainted twice over. Two pollutions were on her.

Chorus. How? Explain that to the judges.

Orestes. She killed her husband, and in killing him she killed my father.

Chorus. And so you are alive, and she is free of it. Her death has cleared her.

Orestes. Then why did you not hunt her into exile while she was alive?

Chorus. The man she killed was not her blood.

Orestes. And I am my mother's blood?

Chorus. How else did she feed you, murderer, inside her body, under her belt? Do you disown the closest blood there is, your mother's?

Orestes. Now give your evidence, Apollo. Explain for me whether I had justice on my side when I killed her. That I did it, exactly as it was done, I do not deny. But whether this blood was shed justly or not, in your judgment, is for you to decide, so that I can tell the court.

Apollo. I will speak to you, Athena's great court, with justice, and being a prophet I will not lie. Never once, from my seat of prophecy, have I said a thing about a man, a woman or a city that Zeus, the father of the Olympians, did not order me to say. Learn how strong this plea of justice is, and take my advice: follow my father's will. No oath is stronger than Zeus.

Chorus. Zeus, you say, gave you this oracle: to tell Orestes here to avenge his father's murder and to pay no honour at all to his mother?

Apollo. Yes. It is not the same thing at all: a noble man killed, a man honoured with the sceptre that Zeus gives, and killed by a woman. Not with the darting arrows of a bow shot from far off, the way an Amazon kills, but in the way you are about to hear, Pallas, and you men who sit to divide this case with your votes. She welcomed him home from the campaign, where he had done better, for the most part, than anyone had hoped, and she welcomed him with kind words. Then, as he stepped from the bath, at the very edge of it, she flung a robe over him like a tent, fettered her husband in the endless embroidered folds of that robe, and hacked him down. That is the death I set before you: the death of a man honoured by everyone, the commander of the fleet. And I have described the woman as I have so that the people appointed to decide this case may feel the sting of it.

Chorus. Zeus, by your account, gives the higher honour to a father's death. Yet Zeus himself put his old father Cronus in chains. How is that not the opposite of what you say? Judges, I call you to witness that you heard it.

Apollo. You beasts that everyone hates, you things the gods loathe! Chains can be undone. There is a cure for that, and plenty of ways to be set loose. But once the dust has drunk up a man's blood, once he is dead, there is no standing up again. For that my father has made no charms, though he turns everything else upside down and sets it back in place without so much as a hard breath.

Chorus. Then look at what your plea for his acquittal means. He poured his mother's blood, his own blood, out on the ground, and after that he is going to live in his father's house in Argos? What public altars can he use? What clan will let him wash his hands in the water where the brotherhood washes its hands?

Apollo. I will answer this too, and see how right I am. The mother is not the parent of the thing that is called her child. She is only the nurse of the seed that was newly sown in her. The one who mounts her is the parent. She keeps the young shoot safe, a stranger keeping a stranger's property, if no god damages it. And I will show you proof of what I say. There can be a father without a mother. The witness is standing right here: the daughter of Olympian Zeus, who was never nursed in the dark of a womb, and yet a shoot such as no goddess could ever bear. For my part, Pallas, in everything else as I know how, I will make your city and your people great, and I sent this man to your hearth as a suppliant so that he would be faithful to you for all time, so that you would gain him and those who come after him as allies, goddess, and so that it would stand for ever: the descendants of these men keeping faith with the pledges made today.

Athena. Shall I now order these men to cast an honest vote according to their judgment? Has enough been said?

Chorus. Every arrow we have has been shot. I am waiting to hear how the contest is decided.

Athena. Well? And you two, how do I arrange this so that neither of you can blame me?

Apollo. You have heard what you have heard. As you cast your ballots, friends, keep the oath sacred in your hearts.

Athena. Hear my ordinance now, people of Attica, as you judge the first case ever tried for spilled blood. From this day on, and for ever, the people of Aegeus will have this council of judges.

This hill of Ares, where the Amazons pitched their tents and camped when they came with an army out of hatred for Theseus, and built this new high-towered city against his city, tower against tower, and sacrificed to Ares, which is how this rock got its name, the Hill of Ares: on this hill the reverence of the citizens, and fear, which is reverence's kinsman, will hold them back from doing wrong by day and by night alike, as long as the citizens themselves do not pollute the laws with foul streams. Foul clear water with mud and you will never find a drink.

Neither anarchy nor a master: that is what I advise my citizens to keep and to respect, and not to throw fear out of the city altogether. What man who fears nothing does what is right? Stand in awe, and rightly, of a majesty like this, and you will have a wall for your land and a saving power for your city such as no people on earth possesses, not among the Scythians, not in the land of Pelops. I found this council here: untouched by greed, worthy of reverence, quick to anger, a guard for the land that stays awake over the sleeping.

That was my advice to my citizens, stretched out over the future. Now you must rise, take up a ballot, and decide the case, respecting your oath. I have said what I have to say.

(The judges rise and go one by one to the urns while the Furies and Apollo argue.)

Chorus. And I advise you: this company of ours is heavy for a land to bear. Do not dishonour it in any way.

Apollo. And I order you to fear the oracles, mine and Zeus's, and not to make them barren.

Chorus. Blood is not your business, yet you show respect for a bloody deed. From now on the oracles you give will be unclean.

Apollo. Then was my father mistaken in his judgment when Ixion, the first man ever to kill, came to him as a suppliant?

Chorus. Talk on. But if I do not get justice in this case, I will settle on this land again, and I will be heavy on it.

Apollo. You have no honour among the gods, the young or the old. I will win.

Chorus. You did the same sort of thing in the house of Pheres. You persuaded the Fates to make mortals free from death.

Apollo. Is it wrong to do good to a man who worships you, above all when he needs it?

Chorus. You were the one who broke up the ancient allotments. You tricked the old goddesses with wine.

Apollo. And you, when in a moment you have lost this case, will spit your venom, and it will not hurt your enemies at all.

Chorus. Since you, a young god, ride me down, an old woman, I will wait to hear the verdict. I have not decided yet whether to be angry with this city.

Athena. This part is mine: to give the final judgment. And I add this vote to Orestes' side. No mother gave birth to me. In everything, except for marriage, I am for the male, with all my heart, and I am altogether my father's child. So I will not give more weight to the death of a woman who killed her husband, the guardian of the house. Orestes wins, even if the votes come out equal. Turn the ballots out of the urns, as fast as you can, you judges who were given that task.

(The ballots are turned out and counted.)

Orestes. Phoebus Apollo, how will it be decided?

Chorus. Night, black Mother, are you watching this?

Orestes. Now it is the end of a rope for me, or I go on seeing the light.

Chorus. And for us it is ruin, or we keep our honours and go on.

Apollo. Count the ballots exactly as they fall, friends. Be in awe of doing wrong as you divide them. When judgment is missing, great pain follows; and one ballot, cast, has set a house back on its feet.

Athena. This man is acquitted of the charge of blood. The count of the ballots is equal.

(Apollo goes.)

Orestes. Pallas, saviour of my house! I had lost my father's land, and you have given me a home in it again. Now some Greek will say: the man is an Argive again and lives on his father's property, by the grace of Pallas and of Apollo, and of the third, the Saviour who brings everything to its end, who looked with respect on my father's death and saves me, though he sees these advocates of my mother's.

I am going home now, but first I swear an oath to this land and to your people, for the whole of time to come: no captain of my country will ever come here bringing a well-armed spear against them. If any of them break the oath I am swearing now, I myself, in my grave by then, will make it fail. I will make their marches dispirited and their roads full of bad omens, so that they regret the effort. But while they keep to the straight road and honour this city of Pallas for ever with an allied spear, I will be all the kinder to them.

And so goodbye, you and the people who guard your city. May you get a grip on your enemies that they cannot slip, a grip that saves you and brings victory to your spear.

(Orestes goes out. The Furies turn on Athena in fury at the verdict.)

Chorus.
	Oh, you younger gods, you have ridden down the old laws,
	you have snatched them out of my hands.
	And I, dishonoured, wretched, heavy with anger,
	on this land, ah,
	I will let venom go, venom for my grief,
	out of my heart, a drip on the ground
	that nothing can bear. Out of it
	a blight with no leaf and no child,
	oh Justice, will race across the plain
	and throw its stains on the land to kill men.
	I groan. What shall I do?
	The citizens laugh at me.
	Unbearable, what I have suffered.
	Oh, great is the grief of the daughters of Night,
	the unlucky ones, dishonoured and mourning.

Athena. Listen to me: do not take it with heavy groaning. You have not been defeated. The case came out with equal votes, honestly, with no dishonour to you. But clear evidence from Zeus was there, and the one who gave the oracle was himself the one who testified that Orestes should take no harm for doing what he did. So do not be angry.
