Do not hurl your heavy rage on this land or make it barren, letting loose the drops of your divine spite, savage spearpoints that eat the seed. I promise you, in all justice, that you will have a seat and deep places in this righteous land, sitting on shining thrones beside your hearths, and these citizens will honour you.

Chorus.
	Oh, you younger gods, you have ridden down the old laws,
	you have snatched them out of my hands.
	And I, dishonoured, wretched, heavy with anger,
	on this land, ah,
	I will let venom go, venom for my grief,
	out of my heart, a drip on the ground
	that nothing can bear. Out of it
	a blight with no leaf and no child,
	oh Justice, will race across the plain
	and throw its stains on the land to kill men.
	I groan. What shall I do?
	The citizens laugh at me.
	Unbearable, what I have suffered.
	Oh, great is the grief of the daughters of Night,
	the unlucky ones, dishonoured and mourning.

Athena. You are not dishonoured. So do not, goddesses, in the excess of your anger, make a land of mortals into a place no charm can cure. I too trust in Zeus, and why should I need to say it? I alone of the gods know the keys of the house where his thunderbolt is kept under seal. But there is no need of that. Let me persuade you. Do not fling the words of a reckless tongue at this land, so that everything that bears fruit fails to prosper. Put the black wave's bitter fury to sleep, since you will have your share of honour and a house beside mine. And when you have the first fruits of this wide land for ever, offerings made for the birth of children and for the rite of marriage, you will praise what I said.

Chorus.
	That I should suffer this, ah,
	I with my ancient mind, to live under the earth,
	ah, a thing disowned, a pollution.
	I breathe fury, I breathe out all my rage.
	Oh, oh, earth, ah!
	What is this creeping into me, what pain in my side?
	Hear my anger, mother
	Night. The gods have stripped my old honours
	from me with tricks that cannot be fought,
	and brought me to nothing.

Athena. I will bear with your anger. You are older, and in that you are far wiser than I am. But Zeus gave me a mind too, and not a bad one. If you go off to some foreign land, you will fall in love with this one. I tell you that now. Time, flowing on, will bring these citizens more and more honour, and you, with your honoured seat by the house of Erechtheus, will get more from the processions of men and women than you could ever win from other mortals anywhere.

So do not throw into my country the whetstones of blood that sharpen the young against each other, damage in the guts, a madness like drunkenness without the wine. Do not tear the heart out of my citizens as if they were fighting cocks and plant war among them, war between kin, bold against each other. Let war be outside the door, and let there be plenty of it, for the man with a terrible love of glory in him. But a fight between birds in one house I will not call a war. This is what you can choose from me: do good, receive good, be honoured well, and have your share in this land the gods love most.

Chorus.
	That I should suffer this, ah,
	I with my ancient mind, to live under the earth,
	ah, a thing disowned, a pollution.
	I breathe fury, I breathe out all my rage.
	Oh, oh, earth, ah!
	What is this creeping into me, what pain in my side?
	Hear my anger, mother
	Night. The gods have stripped my old honours
	from me with tricks that cannot be fought,
	and brought me to nothing.

Athena. No, I will not grow tired of telling you the good that is on offer, so that you can never say that you, an ancient goddess, were thrown out of this land by me, a younger one, and by the mortal guardians of my city, dishonoured, an exile from this soil. But if you hold Persuasion sacred, the sweetness and the spell of my tongue, then you would stay. And if you will not stay, then it would not be just for you to bring down on this city any wrath, any rage, any harm to its people. It is open to you to hold land here by right, with full honour for all time.

Chorus. Lady Athena, what place do you say I will have?

Athena. One free of every pain and every misery. Accept it.

Chorus. Say I have accepted. What honour waits for me?

Athena. That no house will thrive without you.

Chorus. You will do this? You will make me that strong?

Athena. Yes. We will set the fortunes of those who worship you standing upright.

Chorus. And you will give me a pledge for all time?

Athena. I have no need to say what I will not do.

Chorus. Your spell is working on me, I think. I am letting my anger go.

Athena. Then stay in the land, and you will win friends.

Chorus. What blessings, then, do you tell me to sing over this land?

Athena. Those that look to a victory with nothing evil in it: blessings from the earth, from the waters of the sea, from the sky; that the winds blow on the land in bright sunshine; that the fruit of the earth and the herds flow in abundance for my citizens and never fail with time; and that the seed of men be kept safe. And be more generous to those who honour the gods, because I, like a man who tends his plants, love this race of just men and would keep it free of grief. That is yours to give. For my part, I will not put up with this city going without honour among men, this city that wins in the glorious contests of war, where Ares kills.

Chorus.
	I will accept a home with Pallas.
	I will not dishonour a city
	that Zeus the all-powerful and Ares
	hold as a fortress of the gods,
	the jewel that guards the altars of the gods of Greece.
	For it I pray,
	prophesying in kindness,
	that life's good fortune come rushing up
	out of the earth in plenty
	under the bright glare of the sun.
Athena.
	This I do, with a glad heart, for these citizens:
	I have settled here among them
	powers that are great and hard to please.
	Everything that happens to men
	has fallen to them to manage.
	The man who has never felt their weight
	does not know where the blows of life come from.
	His fathers' sins drag him
	before them. Destruction says nothing,
	and however loud he shouts,
	its hateful anger grinds him to dust.
Chorus.
	Let no wind blow that damages the trees,
	I speak my favour now,
	no scorching heat that blinds the buds
	and crosses over its own border,
	no fruitless, creeping, endless plague.
	Let the earth feed the flocks
	so they thrive and drop twin lambs
	at the appointed time, and let the rich yield
	of the earth pay honour
	to the lucky gift of the gods.
Athena.
	Do you hear this, guards of my city,
	what she is bringing to pass?
	The Lady Fury has great power
	with the immortals and the gods under the earth,
	and among men, openly and to the end,
	they get their way: for some, songs,
	for others a life blurred with tears.
Chorus.
	I forbid the deaths that cut men down
	before their time.
	Give lovely girls a life with a husband,
	you who have the power, you Fates,
	goddesses, sisters of ours by one mother,
	powers who deal out what is straight,
	who have a share in every house,
	whose weight is felt at every season
	in your just visitations,
	most honoured of gods, everywhere.
Athena.
	That they are bringing this to pass
	for my land, with good will, makes me glad.
	And I love the eyes of Persuasion,
	that she watched over my tongue and my mouth
	against these women when they refused so savagely.
	But Zeus of the assembly has won.
	Our rivalry in doing good
	is the winner, for all time.
Chorus.
	Civil strife, never satisfied with evil,
	I pray that it never roars
	in this city.
	Let the dust not drink
	the black blood of citizens
	and then, in a rage for revenge,
	snatch greedily at the city's ruin,
	killing for killing.
	Let them give joy for joy
	with a mind for what they share,
	and hate with one heart.
	That is the cure for many things among men.
Athena.
	See, they are finding their way
	to the road of kind words.
	Out of these terrifying faces
	I see great profit for these citizens.
	Be kind to the Kindly Ones, always,
	honour them greatly, and you will be seen
	by all to keep your land and city
	on the straight road of justice.
Chorus.
	Farewell, farewell, in the wealth your fate has given you.
	Farewell, people of the city,
	who sit close to Zeus,
	friends of the virgin he loves,
	learning good sense at last.
	Under Pallas's wings you are,
	and her Father stands in awe of you.
Athena.
	Farewell to you too. I must go ahead
	to show you your chambers
	by the sacred light of these escorts.
	Go, and with these solemn sacrifices
	speed down under the earth.
	Hold back what would ruin the land
	and send up what profits it,
	so that the city wins.
	And you, children of Cranaus who hold this city,
	lead the way for these new settlers.
	And may the citizens have a good mind
	for the good that is done to them.
Chorus.
	Farewell, farewell, and again I say it,
	all of you in the city,
	gods and mortals both,
	who keep the city of Pallas.
	Show respect to me in my new home among you,
	and you will not blame
	the chances of your lives.

Athena. I approve the words of your prayers, and I will escort you by the light of blazing torches to the places below and under the earth, with the attendants who guard my image, as is their duty. The eye of the whole land of Theseus is coming out: a glorious company of children, of women, and a band of old women. Do them honour in robes dyed crimson, and let the light of the fire go forward, so that this kindly company of guests in our land may show itself, from now on, in the good fortune of good men.

(The procession forms with torches, and the Furies, robed in crimson, are led away towards their new home under the earth. The Escort sings as it goes.)

Escort.
	Go to your home, great ones, lovers of honour,
	ancient children of Night, under this kindly escort.
Escort.
	Keep a holy silence, people of the land.
	Under the age-old hollows of the earth,
	with honours and sacrifices, revered on every side,
	keep a holy silence, all the people.
Escort.
	Gracious and of one mind with the land,
	come this way, holy ones, and enjoy
	the fire-eaten torch along the road.
	Raise the cry now, over our song.
Escort.
	Peace for all time, between these new settlers
	and Pallas's people. Zeus who sees everything
	and Fate have come down together to seal it.
	Raise the cry now, over our song.

Prometheus Bound (date unknown)

Prometheus Bound

(A bare mountainside at the edge of the world, in Scythia. Power and Force, the servants of Zeus, drag in Prometheus. Hephaestus follows them with his hammer and his chains.)

Power. We have come to the last ground of the earth, the Scythian road, an empty place where nobody walks. Hephaestus, the orders the Father gave you are your business now: to fasten this criminal to the high cliff rock in chains of adamant that nothing can break. Because it was your flower he stole, the flash of fire that makes every craft possible, and he handed it to mortals. For a crime like that he has to pay the gods what he owes them, so that he learns to love the tyranny of Zeus and gets out of the habit of loving mankind.

Hephaestus. Power, and you, Force: as far as you two are concerned, the command of Zeus is carried out, and nothing stands in your way any longer. But I do not have the stomach to chain a god of my own blood by force to this ravine where the winter storms hit. And yet I have to find the stomach for it, whether I like it or not. It is a heavy thing to brush aside the Father's word.

High-thinking son of Themis, whose counsel runs straight: against your will and against mine I am going to nail you with bronze that no one can undo to this rock where no human ever comes. You will never see a mortal's face here or hear a mortal's voice. The sun's bright flame will roast you and change the flower of your skin. You will be glad when night in her embroidered dress hides the light, and glad again when the sun scatters the morning frost. Always, the weight of whatever pain is on you at the moment will grind you down, because the one who is going to relieve you has not been born yet. That is what you got for your love of mankind. You were a god, and you did not cower at the gods' anger; you gave mortals honours that were more than their due. For that you will stand guard over this joyless rock, upright, sleepless, never bending your knee. You will cry out, many times, groans and laments that do no good, because the mind of Zeus is hard to move. Everyone is harsh whose power is new.

Power. Well? Why are you dawdling and wasting pity? Why don't you hate a god the gods hate most, the one who handed your own privilege over to mortals?

Hephaestus. Kinship is a terrible thing. So is friendship.

Power. Agreed. But disobeying the Father's word, how is that possible? Aren't you more afraid of that?

Hephaestus. You never change. Pitiless, and full of nerve.

Power. Because crying over this one cures nothing. Stop working at what is no use to anyone.

Hephaestus. Oh, my craft. How I hate it.

Power. Why hate it? In plain words, your skill is not to blame for any of the trouble in front of us.

Hephaestus. All the same, I wish it had fallen to someone else.

Power. Every job has its burden, except being king of the gods. Nobody is free except Zeus.

Hephaestus. I know that. This proves it. I have nothing to say back.

Power. Then hurry up and throw the chains round him, before the Father sees you loitering.

Hephaestus. There. The cuffs are ready, you can see them.

Power. Put them round his wrists and hit them with the hammer with all your strength. Nail him to the rock.

Hephaestus. It is getting done, and it is not being done badly.

Power. Hit harder. Squeeze tight. Leave nothing slack. He is frightening at finding a way out where there is no way out.

Hephaestus. This arm is fixed, anyway. He will not get that one loose.

Power. Now pin this one too, and make it safe, so that the clever one learns he is slower than Zeus.

Hephaestus. Nobody but him could fairly find fault with my work.

Power. Now the wedge of adamant, the stubborn tooth of it. Drive it straight through his chest and put your weight behind it.

Hephaestus. Aiai, Prometheus. I groan for your pain.

Power. Hanging back again? Groaning for the enemies of Zeus? Watch out, or one day you will be pitying yourself.

Hephaestus. You are looking at a sight that eyes can hardly bear to look at.

Power. I am looking at a man getting what he deserves. Come on. Throw the straps round his ribs.

Hephaestus. I have to do it. You do not have to keep ordering me.

Power. Oh, I will order you, and I will shout you on as well. Get down there and ring his legs with iron. Use force.

Hephaestus. There. Done, and it was not much work.

Power. Now hammer the fetters right through. Hard. The one who inspects this work is a severe judge.

Hephaestus. Your tongue talks the way your face looks.

Power. You be soft. Just do not lecture me for being stubborn and rough in my temper.

Hephaestus. Let us go. He has the net on his limbs.

(Hephaestus goes down the mountain.)

Power. Now sit there and be outrageous. Rob the gods of their privileges and give them to creatures that live for a day. Can mortals bail you out of any of this pain? The gods call you Prometheus, the Forethinker, and the name is a lie. You are the one who needs some forethought now, to work out how you will wriggle out of this piece of craftsmanship.

(Power and Force go out. Prometheus is alone on the rock.)

Prometheus. Bright sky. Winds with your quick wings. Springs of the rivers, and you, waves of the sea, laughing beyond counting. Earth, mother of everything. And the circle of the sun that sees it all: I call on you. Look at what I, a god, am suffering at the hands of gods. Look at the torments that are tearing me apart, and that I have to wrestle with

Prometheus.
	for ten thousand years.
	This is the shameful chain
	the new commander of the blessed gods
	has invented for me.
	Oh, oh. I groan for the pain that is here
	and the pain that is coming.
	Where will an end to this labour ever rise?

Prometheus. And yet, what am I saying? I know everything that is coming, exactly, in advance. No pain will reach me that I did not expect. What is allotted has to be carried as lightly as it can be, knowing that the strength of Necessity cannot be fought. But I cannot keep silent about my luck, and I cannot speak of it either. I gave gifts to mortals, and for that I am yoked in these bonds, poor wretch. I hunted down the spring of fire, hid it in a hollow fennel stalk, and stole it, and it has turned out to be mortals' teacher in every craft and their great resource. That is the crime I am paying for, nailed up in chains under the open sky.

Prometheus.
	Ah. Ah. What is that?
	What sound, what smell has flown to me, out of nowhere?
	A god's? A mortal's? Something of both?
	Has someone come to this rock at the end of the world
	to watch my pain? Or what does he want?
	Look at me, a god in chains, a god with no luck,
	the enemy of Zeus, whom every god
Prometheus.
	who walks through the court of Zeus has learned to hate,
	because I loved mortals too much.
	Oh, oh. What is that again? A whirring, close by,
	like birds. The air whistles
	under the light beating of wings.
	Everything that creeps toward me frightens me.

(The daughters of Oceanus arrive through the air in a winged chariot and hover near the rock.)

Chorus.
	Do not be afraid. We come as friends,
	this flight of wings
	racing each other to your rock.
	It was hard to talk our father round.
	The quick winds carried me here.
	The clang of iron on iron rang
	down to the bottom of our caves
	and knocked the shyness out of me, for all my grave looks.
	I came without my sandals, in a winged car.
Prometheus.
	Aiai, aiai.
	Children of Tethys, mother of so many,
	daughters of the father who circles the whole earth
	with his stream that never sleeps, Oceanus:
	look at me, see the chain
	that pins me to the top of this ravine,
	the watch I keep
	that nobody would envy.
Chorus.
	I see you, Prometheus.
	A fog of fear, full of tears,
	came over my eyes when I saw your body
	drying out on the rock
	in these insults of adamant.
	New helmsmen are steering Olympus.
	Zeus rules by new laws, and there is no law in them.
	What was huge before, he is wiping out.
Prometheus.
	If only he had thrown me under the earth,
	under Hades who takes in the dead,
	into Tartarus that has no end,
	and chained me there, savagely, in bonds that cannot be undone,
	so that no god and nobody else
	could gloat over this.
	Instead I hang in the wind, a toy for the air,
	and my pain is my enemies' pleasure.
Chorus.
	Which of the gods has a heart hard enough
	to be pleased by this?
	Who does not feel your pain with you,
	except Zeus? He has set his mind
	and it will not bend. He is angry always,
	and he is crushing the children of Heaven.
	He will not stop until his heart is full,
	or until somebody, by some trick, seizes
	the power that is so hard to take.
Prometheus.
	Believe this: the day will come
	when the lord of the blessed gods will need me,
	me, tortured in these strong shackles,
	to show him the new plan
	that strips him of his sceptre and his honours.
	He will not charm me with the honeyed spells of persuasion.
	I will not cower at his hard threats
	and tell him what I know,
	not until he loosens these savage chains
	and agrees to pay
	for this outrage.
Chorus.
	You are bold. You give nothing
	to your bitter suffering,
	and your mouth is far too free.
	A piercing fear has stirred up my mind.
	I am afraid for what happens to you.
	Where will you ever bring this ship of pain to shore
	and see its end? The son of Cronus
	has ways nobody can reach
	and a heart nobody can talk to.
Prometheus.
	I know Zeus is harsh
	and keeps justice in his own hands.
	All the same, I think one day
	he will be soft-minded, when he is broken
	in the way I know.
	He will flatten that iron rage of his
	and come hurrying to me, to be friends and allies,
	and I will be hurrying to meet him.

Chorus. Uncover the whole of it and tell us the story. What was the charge Zeus caught you on, that he tortures you so cruelly and with such dishonour? Teach us, unless the telling does you some harm.

Prometheus. It hurts me to tell this, and it hurts to keep quiet. Either way it is bad luck. As soon as the gods began to quarrel and faction broke out among them, one side wanting to throw Cronus off his seat so that Zeus, if you please, could be king, the other pushing the opposite way, so that Zeus would never rule the gods, that was when I gave the best advice there was and tried to persuade the Titans, the children of Heaven and Earth, and could not. They despised clever schemes. In the pride of their strength they thought they would be masters without effort, by force. But my mother Themis, who is also Earth, one shape with many names, had told me more than once how the future was fated to fall out: that it was not by strength, not by main force, but by cunning that the winners would win. I laid all this out for them in words, and they did not think it worth a glance. So the best thing open to me at the time, it seemed, was to take my mother with me and stand beside Zeus, a willing helper of a willing god. And it is by my plans that the black pit of Tartarus now covers old Cronus and his allies with him. That is the help the tyrant of the gods had from me, and this is how he paid me back, with these foul punishments. It is a sickness that comes with tyranny somehow: it cannot trust the people who are its own.

But you asked what he tortures me for, the charge against me, and I will make that clear. The moment he sat down on his father's throne he began handing out privileges to the gods, this to one and that to another, and dividing up his kingdom. But of wretched mortals he took no account at all. He wanted to wipe out the whole race and plant a new one. Nobody stood against that except me. I dared to. I saved mortals from being smashed to pieces and going down to Hades. That is why I am bent under these agonies, painful to suffer and pitiful to look at. I put mortals first in my pity, and I was not judged worthy of any pity myself. Instead I have been disciplined like this, without mercy, into a sight that shames the glory of Zeus.

Chorus. Anyone who does not ache with you at your torments, Prometheus, has a mind of iron and was carved out of rock. I would never have wanted to see this, and now that I have seen it my heart hurts.

Prometheus. Yes. To the people who are my own I am a pitiful thing to look at.

Chorus. You did not go any further than this, did you?

Prometheus. I did. I stopped mortals from seeing their own death coming.

Chorus. What cure did you find for that sickness?

Prometheus. I planted blind hopes inside them.

Chorus. That was a great gift to give mortals.

Prometheus. And on top of that, I gave them fire.

Chorus. What? Creatures of a day have fire now, with its bright eye?

Prometheus. Yes. And from it they will learn many crafts.

Chorus. So these are the charges Zeus has against you, and he...

Prometheus. And he tortures me and never once lets up.

Chorus. And there is no end set to your ordeal?

Prometheus. None. Only when he decides.

Chorus. And how will he ever decide? What hope is there? Don't you see that you did wrong? How you did wrong is not a pleasant thing for me to say, and it is pain for you to hear, so let us leave it. Look for some way out of this ordeal.

Prometheus. It is easy, when your own foot is clear of trouble, to advise and lecture someone who is in it. I knew all of this. I chose it, I chose it, I did wrong, I will not deny it. I helped mortals and found pain for myself. But I never thought the punishment would be like this: withering on a cliff in mid-air, with this empty rock for my share and no neighbour. Now stop crying over the pain in front of you. Come down to the ground and hear what is creeping toward me, so that you learn all of it to the end. Do it for me. Please. Share the burden with someone who is labouring under it now. Pain wanders. It settles on one of us today and on another tomorrow.

Chorus.
	You did not shout that to ears that were unwilling,
	Prometheus.
	Now, on a light foot, I will leave my racing seat
	and the clean air, the road of the birds,
	and come down to this jagged ground.
	I want to hear your troubles
	from beginning to end.

(The daughters of Oceanus come down from the chariot. Oceanus arrives through the air, riding a winged four-legged creature.)

Oceanus.
	I have come to the end of a long road,
	Prometheus, to you,
	steering this fast-winged bird
	with my mind, and no bit in its mouth.
	Know this: what has happened to you hurts me.
	Kinship makes me feel it, I think,
	and blood aside, there is no one
	I would give a bigger share of my respect to than you.
	You will find that this is true.
	I do not have it in me to talk sweetly for nothing.
	Come, show me what I can do to help you.
	You will never say you had a firmer friend than Oceanus.

Prometheus. What? What am I looking at? You have come too, to watch my pain? How did you find the nerve to leave the stream that carries your name and your caves, roofed with rock, that you hollowed out yourself, and come to this country, the mother of iron? Did you come to look at what has happened to me and to share my grief? Then look. Here is the sight: the friend of Zeus, the one who helped him set up his tyranny, and see what agonies he has bent me under.

Oceanus. I see, Prometheus, and I want to give you the best advice there is, clever as you are. Know yourself. Fit your ways to the new times. There is a new tyrant among the gods too. If you keep throwing out words as harsh and sharpened as these, Zeus may hear you, even sitting far above you, and then the mass of trouble you have now will look like a game. You poor wretch, let go of the anger you are holding and look for a way out of this suffering. Maybe what I am saying sounds old-fashioned to you. But this, Prometheus, is what a tongue that talks too big earns. You are still not humble. You still do not give way to trouble. You want to add fresh trouble to what you have. Take me as your teacher and you will stop kicking against the spurs. You can see that a harsh king rules, and he answers to nobody. Now I am going to go and try, if I can, to get you released from this pain. You keep quiet. Do not let your mouth run away with you. You are very wise; surely you know exactly what an idle tongue gets for its trouble?

Prometheus. I envy you. You shared in all of this, you dared as much as I did, and you are standing there with no blame on you. Now leave it alone. Do not concern yourself. You will not persuade him, whatever you do. He is not easy to persuade. And watch yourself, or the journey will do you harm.

Oceanus. You are far better at teaching your neighbours sense than yourself. I judge by what I see done, not by talk. I am set on going; do not pull me back. I am sure, I am sure, that Zeus will give me this gift, and release you from this pain.

Prometheus. For that, I thank you, and I will never stop thanking you. There is nothing lacking in your goodwill. But do not trouble yourself. You will trouble yourself for nothing and do me no good, if you really do want to take the trouble. Keep quiet and keep yourself out of the way. Because I am in trouble, that is no reason for me to want as many others in it as possible. No. My brother Atlas's fate is grinding at me enough already. He stands in the west, holding up the pillar of sky and earth on his shoulders, a load no arms can hold comfortably. And I pitied the one who lived in the Cilician caves, born of the earth, when I saw him: the murderous monster with a hundred heads, Typhon the furious, broken by force. He stood up against all the gods. Terror hissed out of his hideous jaws, and from his eyes a glare like the Gorgon's flashed, as if he would tear the tyranny of Zeus down by force. But the sleepless missile of Zeus came for him, the thunderbolt that comes down breathing flame, and it knocked him clean out of his big talk. He was hit in the very seat of his mind, and burned to a cinder, and the thunder blasted the strength out of him. And now he lies there, a useless body, a sprawl, near the narrow strait of the sea, pressed flat under the roots of Etna. Hephaestus sits on the topmost peaks and hammers his hot iron. And out of there, one day, rivers of fire will burst and eat the smooth fields of Sicily with savage jaws, Sicily with her lovely crops: that is the rage Typhon will boil up, in hot missiles of a fire-breathing storm nobody can come near, even though the thunderbolt of Zeus burned him to charcoal.

You are not inexperienced. You do not need me to teach you. Save yourself, the way you know how. I will drain the cup of what I have until the mind of Zeus lets go of its anger.

Oceanus. Then do you not know this, Prometheus, that words are the doctors for a sick temper?

Prometheus. If you soften the heart at the right moment, and do not use force to shrink a rage while it is still swelling.

Oceanus. And when a man is eager and takes a risk, what harm do you see in that? Teach me.

Prometheus. Wasted effort and light-headed foolishness.

Oceanus. Let me be sick with that sickness. When you are really wise, it pays best to seem a fool.

Prometheus. That fault will look like mine.

Oceanus. Plainly your words are sending me back home.

Prometheus. Yes. So that grieving for me does not get you hated.

Oceanus. By the one who has just sat down on the throne that rules everything?

Prometheus. Watch him. Do not ever let that heart of his get angry with you.

Oceanus. What has happened to you, Prometheus, is my teacher.

Prometheus. Go. Get moving. Keep the mind you have right now.

Oceanus. You shouted that at me as I was already starting. My four-footed bird is brushing the smooth road of the air with his wings. He will be glad to bend his knees in his own stall at home.

(Oceanus flies off.)

Chorus.
	I groan for your ruined luck, Prometheus.
	Tears drip from my eyes
	and run down my soft cheek
	and soak it in their wet stream.
	Zeus is ruling by laws of his own,
	and what he is doing is past envy;
	he shows the gods who came before him
	the spear-point of his pride.
Chorus.
	The whole country is crying out in grief already.
	They mourn the great, the ancient dignity
	that was yours and your brothers',
	all of them who live in the settled land
	of holy Asia:
	mortals who suffer along with you
	in your loud-groaning pain.
Chorus.
	And the girls who live in the land of Colchis,
	who do not flinch from a fight,
	and the crowd of Scythians who hold
	the last place on earth,
	round the marsh of Maeotis,
Chorus.
	and the warrior flower of Arabia,
	who hold the high cliff fortress
	near the Caucasus,
	a hostile army roaring
	behind sharp-pointed spears.
Chorus.
	One other Titan, a god, one only, have I seen before this
	broken in torment, in insults of adamant:
	Atlas, whose strength
	stood out above every other,
	who groans under the pole of the sky
	as he props it on his back.
Chorus.
	The sea's surf roars as it breaks.
	The deep groans.
	The black hollow of Hades rumbles under the earth,
	and the springs of the holy-flowing rivers
	groan for your pitiful pain.

Prometheus. Do not think it is pride that keeps me silent, or stubbornness. Thought is eating my heart, seeing myself abused like this. And yet, who else but I marked out the privileges of these new gods, every one of them, from start to finish? But I say nothing about that. I would be telling you what you already know. Listen instead to the troubles of mortals, and how I took them, who were children before, and gave them minds and put them in possession of their wits. I say this with no complaint against men. I am only explaining the goodwill behind what I gave.

At first they had eyes and saw nothing; they had ears and did not hear. Like the shapes in dreams they muddled through the length of their lives, all of it at random. They did not know houses of brick that face the sun. They did not know how to work wood. They lived underground like restless ants, in the sunless corners of caves. They had no reliable sign of winter, or of flowering spring, or of summer with its harvest. They did everything without understanding, until I showed them the risings of the stars and their settings, which are hard to tell apart. And number, the cleverest of inventions, I found for them, and the putting together of letters, which is the memory of everything and the working mother of the Muses. I was the first to yoke wild animals and make them slaves to the collar and the pack-saddle, so that they could take over the heaviest labour from mortal bodies, and I brought horses under the chariot and made them love the rein, the pride and ornament of rich men's luxury. And nobody but me invented the sailors' sea-roaming carriages with their wings of linen. Those are the devices I found for mortals, poor wretch, and I have no device of my own to get me out of the pain I am in now.

Chorus. You have suffered a shameful thing. You have lost your wits and gone astray. Like a bad doctor who has fallen ill himself, you are losing heart, and you cannot find the drugs that would cure you.

Prometheus. Hear the rest and you will marvel more, at the crafts and the resources I worked out. The biggest of them first. If anyone fell sick there was no defence at all, nothing to eat, nothing to rub on, nothing to drink. For want of drugs they shrivelled away, until I showed them how to mix gentle remedies, with which they now fight off every disease. I laid out the many roads of prophecy. I was the first to sort out which dreams are going to come true in waking life. I taught them to read chance words, hard to interpret, and the omens met on the road. The flight of birds with crooked talons I marked out exactly: which are lucky by nature and which are on the wrong side, what way of life each kind has, what feuds they have with each other and what loves, and where they gather. The smoothness of the inner organs, and what colour the gall must be to please the gods, and the mottled beauty of the liver's lobe. The thigh-bones wrapped in fat, and the long backbone, I burned, and set mortals on the road into an art that is hard to read. And I opened their eyes to the signs in the flame, which had a film over them before. So much for that. And the things hidden under the earth to help mankind, bronze, iron, silver, and gold: who could claim to have found them before I did? No one, I know it for certain, unless he wants to babble for nothing. Take the whole of it in one short word: every craft mortals have comes from Prometheus, and from no one else.

Chorus. Then do not go on helping mortals past all reason and neglect yourself in your trouble. I have good hope that you will still be set free from these chains and be no weaker than Zeus.

Prometheus. That is not how Fate, who brings everything to its end, has decreed this will finish. Only when I have been bent by ten thousand pains and torments do I escape these chains. Craft is far weaker than Necessity.

Chorus. Then who holds the tiller of Necessity?

Prometheus. The three Fates, and the Furies, who forget nothing.

Chorus. So Zeus is weaker than they are?

Prometheus. He cannot escape what is fated, at any rate.

Chorus. And what is fated for Zeus, except to rule for ever?

Prometheus. That you will not learn yet. Do not press me.

Chorus. It is something solemn, surely, that you are wrapping up.

Prometheus. Think about something else. This is no time to speak of that. It has to be kept covered, as far as it possibly can be. By keeping it safe I escape these shameful chains and these torments.

Chorus.
	May Zeus, who allots everything,
	never set his power against my mind.
	May I never be slow to come to the gods
	with holy feasts of slaughtered oxen
	beside the stream of Oceanus, my father, that never fails.
	May I never sin in what I say.
	Let this stay fixed in me and never melt away.
Chorus.
	It is sweet to stretch out a long life
	on hopes that have courage in them,
	feeding the heart on bright good cheer.
	But I shudder when I look at you,
	torn by ten thousand torments.
	You do not tremble at Zeus.
	You go your own way, Prometheus,
	and you honour mortals too much.
Chorus.
	Come, my friend, what a thankless thanks it was.
	Tell me, where is the strength in it?
	What help can creatures of a day give you?
	Did you not see how feeble they are, how weak,
	no more than a dream,
	the blind race of men with their feet in fetters?
	Never will the plans of mortals
	step outside the order Zeus has made.
Chorus.
	I learned this from looking at what has destroyed you,
	Prometheus.
	And a very different song has flown to me now
	from the one I sang then,
	around your bath and your bed,
	the wedding song, when you won
	my sister Hesione with your gifts
	and led her home to share your bed as your wife.

(Io runs in. She has the horns of a cow on her head, and she is being driven by something no one else can see.)

Io.
	What land is this? What people? Who is this
	I see, storm-beaten
	in a bridle of rock?
	What was your crime, that you are dying for it like this?
	Tell me where on earth
	my wretched wandering has brought me.
Io.
	Ah, ah. Eh, eh.
	It is stinging me again, the gadfly, poor wretch that I am,
	the ghost of Argus, the earth-born. Keep it off. Earth!
	I am afraid when I see him, the herdsman with ten thousand eyes.
	He comes on with his crafty eye,
	and even dead the earth will not hide him.
	He crosses over from the dead
	and hunts me, poor wretch, and drives me,
	starving, along the sand by the sea.
Io.
	And under it the reed pipe, stuck together with wax,
	drones its shrill tune that brings sleep.
	Oh, oh, popoi. Where are they taking me,
	these wanderings that carry me so far?
	What did I do, son of Cronus, what did you find me guilty of,
	that you yoked me in pain like this? Eh, eh.
	Why wear me down with a terror that drives me,
	a gadfly's terror, wretched and out of my mind?
	Burn me with fire, or hide me in the earth,
	or throw me to the beasts of the sea to eat,
	only do not begrudge me
	what I pray for, lord.
	These wanderings, all this wandering, have worked me hard enough.
	I cannot learn how to escape the pain.
	Do you hear the voice of the girl with the horns of a cow?

Prometheus. How could I not hear the girl the gadfly spins round, Inachus's daughter, who warms the heart of Zeus with love, and now, hated by Hera, is forced to run these endless courses?

Io.
	How do you know my father's name to speak it?
	Tell me, wretched as I am, who are you,
	that you call out to a wretched girl, poor sufferer,
	and get it right?
	You have named the sickness the god sent me,
	that wastes me and stings me with goads
	that drive me on. Eh, eh.
	I came here leaping, starving, tormented,
	rushing, beaten down
	by the angry schemes of Hera.
	Who among the unlucky, eh, eh,
	suffers what I suffer?
	Show me plainly
	what is waiting for me to suffer.
	What remedy is there, what cure for the sickness?
	Tell me, if you know.
	Speak. Explain it to a girl who has wandered so badly.

Prometheus. I will tell you plainly everything you want to know, no riddles woven into it, in simple words, the way it is right to open your mouth to friends. You are looking at Prometheus, who gave fire to mortals.

Io. You, who showed yourself the common benefactor of the human race, poor suffering Prometheus, what is this the punishment for?

Prometheus. I have only just stopped weeping over my own troubles.

Io. Then will you not do this one favour for me?

Prometheus. Say what it is you are asking. You can learn anything from me.

Io. Tell me who clamped you into this ravine.

Prometheus. The plan was Zeus's. The hand was Hephaestus's.

Io. And what crimes are you paying for?

Prometheus. I have made enough clear to you already. That is all.

Io. Then tell me this instead: where does my wandering end? How long does a wretched girl like me have to go on?

Prometheus. For you, not knowing is better than knowing.

Io. Do not hide from me what I am going to suffer.

Prometheus. It is not that I begrudge you the gift.

Io. Then why do you hold back from telling me everything?

Prometheus. There is no grudge in it. I am afraid of shattering your mind.

Io. Do not take more care of me than I want taken.

Prometheus. Since you insist, I have to speak. Listen, then.

Chorus. Not yet. Give me my share of the pleasure too. Let us hear about her sickness first, and let her tell us herself the disasters that have ruined her life. Then she can learn from you what struggles are still to come.

Prometheus. Io, it is your job to do them this kindness, all the more since they are your father's sisters. And weeping and wailing over your bad luck is time well spent when the people listening are going to give you a tear for it.

Io. I do not see how I can refuse you. You will hear everything you want to hear, in plain words. And yet even telling it shames me: the storm the gods sent, the ruin of my body, and where it came from, swooping down on me, poor wretch.

Night after night, visions came into my room, into a girl's room, and coaxed me with smooth words. "Lucky, lucky girl, why stay a virgin so long, when the greatest marriage of all is yours for the taking? Zeus has been warmed by the arrow of desire for you, and wants to take love's pleasure with you. Child, do not kick away the bed of Zeus. Go out to Lerna, to the deep meadow, to your father's sheep and cattle pens, so that the eye of Zeus can get some relief from its longing."

Dreams like that held me every night, and I was miserable, until at last I dared to tell my father about the dreams that came walking in the dark. He sent messenger after messenger to Pytho and to Dodona, to find out what he had to do or say to please the gods. They came back reporting oracles with shifting mouths, oracles that meant nothing, worded so that no one could make them out. Then at last a clear message came to Inachus, plainly charging him, plainly telling him, to push me out of his house and out of my country, to roam loose to the farthest edges of the earth. And if he would not, a fiery thunderbolt would come from Zeus and wipe out his whole family.

He believed those oracles of Apollo. He drove me out and barred his doors against me, against his will and against mine. But the bridle of Zeus was on him, and it forced him to do it. At once my body and my mind were twisted out of shape, and I grew horns, as you see, and a gadfly with a sharp mouth stung me, and I went leaping, mad, to the sweet stream of Cerchnea and the spring of Lerna. And a herdsman born from the earth, Argus, hot-tempered and never cooling, came after me, watching every step I took with his crowd of eyes. A sudden death that no one expected took his life from him. But I, stung by the fly, lashed by the gods' whip, am driven from land to land.

You have heard what happened. If you can say what struggles are still left, tell me. And do not pity me and warm me with lies. I say that made-up words are the most shameful sickness there is.

Chorus.
	Ah, ah. Keep it away. Oh.
	Never, never did I think
	words so strange would come to my ears,
	or that sufferings so hard to look at,
	so hard to bear,
	pain and filth and terror,
	would strike my soul with a two-pointed goad.
	Oh, oh, Fate, Fate,
	I shudder to see what has been done to Io.

Prometheus. You are groaning too soon. You are full of fear. Wait until you have learned the rest as well.

Chorus. Speak. Teach us. When someone is sick, it is sweet to know clearly beforehand what pain is still coming.

Prometheus. You got your first request out of me easily enough. You wanted to hear her tell the story of her own struggle first. Now hear the rest, the sufferings this young woman still has to endure at Hera's hands. And you, seed of Inachus, throw my words into your heart, so that you learn where your road ends.

First, from here, turn yourself towards the rising sun and walk across plains that have never seen a plough. You will come to the Scythian nomads, who live in woven houses raised high off the ground on wagons with good round wheels, and carry bows that shoot a long way. Do not go near them. Keep your feet close to the shore, where the sea groans on the rocks, and pass on out of their country. On your left hand live the Chalybes, who work iron. You must watch out for them. They are savages, and strangers cannot approach them. Then you will reach the river Hybristes, the Violent, and it does not lie about its name. Do not cross it. It is not an easy river to cross. Go on until you come to Caucasus itself, the highest of all mountains, where the river blows its strength out of the very brows of the peak. You must climb over those summits, which are neighbours of the stars, and take the road south. There you will come to the army of the Amazons, who hate men. One day they will settle Themiscyra on the river Thermodon, where the rough jaw of Salmydessus opens on the sea, a hateful host to sailors, a stepmother to ships. The Amazons will guide you on your way, and gladly. Then, right at the narrow gates of the lake, you will come to the Cimmerian isthmus. Leave it behind you with a bold heart, and cross the channel of Maeotis. And for all time mortals will tell the great story of your crossing, and it will be named after you: the Bosporus, the Cow's Ford. Then you will leave the soil of Europe and come to the continent of Asia.

Well? Does it not seem to you that the tyrant of the gods is violent in everything alike? He is a god, he wanted to sleep with a mortal girl, and he threw these wanderings on her. Girl, you have found a bitter suitor for your hand. And the words you have just heard, you must understand, are not even the introduction.

Io. Oh, oh, oh. Ee, ee.

Prometheus. Crying out again? Groaning again? What will you do when you learn the evils that are still to come?

Chorus. You mean there are more sufferings still to tell her?

Prometheus. A whole stormy sea of them, a sea of ruinous misery.

Io. Then what do I gain by living? Why did I not throw myself off this rough rock at once, dash myself on the ground and be free of all my troubles? Better to die once than to suffer badly every day of my life.

Prometheus. Then you would find my struggles hard to bear. It is not fated for me to die. Death would have been a release from my sufferings. As it is, there is no end set to my troubles until Zeus falls from his tyranny.

Io. What? Is Zeus really going to fall from power one day?

Prometheus. You would be glad, I think, to see that happen.

Io. How could I not be, when it is Zeus who makes me suffer?

Prometheus. Then you may know it for a fact: it will.

Io. Who will strip him of the tyrant's sceptre?

Prometheus. He will. Himself, and his own empty-headed plans.

Io. How? Tell me, if there is no harm in telling.

Prometheus. He will make a marriage that one day he will be sorry for.

Io. To a goddess, or a mortal? If it can be said, say it.

Prometheus. Why ask to whom? That cannot be spoken aloud.

Io. Is it his wife who will throw him off his throne?

Prometheus. She will bear a son who is stronger than his father.

Io. And is there no way for him to turn this fate aside?

Prometheus. None. Unless I were set free from these chains.

Io. Then who is going to free you, against the will of Zeus?

Prometheus. It has to be one of your own descendants.

Io. What did you say? A child of mine will free you from your suffering?

Prometheus. The third generation after ten more.

Io. Now your prophecy is getting too hard to read.

Prometheus. Then do not try to learn the whole of your own suffering either.

Io. Do not hold out a gift to me and then snatch it back.

Prometheus. I will make you a present of one of two stories.

Io. Which two? Show me them, and let me choose.

Prometheus. I am giving you the choice. Either I tell you clearly the rest of your sufferings, or I tell you who will set me free.

Chorus. Do her the one favour and me the other. Do not refuse my request. Tell her the rest of her wandering. Tell me who will set you free. That is what I long to hear.

Prometheus. Since you are set on it, I will not refuse to declare everything you want to know. To you first, Io, I will describe your wandering, all the tossing about that is still to come. Engrave it on the tablets of your mind, where memory keeps its records.

When you have crossed the stream that divides the two continents, turn towards the flaming east, where the sun walks. You will cross the roaring sea until you reach the Gorgon plains of Cisthene, where the daughters of Phorcys live, three ancient girls shaped like swans, who share one eye between them and have one tooth. The sun never looks down on them with his rays, and the moon never sees them by night. Near them live their three winged sisters, the Gorgons, with snakes for hair, whom the human race hates, and no mortal who has looked at them has drawn another breath. That is one guard-post I am warning you about.

Now hear about another sight, an ugly one. Watch out for the hounds of Zeus that never bark, the griffins with their sharp beaks, and for the one-eyed army of the Arimaspians, who ride on horses and live around the stream of Pluto's river, whose waters run with gold. Do not go near them. Then you will come to a far country, a dark-skinned people who live beside the springs of the sun, where the river Aethiops runs. Go along its banks until you reach the great drop, where the Nile sends down his holy, sweet stream from the Bybline mountains. He will lead you on to the three-cornered land of the Nile, the land Nile makes, where at last, Io, it is fated that you and your children will found your far-off colony.

If any of this is stammered and hard for you to make out, go back over it and learn it clearly. I have more spare time than I want.

Chorus. If there is anything left, or anything you passed over, that you can tell her about her wandering and its ruin, say it. But if you have told everything, then do us the favour we asked in our turn. You remember it, I think.

Prometheus. She has heard her whole journey to its end. But so that she knows she has not been listening to me for nothing, I will tell her what she went through before she came here, and give her that as proof that my words are true. I will leave out the great crowd of details and go straight to the last stretch of your wandering.

When you reached the Molossian plains and the high ridge that stands round Dodona, where the oracle and the seat of Thesprotian Zeus are, and that wonder no one believes in, the talking oaks, you were hailed by them, brightly and with no riddle in it at all, as the one who was going to be the famous wife of Zeus. Does any of that flatter you? From there the gadfly stung you, and you rushed along the coast road to the great gulf of Rhea, and from there you are tossed back the way you came, wandering in reverse. And for all time to come, know this for certain, that inlet of the sea will be called the Ionian Sea, a memorial of your journey to the whole human race.

Those are the signs I give you that my mind sees further than what has been shown. The rest I will tell to you and to her together, coming back onto the track of my earlier story.

Prometheus. There is a city called Canobus at the very edge of the land, right at the mouth of the Nile, on the silt it lays down. There Zeus will give you back your senses, stroking you with a hand that means no harm, and only touching you. And from that touch of Zeus you will bear a child and name him for it: dark Epaphus, the Touched One, who will harvest all the land the broad-flowing Nile waters.

Five generations after him, a family of fifty daughters will come back to Argos, and not by their own choice: they will be running from marriage to their cousins, their own blood. And the cousins, their hearts fluttering, like hawks left not far behind the doves, will come hunting a marriage that should not be hunted. But the god will begrudge them the women's bodies. Pelasgian soil will take them in, after a War made for killing men is fought by women, and the men are beaten by a daring that keeps watch by night. For each wife will rob her husband of his life, dipping a two-edged sword in his throat. May love come to my enemies like that.

But one of the daughters will be charmed by desire, and will not kill the man who shares her bed. The edge will go off her resolve. Of two names she will choose to be called the weaker one: coward rather than murderess. And she will give birth in Argos to a line of kings.

It would take a long speech to go through all this clearly. But from that seed there will be born a bold man, famous with the bow, who will set me free from these sufferings. That is the prophecy my mother told me, Themis, the Titan born long ago. How, and by what means, would take a long story to tell, and you would gain nothing by learning it.

Io.
	Eleleu, eleleu.
	Again the spasm, again the madness
	that hammers the brain, it heats me,
	the gadfly's barb, forged in no fire,
	stings me,
	and my heart kicks at my ribs in terror.
	My eyes roll and spin,
	I am carried out of my course
	by the raging blast of frenzy,
	I have lost the mastery of my tongue,
	and muddy words beat wildly
	against the waves of hateful ruin.

(Io runs off, driven by the gadfly, and does not come back.)

Chorus.
	Wise, he was wise, the man
	who first weighed this in his mind
	and put it into words with his tongue,
	that to marry in your own rank is far the best,
	and that a man who works with his hands
	should not set his heart on marrying
	among people gone soft with money
	or people swollen with their great birth.
Chorus.
	Never, never, you Fates,
	you powerful ones,
	may you see me made
	a partner of the bed of Zeus,
	never may I come near
	a bridegroom from the sky.
	For I shudder when I look at Io,
	a virgin no man loved,
	worn down like this
	by Hera's cruel wandering, by pain.
Chorus.
	For me, when a marriage is level,
	there is no fear in it. I am not afraid.
	But never let the love of the greater gods
	fix me with the eye no one escapes.
	That is a war that cannot be fought,
	a road with no way out and no way through,
	and I do not know what would become of me.
	For I cannot see
	how I could escape the mind of Zeus.

Prometheus. And yet Zeus, stubborn as his heart is, will be humbled. The marriage he is getting ready to make will throw him out of his tyranny and off his throne into nothing. And then the curse his father Cronus laid on him, the curse he spoke as he fell from his ancient throne, will be fulfilled to the last word. No god can show him the way out of those troubles except me. I know what is coming and I know how.

So let him sit there, confident, trusting in the crashes he makes up in the air, shaking his fire-breathing bolt in his hands. None of it will keep him from falling, dishonoured, a fall past bearing. Such is the wrestler he is getting ready now against himself, a wonder that cannot be fought, who will find a flame stronger than the lightning and a mighty crash that outroars the thunder, and will shatter the trident, Poseidon's spear, that plague of the sea that shakes the earth. And when he has tripped over that evil, he will learn how far apart it is to be a ruler and to be a slave.

Chorus. It is only what you wish for that your tongue is putting on Zeus.

Prometheus. I say what will come to pass, and what I want as well.

Chorus. Are we really to expect that someone will be master over Zeus?

Prometheus. Yes. And he will have a neck-load of pains harder to carry than these of mine.

Chorus. How are you not afraid, flinging out words like that?

Prometheus. What should I fear, when I am not fated to die?

Chorus. But he could give you a struggle even more painful than this one.

Prometheus. Let him do it. I expect everything.

Chorus. The wise are the ones who bow down to Necessity, the goddess no one escapes.

Prometheus. Worship, pray, fawn on whoever holds the power at the time. I care less than nothing for Zeus. Let him act, let him hold power for this short time of his, however he likes. He will not rule the gods for long. But look. Here I see the runner of Zeus, the errand boy of the new tyrant. He has come to announce something new, no doubt.

(Hermes comes in, the messenger of Zeus, with the wand and the winged sandals of his office.)

Hermes. You. The clever one. Bitter beyond all bitterness. The one who sinned against the gods by handing out their honours to creatures that live for a day. The thief of fire. I am talking to you. The Father orders you to say what marriage this is you keep boasting about, the one that is going to throw him out of power. And none of it in riddles: set out every single point as it stands. And do not make me come twice, Prometheus. You can see that Zeus is not softened by tricks like yours.

Prometheus. That is a pompous speech, and full of self-importance, as you would expect from a servant of the gods. You are new, and you hold new power, and you really think you live in a stronghold that grief can never reach. Have I not seen two tyrants thrown out of it already? And I will see the third, the one who is lord now, thrown out too, most shamefully and most quickly. Do you think I tremble in front of these new gods and cower down? I am a long way from that. All the way from it. Now hurry back along the road you came by. You will learn nothing you are asking me about.

Hermes. It was wilfulness exactly like this that ran you into this harbour of misery before.

Prometheus. I would not trade my bad luck for your slavery. Be clear about that.

Hermes. Better, I suppose, to be a slave to this rock than to be born the trusted messenger of Father Zeus.

Prometheus. That is how outrage should be answered: with outrage.

Hermes. You seem to be enjoying your present situation.

Prometheus. Enjoying it? I hope I see my enemies enjoying themselves like this. And I count you among them.

Hermes. What? You blame me too, somehow, for your disasters?

Prometheus. In one word: I hate every god that I did good to and that repays me with evil, against all justice.

Hermes. I hear you. You are sick with no small madness.

Prometheus. Sick I may be, if it is a sickness to hate your enemies.

Hermes. You would be unbearable if things were going well for you.

Prometheus. Oh.

Hermes. Oh? That is a word Zeus does not know.

Prometheus. But Time, as he grows old, teaches everything.

Hermes. And yet you, at least, have not learned yet to keep yourself in hand.

Prometheus. No. Or I would not be talking to you, an underling.

Hermes. It seems you will say nothing of what the Father wants.

Prometheus. Of course. I owe him so much. I ought to pay him back the favour.

Hermes. You mock me as if I were a child.

Prometheus. And are you not a child, and stupider than a child, if you expect to learn anything from me? There is no torture and no trick by which Zeus will make me speak this, until these chains that shame me are loosened. So let him throw his blazing flame. Let him stir up everything and shake it with white-winged snow and thunder under the earth. None of it will bend me into saying by whose hand he is fated to fall from his tyranny.

Hermes. Look, now, and see if this course seems likely to help you.

Prometheus. It was looked at long ago, and decided.

Hermes. Bring yourself, you fool, bring yourself at last to think straight about the sufferings you are in.

Prometheus. You are wasting your breath on me, as if you were talking a wave round. Never let it enter your head that I will turn woman-hearted out of fear of what Zeus intends, and beg the one I hate most, with my hands turned up like a woman's, to free me from these chains. I am the whole width of the world from that.

Hermes. I think I am going to say a lot and say it for nothing. Nothing I ask softens you. Nothing wears you down. Like a colt fresh under the yoke you bite the bit and fight against the reins. But the cleverness you are so violent about is a feeble thing. Wilfulness, on its own, in a man who does not think straight, has less strength than nothing at all.

Think, if you will not be persuaded by my words, what a storm, what a triple wave of evils is going to break on you, with no escape. First the Father will tear this jagged ravine apart with thunder and the flame of lightning, and hide your body, and the rock will hold you in its stony arms. And when you have finished a long stretch of time, you will come back up into the light. And then the winged hound of Zeus, the blood-red eagle, will tear your body greedily into great rags, a guest who comes to dinner uninvited and stays the whole day, and will feast on your liver until it is black with his gnawing. Do not expect any end to that agony until some god appears to take over your sufferings, and is willing to go down into Hades, where there is no light, and into the black depths of Tartarus.

Think about it, then. This is no invented boast. It is said all too truly. The mouth of Zeus does not know how to lie: he brings every word to pass. So look around you, and think, and never believe that wilfulness is better than good counsel.

Chorus. To us, at least, what Hermes says does not seem out of place. He is telling you to let go of your wilfulness and go looking for the good counsel that is wisdom. Do as he says. It is shameful for a wise man to go on being wrong.

Prometheus.
	I knew all that before he came
	bawling his message at me.
	And for an enemy to suffer
	at the hands of enemies is no disgrace.
	So let it come.
	Let the two-edged curl of fire be thrown at me,
	let the air be torn
	with thunder and the spasm of wild winds,
	let the blast shake the earth from its foundations,
	roots and all,
	let the waves of the sea in their rough surge
	pile up over the paths of the stars in heaven,
	and let him lift my body
	and hurl it down to black Tartarus
	in the hard whirlpools of Necessity.
	Whatever he does, he cannot kill me.
Hermes.
	These are the plans and these are the words
	you hear from men whose minds are struck.
	What is missing, what does his prayer lack,
	to make it raving? Where does his madness slacken?
	But you, at least, you who share
	the labour of his sufferings,
	get away from this place, somewhere, quickly,
	before the bellow of the thunder
	that nothing softens stuns your minds.
Chorus.
	Say something else. Give me advice
	I could actually take.
	That word you swept past me just now
	is not a thing I can stand.
	How can you order me to practise cowardice?
	Whatever he has to suffer, I want to suffer with him.
	I have learned to hate traitors,
	and there is no sickness
	I spit out more than that one.
Hermes.
	Then remember what I am telling you now,
	and when you are caught in the hunt of ruin
	do not blame your luck,
	and never say that Zeus
	threw you into a suffering you could not foresee.
	No. You did it to yourselves.
	You knew. It was not sudden, it was not secret.
	Your own stupidity will tangle you
	in the net of ruin, the net with no way out.

(Hermes goes out. The storm begins.)

Prometheus.
	Now it is deed and no longer word.
	The earth is rocking.
	The thunder's echo bellows up from the deep,
	the curls of lightning flash out, all fire,
	whirlwinds spin the dust,
	the blasts of all the winds leap at each other
	and show their fight, breath against breath,
	and the sky is churned into the sea.
	This is the blow that Zeus sends against me,
	openly, to make me afraid.
	O holiness of my mother,
	O sky, wheeling the light that everyone shares,
	you see me. You see what injustice I suffer.

(Thunder and lightning. The rock splits and Prometheus goes down into the earth, and the daughters of Oceanus go down with him.)
